## WHAT THIS EDITION IS, AND WHAT IT REFUSES TO DO

This is a Mains-focused edition of the single largest causal system in the Geography syllabus. It is
not a second comprehensive package and not a revision digest. It exists because of one fact that
organises every lesson below.

**The two owner files contain a usable skeleton and almost none of the evidence.** Together they run
to 4,977 words. The certified book corpus behind them — Leong's Chapter 16, Majid's world-monsoon
and jet-stream folios, and above all Khullar's Chapter 5 — carries roughly five times that payload,
and it carries it **with its own defects visible**. Khullar contradicts Khullar six times inside one
chapter. Leong states the rule of monsoon withdrawal exactly backwards. The owner prints a
four-season calendar under a mark meaning *from the source book* when no certified book prints four
seasons. And the owner's single current-affairs anchor is a **forecast** for a season that closed
four days before this edition was drafted, at a figure the owner does not carry.

A topic whose sources disagree is not a defective topic. It is the only kind of topic on which a
Mains candidate can be taught the skill that actually separates scripts: **how to write a sentence
containing a number whose sources disagree.**

Eight commitments govern every lesson.

1. **No conflict is resolved by choosing a number.** Where two certified sources print two figures,
   **both are printed, both are attributed, and neither is adopted**. The word *unresolved* is used
   where the sources leave the matter unresolved. **Thirty-two corrections** are discharged by name;
   seven of them are conflicts *inside a single source*.
2. **Book sentences are printed before book claims are used.** Where an owner tags an assertion with
   the mark meaning *from the source book*, this edition prints what the book actually says, at its
   printed folio and its scan page, and then states plainly whether the owner's sentence is the
   book's sentence, a defensible extension, or neither.
3. **A forecast is never written as an outcome.** The 2026 season produced a complete, officially
   self-assessed forecast-to-outcome cycle: two seasonal forecasts, one onset forecast, an error
   band, a realised figure and IMD's own verification note. It is taught as a **verification
   exercise**, never as a retro-fitted prediction.
4. **Every number carries a source line.** No figure appears in this edition without either a book
   folio or a dated official endpoint. Where a book prints inches or degrees Fahrenheit, **the
   printed unit leads and the metric equivalent follows in brackets, marked as a conversion**.
5. **Nothing is answered that this topic does not own.** Thirteen adjacent and routed-away demands
   are named and refused. Twelve boundaries are drawn by exact owner path. Jet dynamics, ocean
   mechanics, disaster management, agricultural economics and climate-change attribution are
   **used** where the topic needs their results and **never re-derived** here.
6. **No objective answer is ever supplied, implied or reconstructed.** Topic 16 owns exactly two
   verified demands in nine years. One is cross-owned and carries a published audit signal that is
   disclosed rather than concealed. The other is an objective question whose locally held 2026 key
   is **provisional**; its existence is stated, its content is not, and **no answer letter appears,
   is inferred or is implied anywhere in this file**.
7. **Modern nomenclature is labelled as modern nomenclature.** Two names that every coaching note
   uses appear in **no** certified local book. The phenomenon they name is certified; the names are
   presented as later vocabulary attached to a 1970s observational finding.
8. **No trend is claimed.** Not that breaks are becoming more frequent, not that onset is shifting,
   not that the monsoon is becoming more erratic, and not that any single season is attributable to
   climate change. One season is one season. Where IMD offers its own attribution language, that
   language is reported and the sentence stops.

**What this edition adds that neither owner has.** The entire theory lineage — etymology, the
six-months-each-way definition, Al Masudi, Sidi Ali, Halley, Dobby, Conrad, Ramage's four
quantitative criteria and his monsoon rectangle, Flohn, Yin, Pedelaborde and Koteswaram — which
C2's own Mains angle demands and C2's own body cannot support. The observation-programme era, from
the International Indian Ocean Expedition to MONEX-1979, as the evidence that converted a hypothesis
into a measurement. A quantitative orographic transect. A funnel. A depression climatology tested
against the 2026 low-pressure-system record. A three-way adjudication of the break. A complete
variability apparatus with its formula, its national range and its 1951 provenance disclosed. And a
forecast lesson built from two IMD press documents read in full at generation.

## READING CONVENTIONS — FROZEN

| Mark | Meaning |
|---|---|
| ✅ | Printed in a named local book, at the printed folio or page given |
| ⚠️ | Standard physical geography or a stated analytical inference; no book sentence exists for it, and it is never presented as one |
| 📰 | Official or live material reached through a dated retrieval, with its retrieval date and class stated |
| ✗ | A defect in an owner or in a source, quoted so that it can be recognised, never asserted |
| ⇄ | A live conflict between two sources, or inside one source, with both sides shown and neither adopted |
| ↪ | A route-out to the owning file, by exact path |

**Citation conventions, frozen for this topic.**

- **G.C. Leong, *Certificate Physical and Human Geography*, Chapter 16, "The Tropical Monsoon and
  Tropical Marine Climates".** Cited as `Leong printed p. 123 (scan PDF 130)`. The chapter occupies
  **printed pages 122-127 = scan PDF 129-134**; the offset `printed = PDF − 7` was fixed by reading
  the Chapter 16 head on PDF 129 against the printed folio 122 and the Chapter 17 head on PDF 135
  against printed 128. The world-climatic-types table used at Lessons 2 and 3 is **printed p. 114 =
  scan PDF 121**, which belongs to a different chapter and is attributed as such. **No unseen folio
  is minted from the offset.**
- **D.R. Khullar, *India: A Comprehensive Geography*, Chapter 5, "Climate".** Cited as
  `Khullar folio 146 (scan PDF 90, left)`. The scan is **two-up landscape**: scan page *p* carries
  folio **(2p − 34)** on the left and **(2p − 33)** on the right, and upright rotation alternates
  with scan-page parity — 270° on even pages, 90° on odd. Every folio quoted below was read at its
  **running head and folio box** before its body was quoted.
- **Majid Husain, *Indian and World Geography*.** Cited as `Majid IWG folio 3.21 (scan PDF 94)`.
  Pagination is **chapter-relative**: "3.21" is the twenty-first page of chapter 3, not page 321.
  This is the one book of the four with a text layer, and it is a **word-box** layer in which every
  word sits on its own line, so page text was joined before any term was matched.
- **`Indian-geography-majid-hussain.pdf` is a sampled scan and is not used.** Its certified folio
  sequence runs 4.3 → 3.46 → 4.13 → 4.8 → 4.23 → 4.18 → 4.33 across seven consecutive scan pages;
  consecutive scan pages advance by about ten printed folios. The "El Niño and the Indian Monsoon"
  section at printed folio **4.10** is **absent from the local copy**. Nothing in this edition rests
  on that book.
- **Units are printed as the book prints them, with the conversion shown and marked.** Leong's
  chapter is entirely imperial; Khullar's and Majid's are metric. An inch value is never silently
  turned into a centimetre band and then presented as the book's own figure.
- **Every volatile figure carries its issuing agency and its date.** A bare monsoon statistic is
  treated as an error in this edition, not as a shorthand.

**Why X-01 to X-04 matter more than tidy-ups.** This edition's method is to refuse to resolve a
conflict by choosing a number. The handoff applied that method to the books. At generation the same
method, applied to the **live official record**, found four conflicts of exactly the same kind —
two dates for one forecast, two totals for one region, a unit that cannot be right, and an interval
that does not match its own endpoints. **A candidate who learns to handle Khullar's six internal
conflicts is learning to handle IMD's four.** That is the transferable skill, and it is why those
items are taught rather than silently corrected.

Progress: 1 / 20 · Stage: Foundation · Primary visual grammar: **four-column definition ledger with a pass/fail strip**

### The intuition, before any term

Ask an ordinary question: *what makes a wind a monsoon?* The obvious answer — "it reverses with the
seasons" — sounds like a definition. It is not. It is a **description without a threshold**. Every
coastline on Earth reverses its wind twice a day; a sea breeze blows inland by afternoon and a land
breeze blows out by night. If reversal alone made a monsoon, every beach would have one.

So the real question is quantitative: **how much reversal, how reliably, how strongly, and over how
large an area?** Four different authorities have answered that question four different ways, and the
answers are not equivalent. One is purely qualitative. One is a single number. One is a set of four
numerical tests plus a bounding rectangle. And one denies the whole project by saying a monsoon is
just a very large sea breeze.

This is why Topic 16 cannot begin with a mechanism. **You cannot explain a thing before you have
agreed what the thing is**, and the sources have not agreed. Start here, and every later lesson —
including the four rival theories at Lessons 5 to 8 — becomes an argument about which definition the
theory is trying to satisfy.

**What the visual shows.** Read down the "kind of test" column. The four authorities are not four
opinions about one question; they are **four different questions**. Dobby asks whether the wind
turns. Conrad asks by how much. Köppen, Hann and Angot ask whether the phenomenon is new at all.
Ramage asks whether a 5° box of ocean behaves in a specified, measurable way for a specified
fraction of the year. A candidate who writes "the monsoon is a seasonal reversal of winds" has
answered Dobby and ignored the other three.

### The technical vocabulary, each term after the idea it names

- ✅ **Monsoon, etymology.** Khullar folio 122 derives the word from the Arabic ***mausin*** or the
  Malayan ***monsin***, meaning *season*. ⇄ Majid IWG folio 3.21 derives it from the Arabic
  ***Mausim***, also meaning season. **Two books, two spellings, one meaning** — this is **C-23**.
  The disagreement is small and it is still a disagreement; the honest sentence names both.
- ✅ **The double system.** Khullar folio 122: a monsoon is *"a **double system of seasonal
  winds**… the sum of summer and winter winds"*. This matters more than it looks. A monsoon is not
  the summer wind. It is the **pair**, and an answer that describes only the rain-bearing half has
  described half the object.
- ✅ **The six-month rule.** Majid IWG folio 3.21: the term originally described the winds of the
  **Arabian Sea**, blowing *"about **six months from the north-east and six months from the
  south-west**"*, with *"complete reversal of wind direction after every six months"*. This is the
  **oldest operational definition in the topic**, and it is a sailing definition, not a rainfall
  definition — which is why Lesson 5's Arab navigators matter.
- ✅ **A.A. Rama Sastry** is cited at folio 122 in the same cluster of definitional authorities.
- ✅ **Ramage's four criteria**, printed in full at folio 122: (i) prevailing wind direction shifts
  by **at least 120°** between January and July; (ii) average frequency of the prevailing wind
  directions in January and July **exceeds 40 %**; (iii) mean resultant wind velocity in at least one
  month **exceeds 3 m/s**; (iv) **fewer than one cyclone-anticyclone alternation every two years**
  over a 5° grid square.
- ✅ **The monsoon rectangle**, printed at folio 122: approximately **35°N-25°S, 30°W-173°E**.

> ⚠️ **Note the structure of Ramage's set, because it is the examinable point.** Criteria (i) to
> (iii) measure the **reversal**. Criterion (iv) measures something else entirely: the **absence of
> travelling disturbances**. Ramage is saying that a true monsoon region is one where the seasonal
> circulation, not a procession of migrating lows and highs, dominates the wind record. This is the
> only one of the four that India can plausibly fail, and it fails it for the most Indian of
> reasons — the Bay of Bengal makes depressions. Lesson 13 is where that comes back.

### The causal chain, with its assumptions and its limits

```text
  WHY A DEFINITION PROBLEM EXISTS AT ALL

   A seasonally reversing wind is observed over the Arabian Sea
        |  (certified: Majid IWG folio 3.21 - six months each way)
        v
   Navigators need only RELIABILITY and DIRECTION, so a qualitative
   definition is sufficient for roughly a thousand years
        |
        v
   Climatology arrives and needs to CLASSIFY: is this its own type,
   or an enlarged sea breeze?
        |                                   |
        v                                   v
   Koppen / Hann / Angot:              Dobby / Conrad:
   an enlarged sea breeze.             its own type, defined by
   NO new class needed.                the ANGLE of the turn.
        |                                   |
        +---------------+-------------------+
                        v
   Neither camp can draw a MAP, because neither has a test that a
   grid box can pass or fail
                        |
                        v
   RAMAGE 1971: four measurable criteria + a bounding rectangle
                        |
                        v
   A monsoon region becomes a MAPPABLE OBJECT - at the cost of a
   definition that is admittedly a CONVENTION, not a discovery

  ASSUMPTION throughout: that "monsoon" names a WIND system.
  LIMIT: every one of the four tests is a WIND test. NONE mentions
  RAIN. Yet every Indian use of the word - "the monsoon failed",
  "the monsoon arrived" - is about RAIN. The definitional literature
  and the public meaning of the word measure different variables,
  and no certified source closes that gap.
```

### The India hook, with its limitation printed beside it

⚠️ **India is the archetypal monsoon country and can still strain against a monsoon criterion.** On
Ramage's test (iv), a 5° grid square over the head of the Bay of Bengal experiences repeated
cyclone-anticyclone alternations in a season — Khullar folio 147 prints **three to four depressions
per month from June to September** — which is precisely what criterion (iv) is designed to exclude.
India clears the reversal tests and is the least comfortable fit on the disturbance test.

> ⛔ **Limitation that must be printed with it.** Ramage's rectangle and criteria are a **1971
> definition** devised for a world-scale survey. **They are not an IMD operational category.** IMD
> declares onset and withdrawal by its own rainfall, wind-field and radiation gates (Lesson 10), and
> those gates make no reference to Ramage. Writing "India fails Ramage's criterion (iv)" as though it
> had operational consequence is an overreach; writing it as a **definitional tension** is exact.

### Comparison, objection, reply and the unresolved residual

**Comparison — why Conrad and Ramage disagree about India.** Conrad demands **about 180°**, a single
severe threshold, and Menon's measurement of *"nearly 180°"* for the July-January shift means India
clears it by a margin that depends on where you put the word *nearly*. Ramage demands **at least
120°** — a far more permissive angular test — but adds three further conditions. The two are not a
strict and a lax version of the same test. Conrad is asking a **physical** question (is this a
thermal reversal?); Ramage is asking a **statistical** question (does this grid box behave
monsoonally often enough to be mapped as such?). The prior commitment differs: Conrad is interested
in mechanism, Ramage in classification.

**Objection.** *"This is scholasticism. The examiner wants the mechanism, not a parade of
definitions."*

**Reply.** The objection would hold if the definitions were decorative. They are not, for two
reasons that pay directly. First, **C2's own Mains angle demands a critical comparison of classical
and modern theories**, and a comparison of theories is impossible without stating what each theory
was trying to explain — Halley was trying to satisfy a sea-breeze intuition, Flohn and Koteswaram
were trying to satisfy a circulation criterion. Second, a stem of the form *"The Tropical Monsoon
Climate is, in fact, land and sea breezes on a continental scale"* is **set verbatim as an exercise
in Leong's own Chapter 16** (printed p. 127). A candidate who has only the mechanism will agree with
it. A candidate who has this lesson will recognise it as **Köppen's, Hann's and Angot's position,
printed in Khullar at folio 122**, and will be able to say precisely what the sea-breeze analogy
gets right (the pressure-gradient reversal) and what it cannot carry (the depth of the circulation,
the cross-equatorial source of the air, and the upper-air reorganisation of Lessons 7 and 8).

**Unresolved residual.** **No certified source reconciles the wind definition with the rain
meaning.** Every one of the four tests measures wind; every Indian use of the word measures rain.
Khullar folio 122 itself concedes, a few lines later, that *"the origin of monsoons is still
shrouded in mystery"*. This edition prints the gap and **leaves it open**. The honest Mains sentence
is *"'Monsoon' is defined in the literature as a wind regime and used in practice as a rainfall
regime, and no standard definition bridges the two"* — a stronger opening than any textbook formula.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Writing "complete seasonal reversal" as though it were a definition.** It is Dobby's
  description. The **number** belongs to Conrad (about 180°) or to Ramage (at least 120°), and the
  two numbers are not the same number.
- ❌ **Attributing "land and sea breezes on a large scale" to a critic of the monsoon concept.** It
  is printed at Khullar folio 122 as the position of **Köppen (1923), Hann (1932) and Angot (1943)**
  — three climatologists of the first rank — and it is set as an examination exercise by Leong.
- ❌ **Quoting Ramage's rectangle as a region.** It is a **bounding box**, roughly
  **35°N-25°S, 30°W-173°E**. It contains large areas with no monsoon at all.
- ❌ **Treating Ramage's criterion (iv) as another reversal test.** It is a **disturbance**
  criterion, and it is the only one India strains against.
- ❌ **Deriving the etymology from one book.** Khullar prints *mausin* / *monsin*; Majid prints
  *Mausim*. **Both are printed; neither is corrected by the other.**
- ❌ **Letting Köppen's name here drag in his climate letters.** The **Am / Aw** classification, its
  thresholds and its Indian application belong to `Geography/basic/14` and `Geography/advanced/14`.
  This lesson uses only his 1923 sentence about sea breezes.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I, *"Salient features of world's physical geography"* — the identity of a named
world climatic type, which is the clause C1 owns, taken at the level of **what the type is**, not
where it is.

**PYQ position.** **Topic 16 owns no verified PYQ for this content.** Its two owned demands are a
**2023 GS-I** question on the south-west monsoon as *Purvaiya* in Bhojpur, which is **cross-owned**
with `Geography/advanced/37_Cultural-and-Social-Geography-of-India`, and a **2026 Prelims GS-I**
objective demand on Andaman and Nicobar climate and seasonal precipitation whose locally held Set-A
key is **provisional**. Both are discharged at **Lesson 20**, and **no answer letter is recorded,
inferred or implied anywhere in this edition.** This lesson is the definitional foundation on which
the "critically compare the classical and modern theories" stem — set by C2 §8 and unsupported by
C2's own body — becomes answerable at Lessons 5 to 8.

**Answer use.** Never open a monsoon answer with the mechanism. Open with the **definitional
status** in two sentences, because it buys the whole essay's framing: *"'Monsoon', from the Arabic
mausin or mausim meaning season, denotes a double system of seasonal winds rather than a rainy
season; Dobby defines it qualitatively by reversal, Conrad quantitatively by a turn of about 180°,
and Ramage operationally by four criteria — a 120° shift, over 40 per cent prevailing-direction
frequency, a resultant velocity above 3 m/s and fewer than one cyclone-anticyclone alternation every
two years — inside a rectangle running roughly 35°N to 25°S and 30°W to 173°E. India satisfies the
reversal tests comfortably and strains only against the disturbance criterion, because the Bay of
Bengal generates travelling systems in every monsoon season."*

### Revision points

1. ✅ **Etymology, two printed versions:** Khullar folio 122 — Arabic ***mausin*** or Malayan
   ***monsin***; Majid IWG folio 3.21 — Arabic ***Mausim***. Both mean *season*. **C-23**:
   unresolved.
2. ✅ **Definition:** a monsoon is *"a **double system of seasonal winds**… the sum of summer and
   winter winds"* — the **pair**, not the rainy half.
3. ✅ **The sailing definition:** Majid 3.21 — the original Arabian Sea winds blew *"about six
   months from the north-east and six months from the south-west"*, a **complete reversal every six
   months**.
4. ✅ **Dobby:** *"reversal of wind system is the key-note of the monsoonal climate"* — qualitative,
   no threshold.
5. ✅ **Conrad:** *"a true thermal monsoon demands a complete reversal of winds, that is an angle of
   about **180°**"*. ✅ **P.A. Menon** measures the July-January shift at **nearly 180°**.
6. ✅ **Köppen (1923), Hann (1932), Angot (1943):** monsoons are *"simply **land and sea breezes on a
   large scale**"* — a denial that a separate class is needed.
7. ✅ **Leong printed p. 127** sets exactly that proposition as an **end-of-chapter exercise**:
   *"The Tropical Monsoon Climate is, in fact, land and sea breezes on a continental scale."*
8. ✅ **Ramage (1971), four criteria:** ≥ **120°** shift January to July · prevailing-direction
   frequency > **40 %** · resultant velocity > **3 m/s** in at least one month · fewer than **one
   cyclone-anticyclone alternation every two years** per **5° grid square**.
9. ✅ **Ramage's monsoon rectangle:** about **35°N-25°S, 30°W-173°E**.
10. ✅ **A.A. Rama Sastry** is named at folio 122 among the definitional authorities.
11. ⚠️ **The structural point:** criteria (i)-(iii) test the **reversal**; criterion (iv) tests the
    **absence of travelling disturbances**, and that is the one India strains against, because of the
    Bay depressions taught at Lesson 13. Every one of the four tests is a **wind** test; the public
    meaning of the word is a **rainfall** meaning, and **no certified source bridges the two**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A script opens: "The monsoon is defined as the complete seasonal reversal of winds."
State what is right about the sentence, what is missing from it, and the one further clause a
Mains-grade opening would add.

**Model answer:** What is right: reversal is genuinely the organising property, and the formulation
is close to **Dobby's** printed sentence that *"reversal of wind system is the key-note of the
monsoonal climate"* (Khullar folio 122). What is missing is a **threshold**. "Complete" is not a
measurement, and the two authorities who supply one disagree: **Conrad** demands an angle of **about
180°**, while **Ramage (1971)** requires only **at least 120°**, together with three further tests —
prevailing-direction frequency above **40 %**, resultant velocity above **3 m/s**, and fewer than one
cyclone-anticyclone alternation every two years over a 5° grid. The further clause a Mains opening
would add is that a monsoon is a **double system** — the sum of the summer and the winter winds — so
that describing only the rain-bearing half describes half the object. *(130 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That there is one authoritative definition of a monsoon. There are at
least four printed on a single folio, they measure different things, and one of them — Köppen's,
Hann's and Angot's — denies that the category is needed at all. *(39 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 1

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** Examine whether the Indian monsoon satisfies the
standard criteria by which a monsoon is defined.

**Model:** It satisfies most of them, and the one it strains against is the most interesting.
**Dobby's** qualitative test — reversal as the *"key-note"* — India clears trivially. **Conrad**
demands a turn of *"about 180°"*; **Menon's** measured July-January shift of *"nearly 180°"* clears
it, though the word *nearly* carries the margin. **Ramage (1971)** sets four tests: a shift of at
least **120°**, prevailing-direction frequency above **40 %**, resultant velocity above **3 m/s**,
and fewer than one cyclone-anticyclone alternation every two years per 5° grid, inside a rectangle of
roughly **35°N-25°S, 30°W-173°E**. India clears the first three comfortably. The fourth is a
**disturbance** criterion, not a reversal criterion, and the Bay of Bengal supplies three to four
depressions a month through the season — exactly what it excludes. India is therefore the archetypal
monsoon and the awkward case, on a test that is not about reversal at all. *(142 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** **Definitional precision** — all four authorities named with their actual
demands rather than merged into one formula **4** · **Criterion application** — India tested against
each in turn rather than asserted to be monsoonal **3** · **Counter-case handling** — criterion (iv)
identified as a disturbance test and the Bay depressions supplied as the strain **2** ·
**Terminological discipline** — the wind-versus-rain gap stated rather than elided **1**

### Route-outs required of this lesson

| Material | Owner, by exact path |
|---|---|
| Köppen's classification, the **Am** and **Aw** letters, their thresholds, Thornthwaite, Trewartha, Stamp, R.L. Singh, and Khullar folios 156-167 | `Geography/basic/14_Climate-Classification-Koppen`, `Geography/advanced/14_India-Climatic-Regions` |
| The planetary wind system, trade winds and jet streams **as such** | `Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements`, `Geography/advanced/13_India-JetStream-Western-Disturbances` |
| Sea-breeze and land-breeze micro-circulation as a weather element | `Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements` |
| Tropical cyclone genesis and structure, including Bay cyclones as **storms** | `Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements` |

## Lesson 2 — Where the two climates are — and the latitude band that is not in this chapter


Progress: 2 / 20 · Stage: Foundation · Primary visual grammar: **world strip-map in text, banded by latitude**

### The intuition, before any term

A climatic type is a **box drawn on a continuum**, and the box has two kinds of edge. One edge is a
**parallel of latitude**, which is where an author chooses to stop drawing. The other is a
**coastline with a wind on it**, which is where the physics actually changes. The tropical monsoon
and the tropical marine types sit side by side on the same latitudes and are separated entirely by
the second kind of edge.

Here is the whole distinction in ordinary language. Take an eastern tropical coast. If the wind on
that coast **blows onshore all year** — because the trade wind arrives there and never leaves — you
get the **tropical marine** type: wet, steady, no dry season. If instead the wind on that coast
**turns round twice a year**, blowing in from the ocean for one half and out from the continent for
the other, you get the **tropical monsoon** type: wetter in total at some stations, but with a real
dry season carved into it.

So distribution is not a list to memorise. It is a **map of where continents are big enough to
reverse the wind**. Monsoon lands cluster where a very large landmass sits poleward of a very large
ocean. Marine lands cluster where a trade wind runs uninterrupted onto a coast. That is why Leong's
two lists look so different in shape.

### The primary visual — map plate M-1

```text

  Latitude band from Leong printed p.114 (scan PDF 121) - a DIFFERENT CHAPTER.
  Schematic strip map. Not to scale. Longitude runs left to right, 0 to 360.

              W. HEMISPHERE          AFRICA        ASIA            PACIFIC
```

**What the visual shows, and the one thing it refuses to show.** The monsoon entry is a *block*; the
marine entry is a *scatter*. That shape difference is the mechanism, drawn. But the horizontal
double lines — the 10°-30° band — are drawn in a different weight for a reason. **They do not come
from this chapter.** They come from a cross-chapter table that Topic 14 owns, and C1 imported them
under a mark that says they came from the monsoon chapter.

### Correction C-02, stated exactly

✗ **C1 §1 line 12 and C1 §5 line 78 attribute "10°-30° N/S" to the monsoon chapter.** The band is
real and it is printed — **Leong printed p. 114, World Climatic Types, "Hot Zone, latitudes 10°-30°
N and S"**, whose rows include **2(a) Tropical Monsoon**, **2(b) Tropical Marine** and **3 Sudan**.
But **Leong's Chapter 16 states no latitude band anywhere** (negative finding **N-02**).

⚠️ Why this is more than pedantry. The p. 114 table is a **classification artefact**: it is the row
structure of a scheme, and that scheme — with its rows, its thresholds and its Indian application —
is `Geography/basic/14`'s property, not this topic's. Importing a band from a classification table
into a mechanism chapter, and then marking it as the mechanism chapter's own, is how a candidate
ends up defending "10°-30°" as though Leong had derived it from monsoon physics. He did not. He
tabulated it.

⚠️ **The safe formulation**, and it costs eleven words: *"Leong's world-climatic-types table places
both hot-zone types in the 10°-30° belt north and south, although his monsoon chapter itself states
no band and proceeds directly to the regional lists."*

### The two lists, with what each one is doing

| | ✅ **Tropical monsoon** (Leong p. 122) | ✅ **Tropical marine** (Leong p. 122) |
|---|---|---|
| **Printed list** | Indian sub-continent, Burma, Thailand, Laos, Cambodia, parts of Vietnam, south China, northern Australia | Central America, West Indies, north-eastern Australia, the Philippines, parts of East Africa, Madagascar, the Guinea Coast, eastern Brazil |
| **Figure** | Fig. 125(a) / 125 | Fig. 124 |
| **Geometry** | one contiguous Asian block plus an Australian outlier | eight separated eastern coasts and islands on four continents |
| **Wind regime** | complete seasonal reversal | onshore **Trade Winds** all year |
| **What the geometry implies** | ⚠️ requires a **continent** large enough to reverse a pressure gradient | ⚠️ requires only a **trade wind arriving at a shore** |
| **Overlap** | ⚠️ **north-eastern Australia appears in the marine list and northern Australia in the monsoon list** — the same landmass, two types, two coasts | |

> ⚠️ **The Australian overlap is not a misprint and is worth a sentence in an answer.** Leong's own
> p. 122 explains it: the Australian winter high drives an outblowing **South-East Monsoon** which
> **crosses the equator** and arrives over Asia as the **South-West Monsoon**; in the opposite
> season, the Asiatic high drives the **North-East Monsoon**, which **crosses the equator** and
> arrives over northern Australia as the **North-West Monsoon**. Northern Australia is therefore a
> genuine monsoon coast, while its north-east coast, under the South-East Trades, is a genuine
> marine coast. **One continent, two types, because it has two differently exposed coasts.**

✅ **Leong's Fig. 125 labels, read off the rendered page:** *Asiatic Low · Punjab Low · Himalayan
barrier · S.W. Monsoon · S.E. Monsoon (Trades)* for the summer panel; *Asiatic High · Punjab High ·
N.E. Monsoon · N.W. Monsoon* for the winter panel. Note that **Leong names the Indian summer low
"the Punjab Low"**, which becomes important at Lesson 15, where he draws exactly the wrong
conclusion from it.

### The causal chain — why the monsoon list is a block

```text
   A very large landmass (Eurasia) adjoins a very large ocean (Indian Ocean)
        |
        v
   Summer: the interior heats far faster than the sea; Leong p.122 prints
   "Central Asia, backed by the lofty Himalayan ranges, is more than 15 F
   hotter than its normal temperature"  [= 8.3 C, a CONVERSION of a
   temperature DIFFERENCE, not of a level]
        |
        v
   A deep continental low develops; the pressure gradient reverses across
   the whole basin, not merely across one shore
        |
        v
   Air is drawn in from the SOUTHERN hemisphere, crosses the equator and
   is turned by the Coriolis effect into a SOUTH-WESTERLY current
        |
        v
   EVERY coast of that basin facing the current is monsoonal -> a BLOCK
        |
        v
   Winter: the gradient reverses again; the outblowing NE Monsoon crosses
   the equator and reaches northern Australia as the NW Monsoon -> the
   OUTLIER in the list

  ASSUMPTION: that continental size is the operative variable.
  LIMIT: the size argument explains WHERE monsoons are possible. It does not
  explain WHEN they arrive, WHY they burst rather than creep, or WHY they
  fail. Those require Lessons 7, 8 and 18, and the inadequacy of the size
  argument alone is exactly the charge Flohn lays against Halley at Lesson 5.
```

### The India hook, with its limitation printed beside it

⚠️ **India sits inside Leong's 10°-30° band, and Leong's monsoon chapter never says so.** The
subcontinent spans roughly 8°N to 37°N, so its peninsular bulk lies inside the band and its
northern plains lie beyond it — yet the plains are the most monsoonal part of the country, and
Leong's own Fig. 125 puts its **Punjab Low** at about 30°N, on the band's edge.

> ⛔ **Limitation that must be printed with it.** The band is **Topic 14's table**, not this
> chapter's, and a band that excludes the Punjab plains cannot be the criterion that makes the
> Punjab plains monsoonal. The band is a **tabulating convenience** in a classification scheme. The
> criterion is the **wind reversal** of Lesson 1, and the classification itself is routed to
> `Geography/basic/14`.

### Comparison, objection, reply and the unresolved residual

**Comparison — the two types by what makes them wet.** The marine type is wet because a wind that
never stops arriving carries moisture onto a shore; its rainfall is **orographic plus convectional**
and is spread through the year. The monsoon type is wet because a wind arrives for part of the year
only, and it arrives with far greater mass flux; its rainfall is concentrated. The disagreement
between the two is therefore not about **how much** rain but about **how the rain is organised in
time** — which is exactly the distinction Lesson 3 measures with two stations.

**Objection.** *"Distribution lists are the lowest-value content in a geography paper. Learn the
mechanism and skip the lists."*

**Reply.** Partly true and dangerous where it is false. A Mains answer rarely needs all eight marine
names. But it very often needs **two** of them, used as counter-cases: *eastern Brazil* and *the
Guinea Coast* are the standard demonstration that a tropical eastern coast can be permanently wet
without any reversal, which is the cleanest way to show that reversal — not latitude, not heat — is
what makes a monsoon. And **north-eastern Australia** is the single name that proves a continent can
hold both types at once. Three names, carrying an argument, beat eight names carrying none.

**Unresolved residual.** **Leong's Chapter 16 names no Indian station other than Bombay, Madras,
Punjab, Sind, Cherrapunji and the Ganges-Brahmaputra delta** (negative finding **N-03**). There is no
Kerala in it, no Western Ghats rainfall figure, and no onset date. The Indian spatial detail that
every Mains answer needs is therefore **not in the world-climate book at all**; it is in Khullar,
and it arrives at Lessons 10 to 12. This edition records the absence rather than filling it from
memory.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Citing "10°-30° N and S" as Leong's monsoon-chapter band.** It is on **printed p. 114**, in a
  classification table owned by `Geography/basic/14`. **C-02.**
- ❌ **Putting the two types on different latitudes.** They occupy the **same** Hot-Zone band in the
  same table. The separator is the **wind**, not the parallel.
- ❌ **Treating "eastern coast" as sufficient for the marine type.** It must be an eastern coast
  **reached by an onshore trade wind**; eastern coasts in the reversal zone are monsoonal instead.
- ❌ **Writing Australia into one list.** Leong puts **northern** Australia in the monsoon list and
  **north-eastern** Australia in the marine list.
- ❌ **Calling the Indian summer low "the Thar low" when quoting Leong.** Leong's printed label is
  the **Punjab Low**. The **Thar as a desert** is `Geography/advanced/07`'s.
- ❌ **Expecting Kerala, the Ghats or an onset date from Leong.** **N-03**: they are not there.
- ❌ **Naming the South-East Monsoon as a trade wind only.** Leong's own Fig. 125 labels it
  *"S.E. Monsoon (Trades)"* — it is both, and the point of the label is that it **crosses the
  equator** and changes its name.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I, *"Salient features of world's physical geography"* — the world distribution of
two named climatic types, which is C1 §1's owned clause.

**PYQ position.** No verified demand is owned here. One **adjacent** item must be named and refused:
**2018 Prelims Q87**, on coral-reef distribution in tropical waters, is routed to
`Geography/basic/11_Islands-and-Coral-Reefs`; it shares this lesson's tropical-marine coastlines and
is not a climate question. ⛔ **No answer letter is recorded or inferred for it or for any other
item in this edition.**

**Answer use.** When a question asks *where*, answer in three moves and never in one: **(1) the band
with its provenance** — "Leong's world-types table places both in the 10°-30° belt, though his
monsoon chapter states no band"; **(2) the two shapes** — a contiguous Asian block against a scatter
of eastern coasts; **(3) one counter-case** — eastern Brazil or the Guinea Coast, permanently wet
under trades with no reversal at all. That structure converts a list into an argument, which is
what the directive *"distinguish"* is actually asking for.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **Monsoon list, verbatim (Leong p. 122):** *"best developed in the **Indian sub-continent,
   Burma, Thailand, Laos, Cambodia, parts of Vietnam and south China and northern Australia**"*.
2. ✅ **Marine list, verbatim (Leong p. 122):** *"**Central America, West Indies, north-eastern
   Australia, the Philippines, parts of East Africa, Madagascar, the Guinea Coast and eastern
   Brazil** (Fig. 124)"* — and **C1 line 14 reproduces this list correctly**.
3. ✗ **C-02:** the **10°-30° N and S** band is on **Leong printed p. 114**, in the World Climatic
   Types table; **Chapter 16 prints no band** (**N-02**).
4. ✅ **Printed p. 114, Hot Zone rows:** **2(a) Tropical Monsoon**, **2(b) Tropical Marine**,
   **3 Sudan**.
5. ✅ **Summer thermal statement (p. 122):** *"Central Asia, backed by the lofty Himalayan ranges, is
   more than **15 °F** hotter than its normal temperature"* [**= 8.3 °C**, conversion of a
   *difference*].
6. ✅ **The cross-equatorial pair (p. 122):** the Australian winter high drives an outblowing
   **South-East Monsoon** that **crosses the equator** and arrives as the **South-West Monsoon**;
   the Asiatic high drives the **North-East Monsoon**, which crosses the equator and arrives over
   northern Australia as the **North-West Monsoon**.
7. ✅ **Fig. 125 labels:** *Asiatic Low · **Punjab Low** · Himalayan barrier · S.W. Monsoon ·
   S.E. Monsoon (Trades)*; *Asiatic High · **Punjab High** · N.E. Monsoon · N.W. Monsoon*.
8. ⚠️ **The shape argument:** monsoon = a **block** (needs a continent); marine = a **scatter**
   (needs only a trade wind at a shore). **North-eastern Australia proves one landmass can hold
   both.**
9. ✗ **N-03:** Leong's Chapter 16 names **no Indian station** other than **Bombay, Madras, Punjab,
   Sind, Cherrapunji** and the **Ganges-Brahmaputra delta** — no Kerala, no Ghats figure, no onset
   date.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Leong places the tropical monsoon climate between 10° and 30°
north and south." Is the band real, is the attribution correct, and what difference does the answer
make to an examination script?

**Model answer:** The band is **real but misattributed**. It is printed at **Leong printed p. 114**,
in the *World Climatic Types* table, under *"Hot Zone, latitudes 10°-30° N and S"*, where rows
**2(a) Tropical Monsoon**, **2(b) Tropical Marine** and **3 Sudan** all sit. **Leong's Chapter 16
itself states no latitude band** and goes straight to the regional lists. The difference it makes is
real: p. 114 is a **classification** table owned by `Geography/basic/14`, so the band is a tabulating
convenience, not a monsoon criterion — and it **excludes the Punjab plains**, which are among the
most monsoonal ground in Asia and which Leong's own Fig. 125 labels with a *Punjab Low*. A band that
excludes the clearest case cannot be the criterion. *(119 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That a latitude band defines a climatic type. The band summarises where
the type usually falls; the **criterion is the wind regime**, and in this pair it is the only thing
separating two types that share the same latitudes. *(39 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 2

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** Account for the contrasting spatial patterns of the
tropical monsoon and the tropical marine climates.

**Model:** The contrast is a contrast of **shapes**, and each shape follows from its wind. Leong
places the monsoon type in *"the Indian sub-continent, Burma, Thailand, Laos, Cambodia, parts of
Vietnam and south China and northern Australia"* — a **contiguous Asian block with one Australian
outlier** — because reversal requires a continent large enough to reverse a basin-wide pressure
gradient; Leong records Central Asia in summer as *"more than 15 °F"* [8.3 °C] above normal. The
marine type is a **scatter** — *"Central America, West Indies, north-eastern Australia, the
Philippines, parts of East Africa, Madagascar, the Guinea Coast and eastern Brazil"* — because it
needs only an onshore Trade Wind meeting a shore, a condition met on eastern coasts worldwide.
**Australia holds both**, its northern coast monsoonal and its north-eastern coast marine. Both lie
in the same 10°-30° belt of Leong's world-types table; **latitude separates neither.** *(141 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** **Spatial inventory** — both printed lists reproduced accurately, not
summarised into regions **3** · **Provenance accuracy** — the band attributed to the world-types
table rather than to the monsoon chapter **3** · **Band justification** — continental scale versus
trade-wind arrival supplied as the reason for each shape **3** · **Omission control** — the
Australian dual case retained rather than dropped as an inconvenience **1**

## Lesson 3 — The two climates by their numbers — Bombay against Cairns

```text

📚 Owner context : C1 §3 line 43 states that tropical marine rainfall is
                   "commonly around 150 cm+ in the source notes". DEFECT C-03:
                   no certified source prints 150 cm for this type. The printed
                   figures are 70 in [177.8 cm] for the regime at p.114 and
                   88 in [223.5 cm] for the Cairns station at Fig. 126(b).
                   DEFECT C-04: neither owner carries ANY rainfall figure for the
                   tropical MONSOON type. Leong p.114 prints 60 in [152.4 cm] -
                   which makes the MARINE type the wetter of the two.
📕 Book evidence : Leong printed p.114 (scan PDF 121) - rows 2(a) 60 in, 2(b)
                   70 in, 3 Sudan 30 in · Leong printed p.123 (scan PDF 130) -
                   Fig. 126(a) Bombay data block and the monthly rain series ·
                   Leong printed p.124 (scan PDF 131) - Fig. 126(b) Cairns data
                   block and the "no month without any rainfall" sentence
🔍 Live check    : none required. These are 20th-century station normals with no
                   stated base period; a 2026 endpoint would not be comparable
                   data and would create a false currency. None was sought.
📰 CA anchor     : none required, for the reason above. The modern Indian
                   rainfall apparatus arrives at Lesson 17 with its own dates.
🧭 Route-outs    : Geography/basic/14 (Koppen's Am/Aw thresholds and the "dry
                   month" criterion) · Geography/basic/13 (tropical cyclones,
                   hurricanes, typhoons - Leong's Chapter 13 cross-reference) ·
                   Geography/basic/15 (the equatorial Af comparison case)
⚠️ Live gaps     : N-07 (neither owner carries a monsoon-type rainfall figure) is
                   a NEGATIVE finding carried here. U-04: Leong's Figs 124-127
                   were read at caption and axis level; their internal labelling
                   was not transcribed exhaustively, and nothing below rests on
                   an untranscribed label. Map-bank rows ABSENT for Cairns,
                   Queensland and Coromandel (C-31).
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

Progress: 3 / 20 · Stage: Foundation · Primary visual grammar: **twin climograph rendered as paired ASCII bar ladders**

### The intuition, before any term

Everybody assumes the monsoon type is the wetter of the two. It has the drama, the burst, the
flooding and the 1,100-centimetre station. So the assumption is natural, and in the one book that
tabulates both, **it is wrong**.

Leong's own world-types table gives the **tropical monsoon** type *"Heavy summer rain. **60
inches**"* and the **tropical marine** type *"Much summer rain: **70 inches**"*. The marine type is
the wetter regime by ten inches, and it is wetter while having **no dry season at all**.

Once you see that, the real distinction snaps into focus. The two types do not differ principally in
**how much** rain falls. They differ in **how the rain is distributed through the year** — and the
cleanest way to see distribution is to put two stations side by side and look at the shape of the
year, not at the total. That is what this lesson does, with the two stations Leong himself chose.

### The primary visual — two years, two shapes

```text
  TWIN CLIMOGRAPH, DRAWN FROM LEONG'S OWN TWO STATION BLOCKS
  Left : BOMBAY, INDIA. 18 deg 55' N, 73 E. Altitude 37 ft [11.3 m].
         Annual precipitation 72 in [182.9 cm]. Annual range 10 F
         [5.6 C], 86 F down to 76 F [30.0 C down to 24.4 C].
         Source: Leong printed p.123, Fig. 126(a) and text.
  Right: CAIRNS, QUEENSLAND. 17 S, 145 deg 42' E. Under the SE Trades.
         Annual precipitation 88 in [223.5 cm]. Range 12 F [6.7 C],
         max 82 F Jan, min 70 F Jul [27.8 C / 21.1 C].
         Source: Leong printed p.124, Fig. 126(b) and text.
  Bar unit: one '#' is approximately 2 inches of rainfall.

        BOMBAY  (monsoon)                    CAIRNS  (marine)
  Jan   .                                    ########         15.8 in
  Feb   .                                    ########         16.4 in
  Mar   .                                    #########        17.7 in
  Apr   .                                    ######           12.1 in
  May   .                                    ####             (summer max
  Jun   ##########            19.9 in        ###               has passed;
  Jul   ############          24.0 in        ##                NO MONTH IS
  Aug   #######               14.5 in        ##                WITHOUT RAIN)
  Sep   #####                 10.6 in        ###
  Oct   .                                    ####
  Nov   .                                    #####
  Dec   .                                    #######

  WHAT THE TWO SHAPES SAY
   BOMBAY : four bars and eight blanks.  Leong, p.123: "As much as
            95 PER CENT of the annual rainfall is concentrated within
            four months."   ARITHMETIC CHECK AT GENERATION:
            19.9 + 24.0 + 14.5 + 10.6 = 69.0 in of a printed annual
            72 in = 95.8 %.  THE BOOK'S OWN FIGURES CONFIRM THE BOOK'S
            OWN CLAIM.  This is the single cleanest seasonality
            statistic in the chapter.
   CAIRNS : twelve bars, none of them zero.  Leong, p.124: a summer
            maximum "BUT WITHOUT ANY DISTINCT DRY PERIOD" and "no month
            without any rainfall".  Jan-Apr alone = 15.8 + 16.4 + 17.7
            + 12.1 = 62.0 in of 88 in = 70.5 %, so Cairns is strongly
            SEASONAL TOO - and still has no dry month.

  THE DISCRIMINATION THAT ACTUALLY MATTERS
   It is NOT "seasonal versus even". Cairns is seasonal.
   It is "seasonal WITH a dry season" versus "seasonal WITHOUT one".
   Bombay has eight effectively rainless months. Cairns has none.
```

**Why the Cairns arithmetic is in the visual.** Because it destroys the lazy version of the contrast.
If you teach "monsoon = seasonal, marine = even", a candidate meets Cairns' 70 per cent in four
months and concludes the book is wrong. The book is not wrong; the lazy formulation is. **Both types
are summer-maximum regimes.** Only one of them switches off.

### The printed numbers, laid out with their units intact

| Quantity | ✅ Tropical monsoon | ✅ Tropical marine | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Regime rainfall** | *"Heavy summer rain. **60 inches**"* [**152.4 cm**] | *"Much summer rain: **70 inches**"* [**177.8 cm**] | Leong printed p. 114, rows 2(a) and 2(b) |
| **Sudan comparison row** | — | — | row 3: *"Rain mainly in summer: **30 inches**"* [**76.2 cm**] |
| **Station** | **Bombay**, 18°55′N, 73°E, alt. **37 ft** [11.3 m] | **Cairns**, Queensland, 17°S, 145°42′E | Figs. 126(a), 126(b) |
| **Station annual rainfall** | **72 in** [**182.9 cm**] | **88 in** [**223.5 cm**] | Figs. 126(a), 126(b) |
| **Annual temperature range** | **10 °F** [**5.6 °C**], 86° to 76 °F [30.0° to 24.4 °C] | **12 °F** [**6.7 °C**], max 82 °F Jan [27.8 °C], min 70 °F Jul [21.1 °C] | Figs. 126(a), 126(b) |
| **Concentration** | *"**95 per cent** … within four months"* | *"**no month without any rainfall**"* | pp. 123, 124 |
| **Hazard note** | — | *"more favourable for habitation, but prone to severe tropical cyclones, hurricanes or typhoons (Chapter 13)"* | p. 124 ↪ routed |
| **Vegetation** | Monsoon forests | — (not given in the p. 114 row) | p. 114 |

> ✗ **C-03, stated exactly.** C1 §3 line 43 gives the tropical marine type *"commonly around **150
> cm+** in the source notes"*. **No certified source prints 150 cm.** The printed values are
> **70 in = 177.8 cm** (regime, p. 114) and **88 in = 223.5 cm** (Cairns station, Fig. 126b). The
> owner's figure is both **unsourced** and **low** — and it is low in the one direction that
> preserves the false intuition that the marine type is the drier of the two.

> ✗ **C-04, stated exactly.** **Neither owner carries any rainfall figure at all for the tropical
> monsoon type** (**N-07**). Leong p. 114 prints **60 in = 152.4 cm**. Printing it immediately
> produces the inversion: ⚠️ **on Leong's own table the marine type is the wetter regime.**

### The causal chain — why the wetter type is the one without a dry season

```text
   MARINE COAST                            MONSOON COAST
   A trade wind arrives from a             A wind arrives from the ocean for
   warm ocean and NEVER STOPS              PART of the year and from the
   arriving                                continent for the rest
        |                                       |
        v                                       v
   Moisture supply is CONTINUOUS           Moisture supply is SWITCHED
        |                                       |
        v                                       v
   Uplift is orographic where the          Uplift is orographic AND
   trades meet uplands (Leong names        convectional, but ONLY while the
   eastern Brazil) plus convectional       oceanic phase lasts
   from daytime and summer heating
        |                                       |
        v                                       v
   Rain in EVERY month; a summer           Rain CONCENTRATED: Bombay 95 % in
   maximum but "without any distinct       four months; eight months
   dry period"                             effectively rainless
        |                                       |
        v                                       v
   HIGHER ANNUAL TOTAL on Leong's          LOWER REGIME TOTAL on the same
   table: 70 in [177.8 cm]                 table: 60 in [152.4 cm]

  WHY THE INTUITION FAILS: a dramatic four-month total is mistaken for a
  large annual total. Intensity is not accumulation.

  LIMIT OF THE CHAIN: it is built from ONE station per type, with NO STATED
  BASE PERIOD in either figure block, and Bombay is a WEST-coast monsoon
  station with a mountain behind it. Cherrapunji's 1,102 cm (Lesson 12) is
  also a monsoon station, and it out-rains every marine station on Earth.
  THE REGIME AVERAGE AND THE EXTREME STATION ARE DIFFERENT QUANTITIES.
```

### The India hook, with its limitation printed beside it

⚠️ **Bombay's "95 per cent of the annual rainfall within four months" is the sharpest seasonality
figure in the entire chapter, and the book's own monthly series verifies it.** 19.9 + 24.0 + 14.5 +
10.6 inches = 69.0 inches against a printed annual 72 inches, which is **95.8 per cent** — an
independent arithmetic check performed at generation. It is also the number that explains why Indian
water policy is about **storage**, since a country cannot consume its rainfall in the months in
which it arrives.

> ⛔ **Limitation that must be printed with it.** It is **one station**, reported **in inches**,
> with **no stated base period**, in a book whose figure blocks never give one. It is a west-coast
> station with the Western Ghats immediately behind it, so it is nearer the maximum of the Indian
> seasonality distribution than its centre. Khullar's national figure for the same idea — **about
> 87 per cent** of plains rainfall in the season — is a different and better-founded quantity, and
> it arrives at Lesson 17 with its own conflict attached.

### Comparison, objection, reply and the unresolved residual

**Comparison — the temperature ranges are nearly identical, and that is the point.** Bombay's
annual range is **10 °F** [5.6 °C]; Cairns' is **12 °F** [6.7 °C]. Two stations on opposite sides of
the equator, in two different climatic types, differ by **2 °F** [1.1 °C] in annual range. ⚠️ The
inference is clean and worth stating in an answer: **within the hot zone, temperature does not
discriminate between climatic types; rainfall regime does.** That is why both types sit in the same
row-band of the p. 114 table and are separated only by the rainfall column.

**Objection.** *"Leong's 60-inch and 70-inch figures are regime generalisations from a schools
textbook. Quoting them as though they settled anything is false precision."*

**Reply.** The objection is right about what the figures are and wrong about what they are for.
These are **not** precision estimates of world rainfall and nothing here treats them as such. They
are the **internal structure of the source the owner cites**, and the owner's unsourced 150 cm
contradicts them. The use is comparative and internal: *within Leong's own table*, the marine row is
wetter than the monsoon row, so any claim that the monsoon type is definitionally the wetter of the
two cannot be supported from the book that is being cited for it. That is a conclusion about
**attribution**, which is exactly where candidates lose marks, and it does not require the numbers
to be accurate world averages.

**Unresolved residual.** **Neither figure block states a base period, and no certified source gives
one.** Leong's Fig. 126(a) and 126(b) give coordinates, altitude, an annual total and a range, and
nothing about the years over which they were computed. Every comparison in this lesson is therefore
**internally valid and externally undated**, and this edition says so rather than attaching a modern
normal period to a mid-century textbook figure.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Assuming the monsoon type is the wetter type.** On **Leong p. 114** it is not: **60 in**
  against **70 in**.
- ❌ **Repeating C1's "around 150 cm+" for the marine type.** **C-03**: unsourced, and low.
- ❌ **Converting silently.** The book prints **inches** and **degrees Fahrenheit**. The printed unit
  leads; the metric value follows in brackets and is marked as a conversion. **R-07.**
- ❌ **Converting a temperature *range* as though it were a temperature *level*.** A range of 10 °F
  is **5.6 °C**, not 5.6 degrees below a level. The conversion of a difference drops the 32.
- ❌ **Writing "marine rainfall is evenly distributed".** Leong says *"a tendency to a summer
  maximum"* and, at Cairns, **70.5 per cent falls in January to April**. The distinguishing property
  is the **absence of a dry month**, not evenness.
- ❌ **Using Cherrapunji to prove the monsoon type is wetter.** The regime average and the extreme
  station are different quantities; Lesson 12 holds the extreme.
- ❌ **Deriving a Köppen class from these figures.** The **Am / Aw** boundary and the dry-month
  criterion belong to `Geography/basic/14`.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I, *"Salient features of world's physical geography"* — the quantitative identity
of two named types, which is C1 §3's owned clause, repaired at **C-03** and **C-04**.

**PYQ position.** No verified demand is owned here. The lesson supports **Lesson 20's** owned 2026
Prelims demand on **Andaman and Nicobar climate and seasonal precipitation** by establishing the
distinction the demand turns on — *annual total* against *seasonal distribution*. ⛔ The 2026 Set-A
key held locally is **provisional**; its existence is stated and **no answer letter is recorded or
inferred**.

**Answer use.** When a question says *"distinguish"*, give a **number pair** and then the **shape**.
Two sentences: *"Leong's world-types table gives the monsoon regime 60 inches [152.4 cm] and the
marine regime 70 inches [177.8 cm], so the marine type is the wetter of the two on his own figures.
The difference is one of distribution rather than quantity: Bombay concentrates 95 per cent of 72
inches into four months, while Cairns, with 88 inches, has 'no month without any rainfall'."* That
is a complete discriminating paragraph in fifty-five words and it cites both sides.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **Leong p. 114, row 2(a) Tropical Monsoon:** *"Heavy summer rain. **60 inches**"* [152.4 cm];
   vegetation **Monsoon forests**.
2. ✅ **Leong p. 114, row 2(b) Tropical Marine:** *"Much summer rain: **70 inches**"* [177.8 cm].
   ⚠️ **The marine type is the wetter regime on the book's own table.**
3. ✅ **Row 3 Sudan, for scale:** *"Rain mainly in summer: **30 inches**"* [76.2 cm].
4. ✗ **C-03:** C1's *"commonly around **150 cm+**"* for the marine type is **unsourced**.
5. ✗ **C-04 / N-07:** **neither owner carries a monsoon-type rainfall figure at all.**
6. ✅ **Bombay, Fig. 126(a):** **18°55′N, 73°E**, altitude **37 ft** [11.3 m], annual **72 in**
   [182.9 cm], range **10 °F** [5.6 °C] from **86 °F to 76 °F** [30.0 °C to 24.4 °C].
7. ✅ **Bombay's monthly series:** **June 19.9 in · July 24 in · August 14.5 in · September 10.6
   in**; *"As much as **95 per cent** of the annual rainfall is concentrated within four months."*
   **Verified at generation: 69.0 of 72 in = 95.8 %.**
8. ✅ **Cairns, Fig. 126(b):** **17°S, 145°42′E**, under the **South-East Trade Winds**, annual
   **88 in** [223.5 cm], max **82 °F Jan** [27.8 °C], min **70 °F Jul** [21.1 °C], range **12 °F**
   [6.7 °C].
9. ✅ **Cairns' wettest months:** **Jan 15.8 · Feb 16.4 · Mar 17.7 · Apr 12.1 in**; summer maximum
   *"but without any distinct dry period"*; *"**no month without any rainfall**"*. **Verified at
   generation: 62.0 of 88 in = 70.5 % in four months — the marine type is seasonal too.**
10. ✅ **Leong on the marine type's habitability (p. 124):** *"more favourable for habitation, but
    prone to severe tropical cyclones, hurricanes or typhoons (Chapter 13)"* ↪ the storms are
    `Geography/basic/13`'s.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two statements are offered. (a) "The tropical monsoon climate is wetter than the
tropical marine climate." (b) "The tropical marine climate has evenly distributed rainfall." Assess
each against the printed evidence.

**Model answer:** **(a) is not supported by the source usually cited for it.** Leong's
world-climatic-types table at printed p. 114 gives the monsoon regime **60 inches** [152.4 cm] and
the marine regime **70 inches** [177.8 cm]; at station level, Bombay has **72 in** [182.9 cm] and
Cairns **88 in** [223.5 cm]. On Leong's own figures the **marine** type is the wetter. **(b) is also
wrong, though less obviously.** Leong records a *"tendency to a summer maximum"*, and Cairns takes
**62.0 of its 88 inches — 70.5 per cent — in January to April**. The marine type is strongly
seasonal. What it lacks is a **dry season**: *"no month without any rainfall"*. The true
discrimination is seasonal **with** a dry season against seasonal **without** one. *(120 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating "seasonal" and "concentrated into a wet half-year with a dry
half-year" as the same property. Both these types are summer-maximum regimes; only the monsoon type
switches off. *(28 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 3

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** "The difference between the tropical monsoon and
tropical marine climates is one of rainfall distribution rather than rainfall quantity." Examine.

**Model:** The proposition is supported by the standard text, and the text reverses the expected
quantity. Leong's world-types table gives the monsoon regime *"Heavy summer rain. **60 inches**"*
[152.4 cm] against the marine regime's *"Much summer rain: **70 inches**"* [177.8 cm]; at station
level **Bombay 72 in** [182.9 cm] against **Cairns 88 in** [223.5 cm]. **Quantity therefore favours
the marine type.** Distribution separates them absolutely. Bombay takes *"as much as 95 per cent of
the annual rainfall … within four months"* — 19.9, 24, 14.5 and 10.6 inches in June to September —
leaving eight effectively rainless months. Cairns peaks in January to April at 70.5 per cent, yet
Leong records a summer maximum *"without any distinct dry period"* and *"no month without any
rainfall"*. Both are seasonal; only one has a dry season. **Caution: single stations, in imperial
units, with no stated base period.** *(141 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** **Data selection** — both the regime pair and the station pair used, so the
reversal is shown twice **3** · **Unit integrity** — printed inches leading with marked conversions,
and a range converted as a difference **2** · **Comparative inference** — the seasonal/dry-season
distinction drawn rather than "seasonal versus even" **3** · **Station-level caution** — base-period
and single-station limits stated **2**

## Lesson 4 — How many seasons does India have? Three books, three answers
