Progress: 4 / 20 · Stage: Foundation · Primary visual grammar: **three-track seasonal calendar on one time axis**

### The intuition, before any term

"India has four seasons" is one of the first sentences any Indian student learns about Indian
climate, and almost nobody has ever asked **whose four seasons they are**. The answer turns out to
be awkward: of the three books that this topic actually rests on, **one prints three seasons, one
prints two seasons subdivided four ways, and the third does not print an Indian seasonal calendar at
all.** The familiar four-season table is in the owner file, marked as book-sourced, and it is in no
certified book.

The reason this matters is not accuracy for its own sake. A season boundary is a **claim about when
one circulation ends and another begins**, and the three calendars disagree about exactly the
boundaries that carry marks: whether the retreat ends in November or December, whether the hot
season ends in May or mid-June, and whether the retreat is a season at all or merely the tail of the
monsoon season.

Settle this now, before any seasonal narrative is taught, because every later lesson — onset,
break, withdrawal, the north-east monsoon — sits on one of these calendars.

### The primary visual — three calendars on one axis

```text
  THREE PRINTED SEASONAL SCHEMES FOR INDIA, ON ONE TIME AXIS
  Months run left to right. Each track is one source, quoted at its folio.

        J    F    M    A    M    J    J    A    S    O    N    D
      |----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|

  (A) LEONG, printed p.123 (scan PDF 130) - THREE SEASONS
      "In regions like the Indian sub-continent which have a true Tropical
       Monsoon Climate, THREE distinct seasons are distinguishable"
      [=== COOL DRY ===]  [== HOT DRY ==]  [= RAINY =]  [== COOL DRY ==]
       Oct to February     Mar to mid-June  mid-June to      (wraps)
                                             September
      |####|####|%%%%|%%%%|%%%%|%%..|~~~~|~~~~|~~~~|####|####|####|
        #### = cool dry     %%%% = hot dry     ~~~~ = rainy
        Note the SPLIT MONTH: the hot dry season ends at MID-JUNE, so
        June is divided. Leong does not use whole-month boundaries.

  (B) MAJID IWG folio 3.21 (scan PDF 94) - TWO SEASONS, FOUR SUB-SEASONS
      "(i) The season of NORTH-EAST monsoon: (a) January and February,
       winter season; (b) March to May, hot weather season.
       (ii) The season of SOUTH-WEST monsoon: (a) June to September,
       season of general rains; (b) OCTOBER TO DECEMBER, season of
       retreating monsoon."
      |<------- NE MONSOON SEASON ------>|<------ SW MONSOON SEASON ----->|
      |winter   |   hot weather          | general rains  | retreating    |
      |  Jan-Feb|      Mar-May           |   Jun-Sep      |   OCT-DEC     |
      The TOP-LEVEL unit is the MONSOON, not the season. Retreat is
      OCTOBER TO DECEMBER - three months, and it ends in DECEMBER.

  (C) C1 OWNER TABLE, lines 26-32 - FOUR SEASONS, MARKED AS BOOK-SOURCED
      |  Cool dry  |  Hot dry   |   SW Monsoon   | Retreating | Cool dry |
      |   Dec-Feb  |  Mar-May   |    Jun-Sep     |   Oct-Nov  |  (wraps) |
      Retreat is OCT-NOV - two months, ending in NOVEMBER.
      NO CERTIFIED BOOK PRINTS THIS TABLE.      <-- CORRECTION C-01

  WHERE THE THREE DISAGREE, STATED AS DISAGREEMENTS
   1. COUNT        : 3 (Leong) / 2 top-level + 4 sub (Majid) / 4 (C1).
   2. RETREAT END  : not a season at all (Leong) / DECEMBER (Majid) /
                     NOVEMBER (C1).
   3. HOT-SEASON END: MID-JUNE (Leong) / END OF MAY (Majid, C1).
   4. ORGANISING UNIT: the SEASON (Leong, C1) / the MONSOON (Majid).

  THE DISAGREEMENT AT (2) IS THE ONE THAT PAYS. Lesson 16 shows that
  Tamil Nadu's main rainy season is OCTOBER-NOVEMBER and that Khullar
  folio 148 has the monsoon withdrawing from the extreme south-east
  "by DECEMBER". A calendar that closes the retreat in November cannot
  accommodate the country's own south-eastern rainfall maximum.
```

### Correction C-01, stated exactly

✗ **C1 §2's four-season table carries the mark meaning *from the source book*, and no certified
source prints it.** The three certified alternatives are set out above. The defect is not that four
seasons is a wrong convention — it is the convention IMD uses administratively, and it is pedagogically
reasonable — but that it is **attributed to books that print something else**. A candidate who
defends "Oct-Nov, retreating monsoon, from Leong" in a viva or an answer is defending a sentence
Leong did not write.

⚠️ **The safe formulation**, which is both honest and more impressive than the table: *"Indian
seasonal calendars are conventions and the standard texts differ. Leong distinguishes three seasons
— cool dry October to February, hot dry March to mid-June, rainy mid-June to September. Majid
organises the year under two monsoons subdivided four ways, with the retreating monsoon running
October to December. The familiar four-season division, used administratively, closes the retreat in
November."*

### What each calendar carries with it

**(A) Leong's three seasons, with their printed payload (p. 123).** This is the richest block, and
C1 uses almost none of it.

| Season | ✅ Printed payload |
|---|---|
| **Cool dry, October to February** | **Bombay 76 °F** [24.4 °C]; **Punjab only 50 °F** [10.0 °C]; frosts at night in the colder north; a high-pressure centre over the **Punjab**; small Punjab rain from **cyclonic** sources, *vital for winter cereals*; the NE Monsoon crosses the Bay of Bengal and rains on the south-eastern tip — *"In **Madras 50 inches** of rain falls during **October and November**, accounting for **half** its annual rainfall."* [50 in = **127.0 cm**] |
| **Hot dry, March to mid-June** | **Bombay mean May 86 °F** [30.0 °C]; day temperatures of **95 °F** [35.0 °C] usual in central India; **Sind may be as high as 110 °F** [43.3 °C]; coastal relief by sea breezes; an intense low over **north-west India**; **duststorms**; the rains *"**break**"* by the **middle of June** |
| **Rainy, mid-June to September** | **Bombay: 19.9 in June, 24 in July, 14.5 in August, 10.6 in September**; *"As much as **95 per cent** of the annual rainfall is concentrated within four months"*; rain lowers temperature — Bombay **86 °F May → 81 °F July** [30.0 °C → 27.2 °C], and in the north the drop is *"as much as **13 °F**"* [7.2 °C] |

> ⚠️ **Notice what Leong's cool-dry season already contains.** The **Madras October-November
> rainfall** — half the station's annual total — is printed inside the *cool dry* season, not inside
> a separate retreating-monsoon season. Leong has no retreat season, so the north-east monsoon rain
> has to live somewhere, and he puts it in the cool season. **Majid, who does have a retreat season,
> runs it to December and would place the same rain there.** The two books are not describing
> different weather; they are **cutting the same year at different joints**, and the Madras rainfall
> is the test case. Lesson 16 is where this is resolved into an answer.

**(B) Majid's two-monsoon scheme (folio 3.21).** Its virtue is that the **top-level unit is the
circulation**, not the thermometer: the year is divided into the season *of* the north-east monsoon
and the season *of* the south-west monsoon, and the thermal seasons are subdivisions inside them.
⚠️ This is the more physically disciplined scheme, and it is the one that makes the **retreating
monsoon a part of the south-west monsoon season** rather than an interval between two things.

**(C) The pre-monsoon showers, certified only at Khullar folio 140.** The hot-weather season has a
nomenclature that examiners like, and only part of it is certified in this lane.

- ✅ Nor'wester rain is printed as ***spring storm showers***.
- ✅ In **Assam** the May rainfall is about **two-thirds of June's** and is called the ***tea
  showers***.
- ✅ Coastal **Kerala and Karnataka** receive about **25 cm** from thunderstorms and the interior of
  south India about **10 cm**.
- ✅ These are ***mango showers*** in **Tamil Nadu and Andhra Pradesh** and ***cherry blossoms*** in
  **Karnataka**.

> ⛔ **U-03, live in this lesson.** C1's table names the ***Loo*** and the ***Kalbaisakhi***. Those
> terms sit on **Khullar folios 132-139 and 149-151**, which were rendered in the audit lane but
> **not read in full**, and were **not re-certified at generation**. This edition therefore **does
> not quote a Khullar sentence about the Loo or the Kalbaisakhi**. It records that C1 prints them,
> teaches the certified folio-140 shower nomenclature instead, and ↪ routes the **nor'wester,
> Kalbaisakhi and Loo as severe-weather systems** to `Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements` and
> `Geography/advanced/13_India-JetStream-Western-Disturbances`, which own them.

### The causal chain — why calendars disagree

```text
   A season boundary is a CLAIM that one circulation has ended
        |
        v
   But circulations do not end on the first of a month. Leong says the
   rains "break" by the MIDDLE OF JUNE; Khullar folio 142 records onsets
   from 11 MAY to 18 JUNE across the record (Lesson 10)
        |
        v
   So every calendar must choose a CONVENTION, and a convention has a
   PURPOSE
        |
        +--> TEACHING purpose  -> round months, four tidy seasons (C1)
        |
        +--> CLIMATOLOGICAL purpose -> cut at the circulation, two
        |    monsoons subdivided (Majid)
        |
        +--> DESCRIPTIVE purpose -> cut where the WEATHER a traveller
             experiences changes, which is mid-June (Leong)
        |
        v
   THE DISAGREEMENT IS NOT AN ERROR. It becomes an error only when a
   convention adopted for one purpose is ATTRIBUTED to a source that
   adopted a different one.  <-- which is exactly C-01

  LIMIT: none of the three calendars is derivable from the others, and
  no certified source adjudicates between them. This edition prints all
  three and adopts none.
```

### The India hook, with its limitation printed beside it

⚠️ **"India has four seasons" is a teaching and administrative convention, not a finding.** It is
serviceable, it maps onto the kharif-rabi agricultural year, and it is what a candidate will have
been taught since school.

> ⛔ **Limitation that must be printed with it.** **No certified book in this topic's corpus prints
> it.** Leong prints three; Majid prints two subdivided four ways and runs the retreat to **December**,
> not November. Writing the four-season table is fine; writing it **as Leong's** is **C-01**, and the
> November end-date it carries is contradicted by Khullar folio 148's own statement that the monsoon
> withdraws from the extreme south-eastern tip *"by December"*.

### Comparison, objection, reply and the unresolved residual

**Comparison — why Leong and Majid cut the year differently.** Leong is writing a **descriptive
world geography**: he asks what a place is like, so he cuts the year where the experienced weather
changes, which is why his boundaries are mid-month and why he has no retreat season — to a resident
of Madras, October is simply when it rains. Majid is writing an **Indian systematic geography**: he
asks what the circulation is doing, so he cuts the year where the wind system changes, which forces
a retreat season and forces it to last until the north-east monsoon is fully established in
December. The prior commitment produces the boundary; neither is careless.

**Objection.** *"Every Indian textbook, IMD and the school syllabus use four seasons. Insisting on
Leong's three is contrarian."*

**Reply.** Nothing here insists on three. The claim is narrower and harder to dispute: **the
four-season table must not be sourced to a book that prints a different one.** Two further points
make it pay. First, Leong's own end-of-chapter exercise at printed p. 127 reads *"The climate of
India is characterized by **three** distinct seasons. Explain why this is so."* — so the three-season
claim is not incidental to Leong, it is what he sets as the examinable proposition. Second, the
four-season table's **October-November retreat window cannot hold the north-east monsoon**, which
Khullar folio 148 runs to December and which is the main rainy season of Tamil Nadu. The
cleanest answer names the convention, names its author and does not claim it is universal.

**Unresolved residual.** **Which calendar is correct is not a question the sources can answer**,
because it is not a factual question. It is a question about the purpose of a classification, and
the three sources had three purposes. This edition prints all three with their folios and
**adopts none**, and the only sentence it forbids is "Leong's four seasons".

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Attributing the four-season table to Leong or to Majid.** **C-01.** Leong prints three; Majid
  prints two subdivided four ways.
- ❌ **Ending the retreating monsoon in November as though all sources agreed.** Majid runs it
  **October to December**; Khullar folio 148 has the monsoon leaving the extreme south-east *"by
  December"*.
- ❌ **Ending the hot season on 31 May when quoting Leong.** His hot dry season runs **March to
  mid-June**, and the rains *"break"* by the **middle of June**.
- ❌ **Treating the north-east monsoon rain as a separate season in Leong.** He has no retreat
  season; **Madras' 50 inches in October-November sits inside his cool dry season**.
- ❌ **Quoting a Khullar sentence for the Loo or the Kalbaisakhi.** **U-03**: those folios were not
  certified in this lane. The certified pre-monsoon vocabulary is *spring storm showers*, *tea
  showers*, *mango showers* and *cherry blossoms*, at folio 140.
- ❌ **Saying "four seasons" is wrong.** It is a convention, and conventions are not wrong. Only its
  **attribution** is.
- ❌ **Converting Leong's Punjab 50 °F to "50 degrees cold".** It is **10.0 °C**, a winter mean, and
  the sentence it belongs to is about **night frosts in the colder north**.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I — the seasonal structure of the Indian climatic year, which C1 §2 owns and
mis-sources.

**PYQ position.** No verified demand is owned here. The lesson is the **enabling foundation for
Lesson 16**, which serves the north-east monsoon, and for **Lesson 20's** owned 2026 Prelims demand
on **seasonal precipitation** in the Andaman and Nicobar Islands — a demand that turns on knowing
which season a given island's rain belongs to. ⛔ **No answer letter is recorded or inferred.**

**Answer use.** Open any seasonal question by **naming the convention you are using and its author**,
in one clause, then proceed. *"Following Leong's three-season division — cool dry October to
February, hot dry March to mid-June, rainy mid-June to September — …"* costs eighteen words and
immunises the entire answer against a boundary objection. If the question presupposes four seasons,
use four and add *"on the conventional four-season division"*. **Never write a season boundary
without an owner.**

### Revision points

1. ✗ **C-01:** C1 §2's **four-season table** is marked *from the source book*; **no certified source
   prints it**. This is the largest fidelity defect in the owner pair.
2. ✅ **Leong p. 123, verbatim:** *"In regions like the Indian sub-continent which have a true
   Tropical Monsoon Climate, **three** distinct seasons are distinguishable"* — **1.** cool dry
   **October to February**; **2.** hot dry **March to mid-June**; **3.** rainy **mid-June to
   September**.
3. ✅ **Leong p. 127 sets it as an exercise:** *"The climate of India is characterized by **three**
   distinct seasons. Explain why this is so."*
4. ✅ **Majid IWG folio 3.21:** **(i) season of the north-east monsoon** — (a) January-February,
   winter; (b) March-May, hot weather; **(ii) season of the south-west monsoon** — (a) June-September,
   general rains; (b) **October to December**, retreating monsoon.
5. ⚠️ **Three disagreements that matter:** the **count** (3 / 2+4 / 4); the **retreat end** (no
   retreat season / **December** / November); the **hot-season end** (**mid-June** / end of May).
6. ✅ **Cool dry payload:** **Bombay 76 °F** [24.4 °C] · **Punjab 50 °F** [10.0 °C] · night frosts in
   the colder north · high-pressure centre over the **Punjab** · small **cyclonic** Punjab rain
   *vital for winter cereals*.
7. ✅ **Madras:** *"**50 inches** of rain falls during **October and November**, accounting for
   **half** its annual rainfall"* [50 in = **127.0 cm**] — printed inside Leong's **cool dry**
   season.
8. ✅ **Hot dry payload:** **Bombay mean May 86 °F** [30.0 °C] · central India day temperatures
   **95 °F** [35.0 °C] · **Sind up to 110 °F** [43.3 °C] · sea-breeze relief on the coast · intense
   low over **north-west India** · **duststorms**.
9. ✅ **The break of the rains:** the rains *"**break**"* by the **middle of June**. The word *break*
   here means **onset**, and it is the opposite of the *break* of Lesson 14.
10. ✅ **Rainy-season cooling:** Bombay **86 °F May → 81 °F July** [30.0 °C → 27.2 °C]; in the north
    the drop is *"as much as **13 °F**"* [**7.2 °C**].
11. ✅ **Khullar folio 140, pre-monsoon showers:** nor'wester rain = ***spring storm showers***;
    Assam's May rainfall about **two-thirds of June's**, the ***tea showers***; coastal Kerala and
    Karnataka about **25 cm** and interior south India about **10 cm** from thunderstorms;
    ***mango showers*** in Tamil Nadu and Andhra Pradesh; ***cherry blossoms*** in Karnataka.
12. ⛔ **U-03:** the ***Loo*** and ***Kalbaisakhi*** sit on **Khullar folios 132-139 and 149-151**,
    rendered but **not certified**. No sentence here rests on them; they are ↪ routed to
    `Geography/basic/13` as severe-weather systems.
13. ⚠️ **Why the schemes differ:** descriptive purpose cuts at experienced weather (Leong);
    climatological purpose cuts at the circulation (Majid); teaching purpose cuts at round months
    (C1). **A convention becomes an error only when attributed to a source that used a different
    one.**
14. ⚠️ **The decisive test:** a retreat window closing in **November** cannot contain Tamil Nadu's
    October-December main rainy season, which **Khullar folio 148** runs to **December**. Lesson 16.

### Concept check

**Question:** An answer states: "According to G.C. Leong, India has four seasons — cool dry
December-February, hot dry March-May, south-west monsoon June-September and retreating monsoon
October-November." Identify every defect in that sentence.

**Model answer:** There are four. **First, the count is wrong for the author named.** Leong printed
p. 123 states that *"**three** distinct seasons are distinguishable"*, and his p. 127 exercise sets
exactly that claim. **Second, the boundaries are wrong.** Leong's cool dry season runs **October to
February**, not December to February, and his hot dry season runs **March to mid-June**, not to May.
**Third, Leong has no retreating-monsoon season at all**; the October-November Madras rainfall sits
inside his cool dry season. **Fourth, the retreat window is contradicted elsewhere** — Majid runs the
retreating monsoon **October to December**, and Khullar folio 148 has the monsoon withdrawing from
the extreme south-east *"by December"*. The four-season table is a usable convention; it is not
Leong's. *(119 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That a seasonal calendar is a fact to be recalled rather than a
convention to be attributed. Three certified sources cut the Indian year at three different sets of
joints, each for a stated purpose, and none of them prints the familiar four. *(43 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 4

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** "The seasonal division of the Indian year is a
convention, not an observation." Discuss with reference to the standard texts.

**Model:** It is a convention, and the texts prove it by disagreeing. **Leong** (printed p. 123)
distinguishes *"**three** distinct seasons"* — cool dry **October to February**, hot dry **March to
mid-June**, rainy **mid-June to September** — and sets that very claim as a chapter exercise.
**Majid** (folio 3.21) organises the year under **two monsoons** subdivided four ways, with the
retreating monsoon running **October to December**. The familiar **four**-season table closes the
retreat in **November**. The disagreements are purposeful: a descriptive geography cuts the year
where the experienced weather changes, a systematic geography cuts it where the circulation changes,
and a teaching table cuts it at round months. The choice carries consequence: a retreat ending in
November cannot contain Tamil Nadu's October-December maximum, which Khullar folio 148 runs *"by
December"*. **Name the convention and its author; do not present one as the fact.** *(140 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** **Source adjudication** — all three schemes attributed to their actual
sources, with the four-season table not sourced to a book **4** · **Calendar accuracy** — exact
boundaries reproduced, including the mid-June cut **2** · **Conflict disclosure** — the retreat-end
disagreement named rather than smoothed **2** · **Synthesis judgement** — the differences explained
by purpose rather than by error **2**

```text
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

Progress: 5 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **chronological argument spine with a failure branch**

### The intuition, before any term

The classical theory of the monsoon is the one everybody already believes. **Land heats faster than
sea in summer, so air rushes in from the sea; land cools faster in winter, so air rushes out.** It is
the sea breeze, enlarged until it fills a continent and slowed until its cycle takes a year instead
of a day.

It is worth being clear about how good this theory is, because the modern habit is to introduce it
only in order to knock it down. It is not a bad theory. It gets the **direction** right, the
**seasonality** right, the **approximate timing** right and the **basic energetics** right, and it
held the field for roughly three centuries against people who were actively looking for something
better. Any answer that treats Halley as a naive error has misread the problem.

What it cannot do is explain **why the monsoon behaves badly**. Why does it arrive suddenly instead
of strengthening gradually? Why does it stop for a fortnight in August while the land is still hot?
Why is western Rajasthan dry when it is the hottest land in the system and sits directly under the
current? Every one of those questions is about something the thermal theory does not contain, and
each of Lessons 6, 7, 8 and 14 exists to supply one of them.

### The primary visual — the argument spine, with its failure branch

```text
  THE CLASSICAL LINEAGE AND WHERE IT BREAKS
  Source: Khullar folio 123 (scan PDF 78, right), unless marked.

  ANTIQUITY
  |
  +-- The monsoons are MENTIONED in the RIG VEDA, and by GREEK and
  |   BUDDHIST scholars.  [mention, not explanation]
  |
  +-- "The first scientific study is credited to the ARABS."
      |
      +-- c. 10th CENTURY: AL MASUDI OF BAGHDAD describes the REVERSAL
      |   OF OCEAN CURRENTS AND MONSOON WINDS over the north Indian
      |   Ocean.   [observation of a REVERSAL - the ocean as well as
      |   the air. Note that this is simultaneously the first
      |   description of the monsoon current, which is Topic 12's.]
      |
      +-- 1554 A.D.: SIDI ALI reports the COMMENCEMENT DATES of the
          monsoon.  [the first ONSET-DATE record in the lineage -
          a navigator's number, 300 years before a meteorologist's]
  |
  v
  EXPLANATION
  |
  +-- 1686: SIR EDMUND HALLEY explains the monsoon by THERMAL
      CONTRAST, in two parts:
      |     (a) the SUMMER monsoon
      |     (b) the WINTER monsoon
      |
      +-- Khullar's own gloss: "Halley's ideas are basically the same
          as those involved in LAND AND SEA BREEZE except that... DAY
          AND NIGHT ARE REPLACED BY SUMMER AND WINTER."
          [<-- this is also KOPPEN/HANN/ANGOT's position at Lesson 1,

  |
```

**What the spine shows.** The lineage is not a list of names; it is **observation → observation →
explanation → dominance → three specific failures → nine named repairs**. The three failures are the
examinable spine of C2's own Mains angle, and each one names a later lesson. Teach the failures and
the next four lessons organise themselves.

### The technical content, with the vocabulary placed after the idea

- ✅ **The Arab priority.** Khullar folio 123 is explicit: monsoons are *mentioned* in the Rig Veda
  and by Greek and Buddhist scholars, but *"the first scientific study is credited to the **Arabs**"*.
  ⚠️ The distinction between a **mention** and a **study** is the one to carry: the Rig Veda records
  that the rains come; Al Masudi records that the system **reverses**.
- ✅ **Al Masudi of Baghdad, about the 10th century**, described *the reversal of **ocean currents
  and monsoon winds** over the north Indian Ocean*. ⚠️ Two things at once — the **wind** reversal of
  this topic and the **current** reversal, which is `Geography/basic/12`'s. ↪ The current itself is
  routed; the fact that one observer saw both is retained here because it is the earliest statement
  that the monsoon is an **ocean-atmosphere** system, which is the whole burden of Lesson 18.
- ✅ **Sidi Ali, 1554 A.D.**, reported **commencement dates**. ⚠️ Place this beside Lesson 10's IMD
  onset criteria and the point makes itself: an onset date was a **navigator's operational need**
  four centuries before it was a meteorologist's forecast product, and for exactly the same reason —
  somebody has to decide when it is safe to sail.
- ✅ **Sir Edmund Halley, 1686**, explained the monsoon by **thermal contrast**, in two parts —
  **(a) the summer monsoon, (b) the winter monsoon**. ⚠️ Note the two-part structure: Halley, like
  Khullar's definition at Lesson 1, treats the monsoon as a **double system**. The winter half is not
  an afterthought in the classical theory; it is half the theory.
- ✅ **Khullar's own gloss**, which is the sentence to quote: *"Halley's ideas are basically the same
  as those involved in land and sea breeze except that… **day and night are replaced by summer and
  winter**."*
- ✅ **"Dominated the scene for about three centuries."**

### The causal chain, with its assumptions and its limits

```text
  HALLEY'S MECHANISM, STATED COMPLETELY

  SUMMER                                  WINTER
  Land has LOW specific heat and is       Land cools faster than sea by the
  heated faster by the same insolation    same asymmetry
        |                                       |
        v                                       v
  Continental interior becomes much       Continental interior becomes much
  hotter than the adjacent ocean          colder than the adjacent ocean
        |                                       |
        v                                       v
  Air over land expands, rises, and       Air over land contracts and sinks;
  surface pressure FALLS                  surface pressure RISES
        |                                       |
        v                                       v
  A pressure gradient runs from the       The gradient runs from land to sea
  sea to the land                               |
        |                                       v
        v                                 Dry OFFSHORE flow: the winter
  Moist ONSHORE flow: the summer          monsoon, Halley's part (b)
  monsoon, Halley's part (a)
        |                                       |
        +------------------+--------------------+
                           v
          A DOUBLE SYSTEM OF SEASONAL WINDS - which is exactly
          Khullar folio 122's definition at Lesson 1.  The classical
          theory and the classical definition FIT EACH OTHER, and
          that mutual fit is a large part of why it survived.

  ASSUMPTIONS, each of which a later lesson will break
   A1. The atmosphere responds to SURFACE heating only.
       -> broken at Lesson 7 (jets) and Lesson 8 (elevated heating).
   A2. The air that arrives comes from the ADJACENT ocean.
       -> broken at Lesson 9: it crosses the EQUATOR from the
          southern hemisphere.
   A3. A stronger gradient gives more rain.
       -> broken at Lesson 14: breaks occur while the land is still
          hot, and at Lesson 11: Rajasthan is hottest and driest.
   A4. The response is PROPORTIONATE and therefore GRADUAL.
       -> broken at Lesson 10: the BURST is abrupt.

  THE LIMIT, STATED ONCE: the thermal theory is a theory of the
  PRESSURE FIELD. The monsoon's examinable behaviour - burst, break,
  spatial unevenness, interannual failure - is behaviour of the
  CIRCULATION and of the MOISTURE FIELD, which the pressure field
  alone does not determine.
```

### The India hook, with its limitation printed beside it

⚠️ **Halley's theory still explains the first-order Indian reversal, and it is still the right
opening move in an answer.** Leong's printed figures carry it: **Central Asia in summer is *"more
than 15 °F"* [8.3 °C] hotter than its normal temperature** (p. 122), producing the **Punjab Low** of
his Fig. 125 and the inflow that follows. Nothing in the modern theories removes that gradient; they
add to it.

> ⛔ **Limitation that must be printed with it.** The theory **cannot explain why the monsoon
> fails**, and it cannot explain **why western Rajasthan is dry**. Rajasthan is the hottest surface
> in the system, sits under the deepest thermal low, and receives — Khullar folio 148 — as little as
> **12 cm** a year. A theory whose only variable is surface heat predicts the opposite. The answer,
> at Lesson 11, is the **geometry of the third Arabian stream against the Aravali**, which is a
> statement about orography — failure **(2)** on the folio-123 list.

### Comparison, objection, reply and the unresolved residual

**Comparison — the classical theory against its own strongest supporter.** Köppen, Hann and Angot
(Lesson 1) said the monsoon is *"simply land and sea breezes on a large scale"*. Khullar's gloss on
Halley says the same thing — day and night replaced by summer and winter. These are the same claim
in two registers, one classificatory and one causal, and together they are the **strongest form** of
the classical position: not merely that thermal contrast causes the monsoon, but that **no new
physics is involved at all**. That is what Flohn, at Lesson 6, actually denies. He does not say
Halley was wrong about heat; he says the monsoon is a **modification of the planetary wind system**,
which is a different kind of object from an enlarged sea breeze.

**Objection.** *"Three centuries of dominance is an argument from authority. If the theory was
inadequate in 1686, it was inadequate throughout."*

**Reply.** The inadequacy was not visible in 1686, and the reason is instructive. Each of the three
stated failures requires **data the seventeenth, eighteenth and nineteenth centuries did not have**.
Failure (1), unequal development, requires a **global** comparative wind climatology. Failure (2),
shape and orography, requires systematic **relief and rainfall mapping** of continental interiors.
Failure (3), upper-tropospheric circulation, requires **upper-air observation**, which arrives with
radiosondes and aircraft in the twentieth century — which is precisely why the repairers at folio
123 are all twentieth-century figures and why the observational programmes of Lesson 9 matter. The
theory survived for three centuries because **it was not refutable with the available instruments**,
which is a different and more interesting claim than that people were credulous.

**Unresolved residual.** ✅ Khullar prints, in the same block, that *"the origin of monsoons is
still shrouded in mystery"* and that there are two categories of explanation — **1. Classical
Theory, 2. Modern Theories** — without claiming the second has closed the question. ⚠️ Nothing in
Lessons 6 to 8 closes it either: the jet-stream theory is described at folio 124 as *"the latest
theory… and has earned world wide acceptance"*, which is a statement about acceptance, not about
sufficiency. **This edition does not assert that the monsoon is explained.**

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Treating Halley as an error.** He is the **author of the correct first-order mechanism**, and
  Khullar records that it dominated for **about three centuries**.
- ❌ **Omitting the winter half.** Halley's explanation is explicitly **two-part**: (a) summer
  monsoon, (b) winter monsoon. A one-season Halley is half a Halley.
- ❌ **Confusing *mention* with *study*.** The **Rig Veda**, the **Greeks** and **Buddhist scholars**
  *mention*; the **Arabs** are credited with the first **scientific study**.
- ❌ **Dating Al Masudi precisely.** Khullar prints *"about the **10th century**"*. No closer date is
  certified here.
- ❌ **Attributing onset dates to meteorology.** **Sidi Ali, 1554**, reported commencement dates —
  four centuries before IMD's criteria at Lesson 10.
- ❌ **Listing the three failures as "it is incomplete".** The folio prints **three specific**
  failures: unequal development; the shape of continents and orography; upper-tropospheric
  circulation. Name them.
- ❌ **Putting the repairers in the wrong country.** Folio 123 separates the international roster —
  **Flohn, Thompson, Stephenson, Frost, Yin, Hwang, Takahashi, Palmen, Newton** — from the Indian
  meteorologists — **Koteswaram, Krishnan, Raman, Ramanathan, Krishna Murti, Rama**.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I, *"important Geophysical phenomena"* — the explanation of a major atmospheric
system, taken historically. This lesson exists because **C2 §8 line 138 sets a comparison of
classical and modern theories as a Mains angle and C2's body supplies neither theory** (**C-06**).

**PYQ position.** No verified demand is owned here. ⚠️ The **2023 GS-I Purvaiya** demand that Topic
16 does own (Lesson 20) is a *why and how* question about a wind system, and the classical mechanism
is the "how" that a **10-mark, 150-word** answer actually has room for — the modern theories do not
fit the word count. ⛔ No answer letter is recorded or inferred for any item.

**Answer use.** For any *"explain the mechanism"* stem, open with Halley **named and dated**, then
state the three failures as the transition to the modern theories. The transition sentence is the
mark-bearing one: *"Halley's 1686 thermal explanation — in which, as Khullar puts it, day and night
are replaced by summer and winter — dominated for about three centuries and still accounts for the
first-order reversal; it fails on three counts the folio states explicitly: monsoons do not develop
equally everywhere, development is governed by the shape of continents and by orography, and it is
governed by the conditions of air circulation in the upper troposphere."* Those three clauses are
also the **plan** of your next three paragraphs.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **Sequence:** the monsoons are **mentioned** in the **Rig Veda** and by **Greek and Buddhist**
   scholars; the first **scientific study** is credited to the **Arabs**.
2. ✅ **About the 10th century: Al Masudi of Baghdad** described the **reversal of ocean currents
   and monsoon winds** over the north Indian Ocean — the earliest statement that this is an
   **ocean-atmosphere** system.
3. ✅ **1554 A.D.: Sidi Ali** reported the **commencement dates** of the monsoon — an onset record
   four centuries before IMD's criteria.
4. ✅ **1686: Sir Edmund Halley** explained the monsoon by **thermal contrast**, in two parts —
   **(a) summer monsoon, (b) winter monsoon**.
5. ✅ **Khullar's gloss, worth quoting verbatim:** *"Halley's ideas are basically the same as those
   involved in land and sea breeze except that… **day and night are replaced by summer and
   winter**."*
6. ✅ **"Dominated the scene for about three centuries."**
7. ✅ **The three stated failures, in order:** (1) monsoons *"do not develop equally everywhere"*;
   (2) development is governed by *"the **shape of the continents**, **orography**"*; (3) and by
   *"the **conditions of air circulation in the upper troposphere**"*.
8. ✅ **The repairers named at folio 123:** **Flohn, Thompson, Stephenson, Frost, M.T. Yin, Hwang,
   Takahashi, E. Palmen, C. Newton**; Indian meteorologists **P. Koteswaram, Krishnan, Raman,
   Ramanathan, Krishna Murti, Rama**.
9. ✅ **Khullar's own verdict, retained unresolved:** *"The origin of monsoons is still shrouded in
   mystery"*, with two categories — **1. Classical Theory, 2. Modern Theories**.

### Concept check

**Question:** State the three specific grounds on which the classical thermal theory of the monsoon
is held to be inadequate, and name the later development that repairs each.

**Model answer:** Khullar folio 123 prints three. **First, monsoons *"do not develop equally
everywhere"*** — thermal contrast alone would make every comparable continent-ocean pair equally
monsoonal, and the repair is **Flohn's** reframing of the monsoon as a *modification of the planetary
wind system*, so that the global circulation, not local heating, sets the pattern. **Second,
development is governed by *"the shape of the continents, and orography"*** — a heat-only mechanism
cannot contain a mountain; the repair is **Koteswaram's** treatment of the Tibetan plateau as an
elevated heat source, and the orographic distribution of Lessons 11 and 12. **Third, it is governed
by *"the conditions of air circulation in the upper troposphere"*** — a surface theory cannot see the
jets; the repair is the **jet-stream theory of Yin and Pedelaborde**. *(124 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That the modern theories replace Halley. They **supplement** him: the
thermal gradient he described is still the first-order driver, and Khullar still records that the
origin of monsoons *"is still shrouded in mystery"*. *(33 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 5

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** Trace the development of the classical explanation of
the monsoon and assess why it held the field for so long.

**Model:** The lineage runs from **record** to **observation** to **explanation**. The Rig Veda and
Greek and Buddhist writers **mention** the rains; the **Arabs** are credited with the first
scientific study. About the **10th century, Al Masudi of Baghdad** described the reversal of **ocean
currents and monsoon winds** over the north Indian Ocean; in **1554, Sidi Ali** recorded commencement
dates. In **1686, Sir Edmund Halley** supplied the explanation — thermal contrast, in two parts,
summer and winter — which Khullar glosses as the land-and-sea breeze with *"day and night … replaced
by summer and winter"*. It **"dominated the scene for about three centuries"** because each of its
three recorded failures — unequal development, the shape of continents and orography, and upper-air
circulation — **required instruments that did not yet exist**, upper-air observation above all. It
was not credulity; it was **unfalsifiability by available data**. *(137 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** **Chronological ordering** — mention, observation and explanation kept
distinct and correctly dated **3** · **Causal statement** — Halley's two-part mechanism given, not
merely named **2** · **Failure diagnosis** — all three printed failures reproduced **3** ·
**Historical proportion** — longevity explained by instrumental limits rather than dismissed as
error **2**

Progress: 6 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **meridional ITCZ migration cross-section, with three conflicting displacement figures on one axis**

### The intuition, before any term

Halley's theory has a hole in it that is easy to miss. It says air flows from high pressure over the
ocean to low pressure over the land. Fine — but **which air?** Halley's picture implies the air comes
from the ocean next door. The first modern repair says something much stranger: **the air that rains
on India in July was in the southern hemisphere in June.**

Here is the picture in ordinary language. The belt where the planet's two trade-wind systems collide
is a ribbon of converging, rising, raining air that sits near the equator. It is not fixed. It
follows the sun, lagging a little, migrating north in the northern summer and south in the northern
winter. Over an ocean it moves modestly. **Over a continent that is heating violently, it is dragged
much further** — far enough, in July, to lie across northern India.

When it gets there it stops looking like a tropical convergence belt and starts looking like an
elongated low-pressure trough running across the Ganga plain. It acquires a different name. **But it
is the same object**, and the single most useful sentence in this lesson is that the *monsoon trough*
and the *intertropical convergence zone* are the same thing in two positions.

And once the belt has crossed the equator, the trade winds of the **southern** hemisphere have to
cross it too — and a wind that crosses the equator gets turned the other way by the Earth's
rotation. A south-east trade becomes a south-west monsoon. That is the whole repair.

### The primary visual — map plate M-2

```text

  Sources marked. Not to scale.

   20N |

    and counts the systems that form along it.
```

**What the visual shows.** Three figures for one displacement, stacked on the same axis so the
disagreement is visible rather than hidden; the equator-crossing that Halley's theory does not
contain; and the identity of the ITCZ with the monsoon trough, which is the sentence that makes
Lesson 13 possible.

### The technical content, each term after the idea

- ✅ **The convergence.** Khullar folio 124: the **SE trades** and the **NE trades** meet near the
  equator at the **ITCZ**.
- ✅ **The crossing and the turn.** In summer the ITCZ shifts north, *"the **SE trades cross the
  equator and blow south-west to north-east under the Coriolis force**"* (Fig. 5.2). ⚠️ The
  mechanism of the turn is standard: a wind crossing the equator passes from a hemisphere where it
  is deflected left into one where it is deflected right, so a south-easterly becomes a
  south-westerly. ↪ **The Coriolis effect itself is `Geography/basic/13`'s**; this lesson uses its
  result.
- ✅ **The identity that must be learned as a sentence.** *"**These displaced trades are the
  south-west monsoons.**"* The south-west monsoon is not a wind that India generates. It is the
  **southern hemisphere's trade wind, renamed after it turns.**
- ✅ **The Monsoon Front** is the boundary where the displaced trades meet the NE trades.
- ✅ **The July position, with its figure and its name.** *"In the month of **July the ITCZ shifts to
  20°-25° N latitude** and is located in the **Indo-Gangetic Plain**… The ITCZ in this position is
  often called the **Monsoon Trough**"* (Fig. 5.3).
- ✅ **Flohn's rejection, which is the heart of the lesson.** **H. Flohn** rejected the classical
  theory: the tropical monsoon of Asia is *"simply a **modification of the planetary winds of the
  tropics**"*. The displacement he invokes is *an unusually great northward displacement of the
  **NITCZ**, "drawn to about **30° latitude**… associated with the unusually high temperature over
  north India"*.
- ✅ **What the current actually is, in Flohn's account.** The main westerly current is the
  **expanded equatorial westerlies** embedded in the tropical easterlies — not a locally generated
  inflow.
- ✅ **The seasonal pair:** **NITCZ** in July, **SITCZ** in January.
- ✅ **Majid's parallel account (folio 3.21):** *"The monsoon is the product of many factors working
  together"* — (i) the **ITCZ migrates to between 25 and 30**; (ii) the large interior pressure
  change over the land mass enforces that migration; (iii) *"the **Himalaya mountains and the
  Tibetan Plateau** are a very significant topographic barriers, which **split the circulation over
  Asia**"*. ↪ The split itself is Lesson 7's and Lesson 8's; the three-factor framing is used here.
- ✅ **Majid on the rainfall type:** *"The precipitation is primarily **convectional rainfall
  randomly distributed** within the flowing moist air. Some **travelling cyclones associated with
  the ITCZ** add further rainfall. The peak season of rainfall **moves northward** over the
  subcontinent."*

### Correction C-21, printed rather than resolved

⇄ **Three certified figures exist for the summer displacement of the convergence zone, and they do
not coincide.**

| Source | Printed figure | Exact printed wording |
|---|---|---|
| **Khullar folio 124** | **20°-25° N** | *"In the month of July the ITCZ shifts to 20°-25° N latitude and is located in the Indo-Gangetic Plain"* |
| **Majid IWG folio 3.21** | **25 to 30** | *"the ITCZ migrates to between 25 and 30"* |
| **Flohn, via Khullar folio 124** | **about 30°** | *"drawn to about 30° latitude… associated with the unusually high temperature over north India"* |

⚠️ **Why they differ is itself examinable, and it is a reasoning point rather than a source point.**
The three figures are plausibly **measuring different things**: a mean July axis position, a
seasonal maximum excursion, and a theoretical displacement invoked to explain the monsoon's
strength. **No certified source says which.** This edition prints all three, states that they
overlap without coinciding, and **adopts none**. The Mains sentence is *"the convergence zone is
displaced to roughly 20°-30° N in July, the exact figure varying between sources from 20°-25° in
Khullar to about 30° in Flohn's account"*.

### The causal chain, with its assumptions and its limits

```text
   SOLAR DECLINATION moves north after the March equinox
        |
        v
   The ITCZ follows, with a lag
        |
        v
   Over the ASIAN LANDMASS the interior heats far beyond the ocean
   (Majid: "the large interior pressure change over the land mass
   enforces that migration")
        |
        v
   The convergence zone is dragged FURTHER NORTH THAN IT WOULD GO
   OVER AN OCEAN -> to the Indo-Gangetic Plain, where it is called
   the MONSOON TROUGH
        |
        v
   The SE TRADES of the southern hemisphere must now cross the
   equator to reach it
        |
        v
   Crossing the equator reverses the sense of Coriolis deflection:
   a SE trade becomes a SW flow
        |
        v
   THE SOUTH-WEST MONSOON = THE DISPLACED SOUTH-EAST TRADES
        |
        v
   Where they meet the NE trades: the MONSOON FRONT
        |
        v
   Rain is "primarily convectional... randomly distributed", with
   "travelling cyclones associated with the ITCZ" adding more, and
   the peak "MOVES NORTHWARD" over the subcontinent (Majid 3.21)

  WHAT THIS REPAIRS IN HALLEY
   - supplies the SOURCE of the air (southern hemisphere, not the
     adjacent sea) - breaking assumption A2 of Lesson 5
   - supplies a MECHANISM for the turn (equator crossing + Coriolis)
   - supplies a reason monsoons are UNEQUAL: the displacement depends
     on continental configuration, which answers failure (1)

  WHAT IT STILL DOES NOT EXPLAIN - and the honesty here is the mark
   - WHY THE ONSET IS ABRUPT. A belt that migrates with the sun
     should arrive gradually. It does not: Leong's rains "break",
     Khullar's folio 141 calls it a BURST. -> Lesson 7.
   - WHY THE BELT SOMETIMES RETREATS MID-SEASON while the land is
     still hot. -> Lesson 14.
   - WHAT SUPPLIES THE ENERGY to drag the belt to 30 N. Flohn
     attributes it to "unusually high temperature over north India",
     which is a SURFACE argument - and Koteswaram will answer with
     an ELEVATED one. -> Lesson 8.
```

### The India hook, with its limitation printed beside it

⚠️ **The monsoon trough over the Indo-Gangetic plain *is* the displaced intertropical convergence
zone.** Khullar folio 124 states it outright, and the consequence is practical: every feature of the
Indian rainy season that depends on the trough — its pressure field, the depressions that form along
it, the active and break spells of Lesson 14 — is a property of a **global** circulation feature
sitting temporarily over northern India, not of a local Indian low.

> ⛔ **Limitation that must be printed with it.** **The displacement figure is disputed three ways**
> — 20°-25°, 25-30, about 30 — and no certified source adjudicates. Any answer that states a single
> figure as settled is overclaiming. Further, the identity between ITCZ and monsoon trough is a
> statement about **position and function**, not about identical structure: the trough over the plain
> has an axis, a pressure field and a system-generating behaviour that the oceanic ITCZ does not.
> Lesson 13 measures those, and the trough's **northward displacement to the Himalayan foothills**
> during a break (Lesson 14) is behaviour no oceanic convergence belt exhibits.

### Comparison, objection, reply and the unresolved residual

**Comparison — Flohn against Halley, stated as the disagreement it is.** Halley says the monsoon is
**generated** by a thermal contrast: the continent makes its own wind. Flohn says the monsoon is a
**modification of the planetary winds of the tropics**: the wind already exists, globally, and the
continent displaces it. The difference is not emphasis. On Halley's account the monsoon is a local
object; on Flohn's it is the **tropical general circulation, locally deformed** — which is why
Flohn's account can explain unequal development across continents and Halley's cannot. ⚠️ Note also
what Flohn **keeps**: the *"unusually high temperature over north India"* is still doing the work.
He has not removed heat from the explanation; he has changed **what the heat acts upon**.

**Objection.** *"If the south-west monsoon is just the south-east trades turned around, why is it so
much stronger and wetter than the trades it came from?"*

**Reply.** Three printed elements answer this without leaving the certified corpus. First, the flow
is not merely turned but **concentrated**: Flohn's *expanded equatorial westerlies* are a current
embedded within the tropical easterlies, not a diffuse drift. Second, the air has crossed a long
oceanic fetch — Lesson 9's cross-equatorial flow is measured over the Indian Ocean, and Lesson 11
shows the Arabian branch arriving with enough moisture to deposit **over 250 cm** on the Western
Ghats. Third, the convergence into which it flows has been dragged to 20°-30° N and now sits over a
heated continent, so the uplift at the receiving end is far stronger than at an oceanic ITCZ. The
strength is a property of **the whole displaced system**, not of the trade wind alone.

**Unresolved residual.** **The displacement figure (C-21) is unresolved**, and so is a second,
quieter question: Flohn's displacement is attributed to surface temperature over north India, while
Koteswaram's thermal engine (Lesson 8) attributes the driving to **elevated** heating over Tibet.
Khullar prints both at folios 124-126 and records at folio 126 only that *"Thomson (1951), Flohn
(1960) and Stephenson (1965) express similar views, but **Flohn's concept is widely accepted**"* —
a statement about acceptance that leaves the physical question open. ⚠️ **This edition does not
decide between surface and elevated heating.**

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Giving one ITCZ displacement figure as settled.** **C-21**: Khullar 20°-25°, Majid 25-30,
  Flohn about 30.
- ❌ **Treating the ITCZ and the monsoon trough as two features.** Khullar folio 124: the ITCZ *"in
  this position is often called the Monsoon Trough"*. **One object, two positions, two names.**
- ❌ **Saying the south-west monsoon is air drawn from the Arabian Sea.** Its air is the **southern
  hemisphere's south-east trades**, deflected after crossing the equator.
- ❌ **Attributing the turn to anything but the equator crossing.** The deflection sense reverses
  because the hemisphere changes; the **Coriolis effect itself is `Geography/basic/13`'s** and is not
  re-derived here.
- ❌ **Reporting Flohn as agreeing with Halley.** He **rejected** the classical theory and called the
  Asian monsoon *"simply a modification of the planetary winds of the tropics"*.
- ❌ **Writing "ITCZ" and never naming the hemispheric pair.** Khullar prints **NITCZ** (July) and
  **SITCZ** (January).
- ❌ **Calling monsoon rainfall purely orographic.** Majid folio 3.21 prints *"primarily
  **convectional** rainfall randomly distributed"* with **travelling cyclones** adding more — and
  Lesson 13 shows the depressions doing most of the work inland.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I, *"important Geophysical phenomena"* — the dynamic mechanism of the monsoon,
which C1 §10.2 states at level 2 without evidence and which this lesson supplies.

**PYQ position.** No verified demand is owned here. One **adjacent** item is named and refused:
**2020 Prelims Q99**, on jet streams and cyclone-eye temperature, is routed to
`Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements` under refusal **R-06** — this edition **uses** jets at Lesson
7 and does not re-derive them. ⛔ No answer letter is recorded or inferred.

**Answer use.** For a *"mechanism"* stem, this lesson supplies the **second paragraph** and the
sentence that earns the mark is the identity: *"The intertropical convergence zone, displaced
northward over a heated continent to roughly 20°-30° N — Khullar gives 20°-25° for July, Majid
25-30, Flohn about 30 — lies across the Indo-Gangetic plain, where it is called the monsoon trough;
the south-east trades, obliged to cross the equator to reach it, are turned by the Coriolis effect
and arrive as the south-west monsoon, so that the Indian rainy season is supplied by the southern
hemisphere's trade wind under a new name."* That is one sentence, three sources, a disclosed conflict
and the whole dynamic repair.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **Convergence:** the **SE trades** and the **NE trades** meet near the equator at the **ITCZ**
   (Khullar folio 124).
2. ✅ **The crossing:** in summer *"the SE trades **cross the equator** and blow **south-west to
   north-east** under the **Coriolis force**"* (Fig. 5.2).
3. ✅ **The identity to memorise:** *"**These displaced trades are the south-west monsoons.**"*
4. ✅ **The Monsoon Front** = where the displaced trades meet the NE trades.
5. ✅ **July position:** *"the ITCZ shifts to **20°-25° N**… located in the **Indo-Gangetic
   Plain**… often called the **Monsoon Trough**"* (Fig. 5.3).
6. ⇄ **C-21, three printed figures:** Khullar **20°-25°** · Majid **25-30** · Flohn **about 30°**.
   Overlapping, not coinciding, **none adopted**.
7. ✅ **Flohn's rejection:** the Asian tropical monsoon is *"simply a **modification of the planetary
   winds of the tropics**"* — not a locally generated wind.
8. ✅ **Flohn's current:** the main westerly current is the **expanded equatorial westerlies**
   embedded in the tropical easterlies.
9. ✅ **Seasonal pair:** **NITCZ** (July), **SITCZ** (January).
10. ✅ **Majid's three factors (folio 3.21):** the ITCZ migration; the **interior pressure change**
    that enforces it; the **Himalaya and Tibetan Plateau** as topographic barriers that **split the
    circulation over Asia**.
11. ✅ **Majid on rainfall type:** *"primarily **convectional** rainfall **randomly distributed**"*,
    plus *"**travelling cyclones associated with the ITCZ**"*, with the peak *"**moving
    northward**"* over the subcontinent.
12. ⚠️ **What this repair does and does not do:** it supplies the **source** of the air and a reason
    for **unequal development**; it does **not** explain the **abrupt burst** (Lesson 7), the
    **mid-season break** (Lesson 14), or **what energy** drags the belt to 30° N (Lesson 8).

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the south-west monsoon blows from the south-west, and state precisely what
the monsoon trough is.

**Model answer:** It blows from the south-west because **it is not Indian air**. The convergence
zone migrates north in the northern summer, and the **south-east trades of the southern hemisphere**
must cross the equator to reach it. Crossing the equator reverses the sense of Coriolis deflection,
so a south-easterly is turned into a south-westerly: Khullar folio 124 states that the SE trades
*"cross the equator and blow south-west to north-east under the Coriolis force"*, and that **"these
displaced trades are the south-west monsoons"**. The **monsoon trough is the ITCZ itself**, in its
displaced July position — *"the ITCZ shifts to 20°-25° N latitude and is located in the
Indo-Gangetic Plain… often called the Monsoon Trough"*. It is one object with two names, and the
displacement figure is printed three different ways across the sources. *(131 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That the south-west monsoon is air sucked off the Arabian Sea by the
Indian thermal low. The air is the southern hemisphere's trade wind, renamed after it turns; the
Indian low determines **where it converges**, not **where it comes from**. *(40 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 6

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** Examine the proposition that the Indian south-west
monsoon is a modification of the planetary wind system rather than a locally generated circulation.

**Model:** The proposition is **Flohn's**, and its evidence is the wind's origin. Flohn rejected
the classical theory, holding the Asian tropical monsoon to be *"simply a **modification of the
planetary winds of the tropics**"*, with the northern intertropical convergence zone *"drawn to about
**30° latitude**"* by the unusually high temperature over north India, and the main current composed
of **expanded equatorial westerlies** embedded in the tropical easterlies. Decisively, the air is
**not Indian**: the **south-east trades cross the equator**, are turned by the
Coriolis effect and arrive as the south-west monsoon — *"these displaced trades are the south-west
monsoons"*. The displaced convergence zone, lying over the Indo-Gangetic plain, is the **monsoon
trough**. Its printed July latitude varies — **20°-25°** in Khullar, **25-30** in Majid, **about 30°**
in Flohn — and **none of the three is corrected by another.** Thermal contrast remains, but it
**deforms** a global system rather than creating one. *(147 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** **Mechanism articulation** — equator crossing, Coriolis reversal and the
identity of the displaced trades with the monsoon stated as a chain **3** · **Displacement
reasoning** — the ITCZ-to-monsoon-trough identity made explicit **3** · **Numerical conflict
handling** — all three displacement figures printed and none adopted **2** · **Diagram fidelity** —
Figs. 5.2 and 5.3 used accurately, NITCZ and SITCZ named **2**

Progress: 7 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **seasonal jet-position overlay map, winter against summer**

### The intuition, before any term

Lesson 6 left one question conspicuously open. If the monsoon is the arrival of a migrating
convergence belt, it should **creep in**. Belts migrate; they do not pounce. Yet every certified
source describes an abrupt event: Leong's rains *"break"* by mid-June, Khullar folio 141 names the
arrival a ***burst***, with thunder, lightning and heavy downpour.

An abrupt change in a slowly forced system almost always means the same thing: **something was
holding it back, and the obstacle moved.** The obstacle, on this account, is not at the surface at
all. It is a ribbon of very fast west-to-east wind, eight to twelve kilometres up, which in winter
runs along the southern side of the Himalaya and in summer jumps to the far side of the Tibetan
plateau.

While that ribbon sits south of the mountains, the circulation beneath it is pinned. When it jumps —
and it jumps *"rather dramatically"*, in Khullar's phrase — the pin is removed and the monsoon
arrives all at once.

This lesson teaches that repair and then immediately disciplines it, because the single most common
error on this material is to promote an **indicator** into a **cause**.

### The primary visual — map plate M-3

```text

  Pedelaborde"; Majid IWG folio 3.35 (scan PDF 108) for parameters.

  ALTITUDE
```

### Correction C-22, printed rather than resolved

⇄ **Two certified sources print two different extreme jet speeds.**

| Source | Printed extreme | Printed typical range |
|---|---|---|
| **Khullar folio 124** | *"extreme **400 km/h**"* | westerly jet speeds *"commonly **150-300 km/h**"* |
| **Majid IWG folio 3.35** | *"in exceptional cases a speed of **375 kmph**"* | core *"can exceed **300 kmph**"*; means **110 km/h** summer to **190 kmph** winter |

⚠️ The two are **not reconcilable by rounding** — 375 and 400 differ by 25 km/h — and neither book
states a location, a level or a record date for its extreme. **Both are printed; neither is
adopted.** The safe formulation names the band and attributes the extremes: *"typical westerly jet
speeds of 150-300 km/h, with printed extremes of 375 kmph in Majid and 400 km/h in Khullar"*.

### The technical content, with attribution restored

- ✅ **Jet Stream Theory, Khullar folio 124.** The jet stream lies at about **12 km**. Westerly jet
  speeds are *"commonly **150-300 km/h**"*, extreme **400 km/h**. Khullar's own assessment: it is
  *"**the latest theory regarding the origin of the monsoons and has earned world wide
  acceptance**"*.
- ✅ **M.T. Yin (1949).** The burst depends on **upper-air circulation**, and specifically: *"the low
  latitude upper air trough shifts from **90°E to 80°E** longitude in response to the northward shift
  of the western jet stream in summer."* ⚠️ Note that this is a **longitudinal** statement. The
  familiar account of the jet-stream repair is entirely about latitude; Yin's actual printed claim
  adds a westward shift of a trough by ten degrees of longitude, and it is the trough — not the jet —
  that does the proximate work on the burst.
- ✅ **Pierre Pedelaborde (1963)**, in his book ***The Monsoon***, recognised Yin's ideas. Khullar's
  **Fig. 5.4** is captioned *Jet Stream (after Pedelaborde)*. His printed account: in **winter** the
  western jet flows along the **southern slopes of the Himalayas**; in **summer** it shifts
  northwards *"rather dramatically"* to the **northern edge of the Tibet Plateau**; and *"the
  periodic movements of the Jet stream are often **indicators** of the **onset and subsequent
  withdrawal** of the monsoon"*.
- ✅ **The sentence from folio 126 that every answer should carry:** *"The periodic movements of the
  sub-tropical jet stream provide a useful indication of the onset and subsequent withdrawal of the
  monsoon. In fact, **northward movement of the subtropical jet is the first indication of the onset
  of the monsoon over India**."*

> ⚠️ **Indicator against cause — the discipline of this lesson, stated once and applied everywhere.**
> Both Pedelaborde and Khullar use the words *indicator* and *indication*. Neither says the jet
> **causes** the onset. The distinction is not pedantic. An indicator is something you **watch** to
> know that a change is occurring; a cause is something whose removal **prevents** the change.
> ✅ Khullar's folio 126 does make one statement with causal form — about Tibetan snow and the
> easterly jet — and that statement belongs to **Lesson 8**, which is where Topic 16's ownership of
> Tibet **as a monsoon cause** is discharged.

### The causal chain, with the boundary marked inside it

```text
   WINTER
   The upper westerlies approach Asia and meet the Himalaya-Tibet mass
        |
        v
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
   | THE SPLIT ITSELF IS TOPIC 13's PROPERTY (refusal R-06).       |
   | This lesson USES the result: in winter the jet runs along the |
   | SOUTHERN SLOPES of the Himalaya (Pedelaborde, folio 125).     |
   | Khullar folio 126's winter numbers - a southern branch        |
   | averaging about 240 km/h at 200 mb against 70-90 km/h for the |
   | northern branch - are taught at LESSON 8, with the plateau.   |
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
        |
        v
   While the southern branch is in place, subsidence beneath it
   maintains dry outblowing flow over north-west India
        |
        v
   SPRING: the land heats; the meridional temperature gradient that
   sustains the jet weakens  (Majid 3.21: "as the temperature
   differential over the continent diminishes, THE JET STREAM BREAKS
   DOWN. The subtropical high and ITCZ shifts rapidly northward and
   bring the SUDDEN SHIFT OF WINDS ONSHORE.")
        |

        |

        |      trough shifts from 90 E to 80 E
        |

   The restraint on the low-level circulation is removed; the
   convergence zone of Lesson 6 can occupy northern India

   indicates WITHDRAWAL (Pedelaborde). -> Lesson 15.

  ASSUMPTION: that an upper-air feature can hold down a surface
  circulation. This is standard dynamics and is Topic 13's to justify.
  LIMIT 1: the sources describe INDICATION, not CAUSATION, and this
  edition preserves their wording.
  LIMIT 2: a jet shift explains the TIMING of the burst. It does not
  explain the DEPTH of the moisture supply, the BRANCH structure, or
  the BREAK. Lessons 9 to 14 supply those.
  LIMIT 3: Majid's "the jet stream breaks down" and Pedelaborde's
  "shifts northwards" are NOT the same claim. Breaking down and
  moving are different fates. NO CERTIFIED SOURCE RECONCILES THEM.
```

### The India hook, with its limitation printed beside it

⚠️ **The northward jump of the subtropical westerly jet is the first indication of monsoon onset
over India** — a printed sentence at Khullar folio 126, and the most useful single fact in the
mechanism half of this topic, because it converts the onset from an event you observe to a
**transition you can anticipate**.

> ⛔ **Limitation that must be printed with it.** It is an **indicator**, not a cause, and the
> sources say so in those words. And the **jets themselves are `Geography/basic/13`'s and
> `Geography/advanced/13`'s**: their formation, the thermal-wind relation, the bifurcation by the
> Tibetan massif, Rossby waves, blocking and western disturbances are all taught there. This lesson
> may **use** the jet's seasonal position; under refusal **R-06** it may not re-derive the jet. The
> **one** jet-related claim Topic 16 does own — Tibet as a **monsoon cause** — is held back to
> Lesson 8 deliberately.

### Comparison, objection, reply and the unresolved residual

**Comparison — Yin against Pedelaborde, who are usually quoted as one.** They are not one. **Yin
(1949)** makes a **zonal** claim: the low-latitude upper-air trough shifts **from 90°E to 80°E**,
and it is that shift which is tied to the burst. **Pedelaborde (1963)** makes a **meridional** claim:
the jet moves from the southern slopes of the Himalaya to the northern edge of Tibet. Khullar prints
Pedelaborde as **recognising** Yin's ideas, not as restating them. ⚠️ The combined picture — a jet
that moves north while a trough moves west — is richer than either, and quoting Yin's longitudes is
the cheapest way to show that the jet material has been read rather than absorbed.

**Objection.** *"If the jet merely indicates the onset, the jet-stream theory is not a theory of the
monsoon at all. Khullar calls it 'the latest theory regarding the origin of the monsoons' — which is
it?"*

**Reply.** This is the sharpest available objection and it deserves a precise answer. The jet-stream
theory is a theory of the **timing and abruptness** of the monsoon, not of its **existence**. Nothing
in Yin or Pedelaborde explains why a monsoon circulation exists over southern Asia; that remains
Flohn's displaced planetary-wind account at Lesson 6, with Halley's gradient underneath it. What the
jet material explains is the thing neither of those can explain — **why the arrival is a burst**.
Khullar's "origin" is doing loose work; his own text, two folios later, is careful to say
*indication*. ⚠️ **The honest formulation is that the jet-stream theory completes the classical and
air-mass accounts on the one variable they could not handle, and does not replace them.**

**Unresolved residual.** ⇄ **The two books say different things about what happens to the jet.**
Majid folio 3.21 says *"the jet stream **breaks down**"*; Khullar, via Pedelaborde, says it **shifts
northwards**. A current that breaks down and a current that relocates are different objects with
different consequences, and **no certified source reconciles the two descriptions**. ⇄ And **C-22**
leaves the extreme speed printed two ways, 375 and 400 km/h. Both residuals stand.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Writing that the jet *causes* the onset.** The printed words are *indicator* and
  *indication*. **Preserve them.**
- ❌ **Quoting Pedelaborde's latitude shift without Yin's longitude shift.** Yin's printed claim is
  **90°E to 80°E**, and it is a different axis.
- ❌ **Merging Yin and Pedelaborde.** **Yin 1949**; **Pedelaborde 1963**, who *recognised* Yin in his
  book *The Monsoon*; Fig. 5.4 is captioned *after Pedelaborde*.
- ❌ **Giving one extreme jet speed.** **C-22**: Khullar **400 km/h**, Majid **375 kmph**.
- ❌ **Confusing "breaks down" with "shifts north".** Both are printed, in different books, and they
  are not the same claim.
- ❌ **Re-deriving the jet's bifurcation by the Himalaya.** Refusal **R-06**: that derivation is
  `Geography/advanced/13`'s. Topic 16 uses the **result**.
- ❌ **Importing western disturbances into a monsoon answer.** They are **extra-tropical, frontal,
  winter** systems in the westerly flow, owned by `Geography/advanced/13`, and the 2026 season's own
  western-disturbance record is routed, not taught here.
- ❌ **Using "subtropical jet" and "tropical easterly jet" interchangeably.** The westerly
  subtropical jet is this lesson's; the **easterly** jet is **Lesson 8's**, and its height is printed
  two different ways there.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I, *"important Geophysical phenomena"* — the upper-air component of the monsoon
mechanism, which C1 §10.2 states at level 3 and which both owners leave unattributed.

**PYQ position.** No verified demand is owned here, and **two adjacent demands are refused by name**:
**2020 Prelims Q99** (jet streams; cyclone-eye temperature) and **2024 GS-I Q4** (sea-surface
temperature rise and tropical-cyclone formation), both routed to `Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements`
under **R-06**. ⛔ No answer letter is recorded or inferred. This lesson supplies the **burst**
mechanism that a 20-mark monsoon-mechanism answer requires and a 10-mark answer does not.

**Answer use.** Use the jet material **only to explain abruptness**, and signal the boundary as you
use it. *"The suddenness of the onset is an upper-air phenomenon: the subtropical westerly jet,
which in winter runs along the southern slopes of the Himalaya, shifts in summer — 'rather
dramatically', in Pedelaborde's account — to the northern edge of the Tibetan plateau, while Yin's
low-latitude upper-air trough moves from 90°E to 80°E. Khullar is careful to call the northward
movement the first **indication** of onset rather than its cause."* Four clauses, two named
authorities, two dates, and the indicator discipline visible on the page.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **Jet Stream Theory (Khullar folio 124):** jet at about **12 km**; westerly speeds *"commonly
   **150-300 km/h**"*; **extreme 400 km/h**; described as *"the latest theory… world wide
   acceptance"*.
2. ✅ **M.T. Yin (1949):** the burst depends on **upper-air circulation**; *"the low latitude upper
   air trough shifts from **90°E to 80°E** longitude in response to the northward shift of the
   western jet stream in summer"*.
3. ✅ **Pierre Pedelaborde (1963)**, book ***The Monsoon***; Khullar's **Fig. 5.4** is captioned
   *Jet Stream (after Pedelaborde)*.
4. ✅ **Pedelaborde's seasonal pair:** **winter** — along the **southern slopes of the Himalayas**;
   **summer** — northwards *"rather dramatically"* to the **northern edge of the Tibet Plateau**.
5. ✅ **The indicator sentence:** jet movements are *"indicators of the **onset and subsequent
   withdrawal**"*; and *"**northward movement of the subtropical jet is the first indication of the
   onset of the monsoon over India**"* (folio 126).
6. ✅ **Majid IWG folio 3.35, jet definition:** *"strong wind blowing from **west to east** in the
   upper part of the troposphere, usually near the level of tropopause (**about 12,000 m**)"*.
7. ✅ **Majid's jet dimensions:** **160-480 km wide** by **900-2,150 m thick**; core *"can exceed
   **300 kmph**"*; means **110 km/h in summer** to about **190 kmph in winter**.
8. ⇄ **C-22, two printed extremes:** Majid *"in exceptional cases **375 kmph**"* · Khullar *"extreme
   **400 km/h**"*. **Neither adopted.**
9. ✅ **Majid's three jet types:** **sub-tropical**, **polar front**, **Arctic/Antarctic**; and the
   monsoon relevance — *"the **behaviour of monsoon**, the intensity of precipitation… largely
   influenced by the position, thickness, dimensions and movements of the jet streams"*.
10. ⇄ **Unresolved:** Majid prints *"the jet stream **breaks down**"*; Pedelaborde via Khullar prints
    that it **shifts northwards**. **Different claims, not reconciled.**
11. ⚠️ **Boundary and discipline:** jets **as such** are `Geography/basic/13`'s and
    `Geography/advanced/13`'s (**R-06**). Topic 16 uses the jet's seasonal **position** as an
    **indicator**, and owns only Tibet **as a monsoon cause**, at Lesson 8.

### Concept check

**Question:** A script states: "The monsoon bursts because the subtropical westerly jet causes the
onset by moving north." Correct the sentence, and supply the one element of the jet-stream account
that most candidates omit.

**Model answer:** Two corrections. **First, replace "causes" with "indicates".** Khullar folio 126
prints that *"northward movement of the subtropical jet is the **first indication** of the onset"*,
and Pedelaborde's printed wording is *"indicators of the onset and subsequent withdrawal"*. Neither
source asserts causation, and the distinction is real: an indicator is watched, a cause is removed.
**Second, name the authorities and dates** — **Yin (1949)** and **Pedelaborde (1963)**, whose
*The Monsoon* recognised Yin — rather than attributing the account to no one. The omitted element is
**Yin's zonal component**: the low-latitude upper-air trough shifts *"from **90°E to 80°E**
longitude"* in response to the jet's northward shift. The standard account gives only the
north-south movement; the printed claim has a west-east movement in it as well. *(121 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That the jet-stream theory explains the monsoon. It explains the
monsoon's **timing and abruptness**. The existence of the circulation still rests on the displaced
planetary winds of Lesson 6 and the thermal gradient of Lesson 5. *(36 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 7

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** "Upper-air circulation explains the suddenness of the
Indian monsoon's arrival, not its existence." Examine.

**Model:** The distinction holds, and the sources support it in their own wording. A migrating
convergence belt should arrive gradually; the Indian onset is a **burst**. The upper-air account
supplies the missing switch. **Pedelaborde (1963)**, in *The Monsoon*, describes the western jet
flowing along *"the southern slopes of the Himalayas"* in winter and shifting *"rather dramatically"*
to the **northern edge of the Tibet Plateau** in summer; **Yin (1949)** adds that the low-latitude
upper-air trough shifts *"from **90°E to 80°E** longitude"* in response. Khullar reports jet speeds of
**150-300 km/h**, extreme **400** — Majid prints **375 kmph** — at about **12 km**. But the sources
call these movements *"**indicators** of the onset and subsequent withdrawal"*, and **nothing in
them explains why a monsoon circulation exists over southern Asia**; that remains Flohn's displaced
planetary winds over Halley's gradient. The jet explains **when**, not **whether**. *(139 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** **Upper-air reasoning** — the restraint-and-release logic stated as a
mechanism, with both the meridional and Yin's zonal component **3** · **Indicator-versus-cause
discipline** — the printed wording preserved and the limit stated **3** · **Attribution accuracy** —
Yin 1949 and Pedelaborde 1963 correctly separated and dated **2** · **Boundary compliance** — jets
used, not re-derived, with the owner acknowledged **2**

Progress: 8 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **labelled thermal-engine schematic with a vertical pressure-level scale**

### The intuition, before any term

Everyone knows the Himalaya is a wall. The standard sentence is that it blocks the monsoon from
escaping northward and blocks the cold Central Asian air from coming south. That sentence is true,
it is useful, and **it is not this lesson**, because it belongs to the topic that owns the mountain
as a barrier.

Topic 16 owns a stranger and more powerful idea. Behind the wall sits a plateau the size of a
sub-continent, four kilometres above sea level, which in summer is **heated directly by the sun at
an altitude where the air is thin**. It therefore becomes a mass of warm air sitting *in the middle
of the troposphere* — not warm ground with cool air above it, but a **heat source placed high up in
the atmosphere**.

Think of a radiator. A radiator on the floor warms a room from below and the warm air has to climb.
A radiator mounted near the ceiling does something different: it pumps warm air outward at the
ceiling, which then cools, sinks somewhere else, and returns along the floor. **Tibet is the
ceiling-mounted radiator of the Asian summer.** Air rises over the plateau, spreads outward at high
level, sinks over the equatorial Indian Ocean, and returns at low level towards India as a moist
westerly current.

That is Koteswaram's thermal engine, and it is the one piece of Himalayan physics that belongs to
the monsoon topic rather than to the jet topic.

### The primary visual — the engine, drawn with its pressure levels
