### Correction C-17, printed rather than resolved

⇄ **Khullar prints the tropical easterly jet at two heights and two pressure levels, one folio
apart.**

| Folio | Printed statement | Height | Level |
|---|---|---|---|
| **125** | *"core of the easterly jet at **13 km (150 mb)**"*, against the **westerly jet at 9 km** | 13 km | 150 mb |
| **126** | Fig. 5.6 caption: *"Axis of the Easterly and Westerly Jet stream at **12 km** (after Koteswaram)"*, with the easterly band described at **100-200 mb** | 12 km | 200 mb |

⚠️ A possible reading is that *core* and *axis* are different constructs, or that one figure is the
jet's maximum and the other a representative level for both jets on one diagram. **No certified
source says so.** This edition prints both, notes the plausible reading as an inference, and
**adopts neither**. The safe Mains formulation: *"the tropical easterly jet occupies roughly the
100-200 mb layer, Khullar printing its core at 13 km (150 mb) at folio 125 and the axis of both jets
at 12 km at folio 126"*.

### The technical content, in the order the engine runs

1. ✅ **The heat source.** Khullar folio 125, after **P. Koteswaram (1952)**: Tibet is an
   **ellipsoidal plateau** at about **4,000 m** above sea level, area about **4.5 million sq km**,
   surrounded by ranges rising **6,000-8,000 m**. Heated in summer, it becomes *"**2 °C to 3 °C
   warmer** than the air over the adjoining regions"*.
2. ✅ **The ascent and the outflow.** *"As a heat source it generates rising air which spreads
   outward and sinks over the equatorial Indian Ocean."* Deflected to the right, the outflowing air
   *"moves **anticyclonically in the upper troposphere over Tibet around 300-200 mb (9-12 km)**"*.
3. ✅ **The return.** It *"returns to the **west coast of India from the south-west as the
   equatorial westerlies** (Fig. 5.5), picking up moisture and causing **copious rainfall**"*.
4. ✅ **The easterly jet.** Strong upper easterlies with a pronounced jet at **100-200 mb**, speeds
   *"often exceeding **100 knots**"*, **first inferred by P. Koteswaram and P.R. Krishna in 1952**.
   Folio 126: the jet runs from the **southern tip of the peninsula to about 20° N**, with speeds
   exceeding **100 knots**.
5. ✅ **The snow statement — the one causal claim this topic owns about Tibet.** Folio 126: *"The
   easterly jet **does not come into existence if the snow over the Tibet Plateau does not melt**…
   any year of thick and widespread snow over Tibet will be followed by a year of **weak monsoon and
   less rainfall**."*
6. ✅ **The attribution of the wider view.** *"**Thomson (1951), Flohn (1960) and Stephenson
   (1965)** express similar views, but **Flohn's concept is widely accepted**."*
7. ✅ **The winter counterpart, with its numbers.** Folio 126: in winter the upper westerlies are
   **split into two distinct currents by the topographical obstacle of the Tibet Plateau**, one
   north and one south, reuniting **off the east coast of China**; the **southern branch is stronger,
   averaging about 240 km p.h. at 200 mb against 70 to 90 km p.h.** for the northern branch; air
   subsiding beneath it gives the dry outblowing northerly winds from the subtropical anticyclone
   over north-western India and Pakistan. ↪ **The split itself is `Geography/advanced/13`'s**; the
   numbers are printed here because Topic 16 needs the **subsidence** consequence to explain the dry
   season and the pre-monsoon low.

> ⚠️ **Why "2 °C to 3 °C" is a bigger number than it looks.** It is **not** a comparison between
> Tibet's surface and the Indian plains. It is a comparison between the air **over** Tibet and the
> air at **the same altitude** over the surrounding regions. A persistent 2-3 °C excess maintained
> over **4.5 million square kilometres** in the middle troposphere is an enormous standing thermal
> anomaly, and that is what drives an outflow strong enough to close a hemispheric-scale circuit.

### Route-in I-6, discharged explicitly

> ✅ **Topic 13's released edition routes "the Tibetan plateau treated as a monsoon cause" into
> Topic 16, retaining the plateau only as a jet-splitting barrier.** This lesson discharges that
> obligation in full. The division is clean and should be reproduced in any answer that needs both:
>
> | Role of the plateau | Owner | What it explains |
> |---|---|---|
> | **Mechanical barrier**, splitting the winter upper westerlies into two branches | `Geography/advanced/13` | the existence and geometry of the two jet branches; the track of western disturbances |
> | **Elevated heat source**, 2-3 °C warmer at level, driving an anticyclonic outflow at 300-200 mb and a return equatorial westerly current | **Topic 16, this lesson** | why the monsoon current reaches the west coast of India moist and strong; the existence of the tropical easterly jet; the snow-monsoon relation |
>
> ⚠️ C2 §1 line 17 collapses both roles into one table cell — *"Mechanical barrier and elevated
> heating"* — and names neither the author nor the mechanism. **A Mains answer should separate them
> in two clauses**, because they operate in different seasons, at different levels, and through
> different physics.

### The causal chain, with its assumptions and its limits

```text
   SUMMER insolation falls on a 4.5 million sq km plateau at 4,000 m
        |
        v
   Thin air, low heat capacity, high insolation -> the air column over
   Tibet becomes 2-3 C warmer THAN AIR AT THE SAME LEVEL nearby
        |
        v
   A warm anomaly at mid-troposphere = a HIGH-LEVEL HIGH (the
   "Tibetan High"). Air rises beneath it and diverges above it
        |
        v
   Outflow at 300-200 mb (9-12 km), deflected right -> ANTICYCLONIC
   circulation in the upper troposphere over Tibet
        |
        +--------------------------+
        |                          |
        v                          v
   Southward branch of the    An intense upper EASTERLY flow is
   outflow SINKS over the     established on the southern flank:
   equatorial Indian Ocean    the TROPICAL EASTERLY JET, 100-200 mb,
        |                     >100 knots, peninsular tip to ~20 N
        v
   Subsidence there completes the circuit by forcing a LOW-LEVEL
   RETURN FLOW northward
        |
        v
   The return crosses warm ocean, PICKS UP MOISTURE, and arrives at
   the WEST COAST OF INDIA FROM THE SOUTH-WEST as the EQUATORIAL
   WESTERLIES -> "copious rainfall"
        |
        v
   AND THE SWITCH: "the easterly jet DOES NOT COME INTO EXISTENCE if
   the snow over the Tibet Plateau DOES NOT MELT" -> thick, widespread
   Tibetan snow in a given year -> weak monsoon, less rainfall

  ASSUMPTIONS
   A1. Elevated heating can drive a circulation comparable to surface
       heating. (This is the whole Koteswaram claim.)
   A2. The snow-melt condition operates through ALBEDO and the energy
       budget - a mechanism Khullar states as a RESULT, not a
       derivation.
  LIMITS
   L1. The 2-3 C figure is KOTESWARAM'S 1952 ESTIMATE. Khullar prints
       no measurement period, no station network and no error bound.
   L2. The easterly jet's height is printed TWO WAYS one folio apart
       (C-17).
   L3. Khullar's own text says FLOHN's concept - the displaced
       planetary winds of Lesson 6 - "is widely accepted", not
       Koteswaram's. The two accounts sit side by side in the chapter
       and ARE NOT RECONCILED THERE.
   L4. A snow-monsoon relation stated for "any year" is a
       CLIMATOLOGICAL TENDENCY. This edition states the relation as
       printed and MAKES NO TREND CLAIM about it (refusal R-03).
```

### The India hook, with its limitation printed beside it

⚠️ **Tibet is 2-3 °C warmer than the air over the adjoining regions and drives the easterly jet that
sits over peninsular India through the rainy season.** This is the lesson's payoff for an Indian
answer: the rain that falls on the Western Ghats arrives in a current whose **upper-level return
path runs over a plateau outside India's borders**, and the jet that caps the system reaches from
the southern tip of the peninsula to about 20° N.

> ⛔ **Limitation that must be printed with it.** The **2-3 °C** figure is **Koteswaram's 1952
> estimate**, printed without a measurement period or an error bound; and the **jet's height is given
> two ways one folio apart** (**C-17**). Further, Khullar's own verdict two paragraphs later is that
> **Flohn's** concept — a displaced planetary wind system, not a thermal engine — *"is widely
> accepted"*. The thermal engine is a **named, dated, quotable account** that the chapter prints in
> full and then does not endorse above its rival. Write it as Koteswaram's account, not as the
> settled mechanism.

### Comparison, objection, reply and the unresolved residual

**Comparison — Flohn's heat and Koteswaram's heat are in different places.** Flohn (Lesson 6)
attributes the extreme northward displacement of the convergence zone to *"the unusually high
temperature over **north India**"* — a **surface** anomaly over the plains. Koteswaram attributes the
driving to an **elevated** anomaly over **Tibet**, at 4,000 m, 2-3 °C warmer at level. These are
different heat sources, in different places, at different altitudes, producing different circuits:
Flohn's deforms a planetary wind belt; Koteswaram's closes a meridional cell with subsidence over
the equatorial ocean. Khullar prints both within two folios and reconciles neither. ⚠️ **An answer
that treats "thermal low over north-west India" and "Tibetan heat engine" as the same point has
merged two rival accounts.**

**Objection.** *"The snow-melt statement is deterministic — 'any year of thick and widespread snow
over Tibet will be followed by a year of weak monsoon'. That is far too strong, and quoting it
rewards a false certainty."*

**Reply.** The objection is right about the wording and wrong about the remedy. The wording is
indeed stronger than the evidence a 1952-era account could support, and this edition therefore
quotes it **as a printed claim with its author's vintage attached** rather than asserting it. What
makes it worth quoting is that the **class of predictor is still in operational use**: IMD's own
first-stage 2026 forecast rationale prints the **January-March northern-hemisphere snow-cover
extent** and its *"general inverse relationship with the subsequent southwest monsoon seasonal
rainfall"*, which is anchored with its date at Lesson 19. The honest sentence is that **snow cover
remains a recognised predictor and that the relationship is a tendency, not a determination** —
which is exactly what the 2026 document's own word *"general"* concedes. ⛔ **No trend claim is made
here** (**R-03**).

**Unresolved residual.** Two, and both stand. ⇄ **C-17**: the easterly jet's core/axis height, 13 km
/ 150 mb against 12 km / 200 mb. ⇄ And the **surface-versus-elevated** question: Khullar presents
Flohn and Koteswaram consecutively, records that Flohn's concept is widely accepted, and **never
states whether the Tibetan engine is the principal driver or a contributing element**. This edition
does not decide it either.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Teaching Tibet as a barrier in a monsoon answer.** The barrier role is
  `Geography/advanced/13`'s (**I-6**). Topic 16 owns the plateau as a **heat source**.
- ❌ **Reading "2-3 °C warmer" as warmer than the plains.** It is warmer than **the air over the
  adjoining regions at the same level**.
- ❌ **Giving one height for the tropical easterly jet.** **C-17**: **13 km / 150 mb** at folio 125,
  **12 km / 200 mb** at folio 126.
- ❌ **Omitting the subsidence limb.** The engine is a **closed circuit**: rise over Tibet → outflow
  at 300-200 mb → **sinking over the equatorial Indian Ocean** → low-level return to the west coast.
  Without the subsidence there is no return flow.
- ❌ **Crediting the tropical easterly jet to no one.** **P. Koteswaram and P.R. Krishna, 1952.**
- ❌ **Converting 100 knots silently.** The printed unit is **knots**; state it as printed.
- ❌ **Turning the snow statement into a trend.** It is a printed climatological tendency about
  *"any year"*, and **R-03** forbids any claim that the relationship is strengthening, weakening or
  shifting.
- ❌ **Presenting Koteswaram's engine as the accepted mechanism.** Khullar's own text records that
  **Flohn's** concept *"is widely accepted"*.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I, *"important Geophysical phenomena"* — the elevated-heating component of the
monsoon mechanism, and the discharge of route-in **I-6** from Topic 13's released edition.

**PYQ position.** No verified demand is owned here. This lesson is the **highest-value single block
for a 20-mark mechanism question**, because it supplies a **named author, a date, four printed
dimensions, a pressure-level range, a jet with a speed and an extent, and a stated failure
condition** — the kind of specificity that distinguishes a script. ⛔ No answer letter is recorded or
inferred.

**Answer use.** Deploy this as the **third mechanism paragraph**, after the thermal gradient and the
displaced convergence zone, and signal the boundary in the same sentence that uses it: *"Beyond its
role as a barrier — which governs the winter jet's bifurcation — the Tibetan plateau acts, on
Koteswaram's 1952 account, as an **elevated heat source**: an ellipsoidal mass of about 4.5 million
sq km at roughly 4,000 m, in summer 2-3 °C warmer than air at the same level nearby, generating an
anticyclonic outflow at 300-200 mb that subsides over the equatorial Indian Ocean and returns to the
west coast of India from the south-west as moist equatorial westerlies, with a tropical easterly jet
of over 100 knots on its southern flank."* One sentence; a complete, attributed, quantified circuit.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **Koteswaram (1952), plateau dimensions:** **ellipsoidal**, about **4,000 m** asl, area about
   **4.5 million sq km**, rim ranges rising **6,000-8,000 m**.
2. ✅ **The anomaly:** in summer *"**2 °C to 3 °C warmer** than the air over the adjoining
   regions"* — **at level**, not against the plains at the surface.
3. ✅ **The outflow:** rising air *"spreads outward and sinks over the equatorial Indian Ocean"*;
   deflected right, it moves **anticyclonically in the upper troposphere over Tibet around
   300-200 mb (9-12 km)**.
4. ✅ **The return:** it *"returns to the **west coast of India from the south-west as the
   equatorial westerlies** (Fig. 5.5), picking up moisture and causing **copious rainfall**"*.
5. ✅ **Fig. 5.5** is captioned *"Monsoon as Thermal Engine (After P. Koteswaram)"*.
6. ✅ **Tropical easterly jet:** pronounced jet at **100-200 mb**, speeds *"often exceeding **100
   knots**"*, **first inferred by P. Koteswaram and P.R. Krishna in 1952**; runs from the **southern
   tip of the peninsula to about 20° N**.
7. ⇄ **C-17:** core at **13 km (150 mb)** against the westerly jet at **9 km** (folio 125); Fig. 5.6
   axis of **both** jets at **12 km** (folio 126), easterly band **100-200 mb**. **Both printed;
   neither adopted.**
8. ✅ **The snow statement:** *"The easterly jet **does not come into existence if the snow over the
   Tibet Plateau does not melt**… any year of thick and widespread snow over Tibet will be followed
   by a year of **weak monsoon and less rainfall**."*
9. ✅ **Attribution of the wider view:** *"**Thomson (1951), Flohn (1960) and Stephenson (1965)**
   express similar views, but **Flohn's concept is widely accepted**."*
10. ✅ **Winter split numbers:** the upper westerlies are divided by the plateau into two currents
    reuniting **off the east coast of China**; the **southern branch averages about 240 km p.h. at
    200 mb** against **70-90 km p.h.** for the northern; subsidence beneath the southern branch
    gives the **dry outblowing northerlies** from the subtropical anticyclone over north-western
    India and Pakistan. ↪ the split is `Geography/advanced/13`'s; the **subsidence consequence** is
    used here.
11. ✅ **Route-in I-6 discharged:** plateau **as barrier** = Topic 13; plateau **as monsoon cause** =
    Topic 16, this lesson. ⚠️ C2 §1 line 17 merges both into one cell and names no author.
12. ⚠️ **Flohn's heat is at the surface over north India; Koteswaram's is elevated over Tibet.**
    Different sources, different circuits, **not reconciled in the chapter**.
13. ⛔ **R-03 held:** the snow-monsoon relation is stated as printed; **no trend of any kind is
    claimed**, here or anywhere in this edition.

### Concept check

**Question:** Distinguish the Tibetan plateau's role as a barrier from its role as a heat source,
and state what the second role explains that the first cannot.

**Model answer:** As a **barrier**, the plateau splits the winter upper westerlies into two currents
that reunite off the east coast of China — the southern branch averaging about **240 km p.h. at
200 mb** against **70-90 km p.h.** for the northern — and that role, with the jets themselves,
belongs to `Geography/advanced/13`. As a **heat source**, on **Koteswaram's 1952** account, it is an
ellipsoidal mass of about **4.5 million sq km** at roughly **4,000 m** which in summer is **2-3 °C
warmer than air at the same level** nearby; the rising air spreads out **anticyclonically at
300-200 mb**, sinks over the **equatorial Indian Ocean**, and returns to the **west coast of India
from the south-west as moist equatorial westerlies**. The barrier explains where the jets go. Only
the heat source explains **why the monsoon current arrives moist and strong**, and why a **tropical
easterly jet** of over **100 knots** exists at all. *(149 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That the Himalayan-Tibetan influence on the monsoon is mechanical. The
mechanical role governs the jets; the role Topic 16 owns is **thermodynamic**, and it operates at
mid-troposphere rather than at the surface. *(32 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 8

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** Explain how the Tibetan plateau functions as a heat
engine in the Indian summer monsoon, and state the limits of that account.

**Model:** On **Koteswaram's (1952)** account, printed by Khullar at folio 125, Tibet is an
ellipsoidal plateau of about **4.5 million sq km** at roughly **4,000 m**, ringed by ranges of
**6,000-8,000 m**, which in summer becomes *"**2 °C to 3 °C warmer** than the air over the adjoining
regions"* at the same level. Air rises and spreads outward, moving **anticyclonically at
300-200 mb (9-12 km)**, sinks over the **equatorial Indian Ocean**, and *"returns to the west coast
of India from the south-west as the **equatorial westerlies**… causing copious rainfall"*. On its
southern flank runs the **tropical easterly jet**, over **100 knots**, first inferred by
**Koteswaram and P.R. Krishna in 1952**. Limits: the **2-3 °C** figure is a 1952
estimate without an error bound; the jet's height is printed as **13 km/150 mb** and **12 km/200 mb**
one folio apart; and Khullar records that **Flohn's** rival concept *"is widely accepted"*.
*(148 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** **Thermodynamic chain** — rise, upper outflow, remote subsidence and
low-level return given as a closed circuit, not a list **3** · **Scale reasoning** — the printed
dimensions and the at-level meaning of the 2-3 °C anomaly used correctly **2** · **Figure
reconciliation** — C-17's two heights both printed and neither adopted **2** · **Author
attribution** — Koteswaram and Krishna dated, and Flohn's competing standing disclosed **3**

Progress: 9 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **expedition timeline with an instrument-inventory strip**

### The intuition, before any term

Lesson 6 made a startling claim and gave an argument for it: the air that rains on India crossed the
equator. Lesson 8 added a second circuit that also closes over the ocean. Both are **inferences from
theory**. Neither is a measurement.

Between 1962 and 1979 people went and looked. They put ships across the equator, flew aircraft
through the low-level flow, released balloons over the Bay of Bengal, and coordinated the whole
effort across dozens of countries. This lesson is about what they found — and it matters for two
reasons that have nothing to do with trivia.

**First**, the cross-equatorial flow stopped being a hypothesis and became a measured feature with a
located zone: *a specific zone off the coast of Kenya*. **Second**, the measurements showed that
**fluctuations in that flow tracked fluctuations in rainfall over Maharashtra** — which is the
observational bridge between a planetary-scale circulation and a district-scale harvest, and it is
the strongest single piece of evidence in the whole mechanism half of this topic.

And this is also the lesson where an uncomfortable fact has to be faced honestly: the two names
everybody uses for this flow are **not in any of the books this topic rests on**.

### Correction C-32, handled honestly rather than by deletion

⚠️ **The right treatment of a name without a local source is neither to assert it nor to suppress
it.** Three statements, each true, and an answer should be able to make all three:

1. ✅ **The phenomenon is certified.** Khullar folios 128-129 print a **located crossing zone off
   the Kenya coast**, a **southern-hemisphere origin**, **fluctuating intensity**, and a **measured
   link to Maharashtra rainfall**.
2. ✗ **The names are not certified from this corpus.** *"Mascarene High"*, *"Somali Jet"* and
   *"Findlater Jet"* appear in **no** certified local span (**N-04**). C1 nonetheless lists
   **Mascarene High** and **Somali jet** among its owned core.
3. ⚠️ **The names are standard modern nomenclature** — the subtropical high of the south Indian
   Ocean and the low-level cross-equatorial jet along the East African coast — attached, later, to
   the observation the expeditions made. **Using them is normal practice; sourcing them to Leong,
   Khullar or Majid is not.**

> ⚠️ **The safe Mains sentence**, which loses nothing and risks nothing: *"The monsoon current
> crosses the equator in a narrow zone off the East African coast — identified by the Indian Ocean
> expeditions of the 1960s and 1970s and referred to in modern literature as the Somali or Findlater
> low-level jet — drawing out of the subtropical high of the southern Indian Ocean, commonly called
> the Mascarene High."* The phrase *"referred to in modern literature as"* is the whole correction,
> and it costs five words.

### The technical content, in its printed order

- ✅ **Raman and Ramanathan**, on the tropical easterly jet: easterlies become **very active in the
  upper troposphere after the rainy season begins**; **latent heat from cloud cover** produces
  *inversion of temperature* and causes rainfall. ⚠️ Note the direction of this claim: the jet
  intensifies **after** the rains start, which makes it partly a **consequence** of the monsoon's
  own latent heating rather than purely a driver — a genuine qualification on Lesson 8.
- ✅ **Ananth Krishnan:** the south-west monsoons are deeply influenced by **subtropical cyclones in
  the upper troposphere between 20° and 25° N**, which *"start developing in the beginning of the
  summer season and shift to **30° N about 5-6 weeks later**"*; intensive heat between **20° and
  40° N** gives further strength.
- ✅ **S. Parthasarthy**, in *Trying to solve the Monsoon Riddle*: *"a **weak north-east trade wind
  results in a weak monsoon** and leads to drought conditions."* ⚠️ A striking claim, because it
  makes the **winter** half of the double system a predictor of the **summer** half — which is
  exactly what Lesson 1's "double system" definition would lead you to expect, and which nothing
  else in the chapter develops.
- ✅ **IIOE, 1962-1965**, organised by **ICSU, SCOR and UNESCO with WMO**.
- ✅ **ISMEX, 1973**, and **Monsoon-77**.
- ✅ **The finding, stated across the folio break:** *"there is a **specific zone off the coast of
  Kenya** where the monsoons from the southern hemisphere **crossed the equator** on their way to
  India. It was also observed that the **fluctuations in the intensity of low-level across the
  equator resulted in the fluctuations of rainfall over Maharashtra**."* Upper-air observations over
  the **Bay of Bengal** were also made in **1977**.
- ✅ **MONEX-1979**, organised by **GARP** (ICSU) and **WMO** under **World Weather Watch**;
  **45 countries**; in May 1979, **52 research ships between 10° N and 10° S** and **104 aircraft
  missions**. Three components: **(i) Winter MONEX, 1 December 1978 - 5 March 1979**; **(ii) Summer
  MONEX, 1 May - 31 August 1979**, covering the east coast of Africa, the Arabian Sea and the Bay of
  Bengal; **(iii) WAMEX**, 1 May - 31 August 1979.
- ✅ **IMMC** — International MONEX Management Centres at **Kuala Lumpur and New Delhi**.
- ✅ **The abnormality of 1979:** no intense cold surge in the China Sea; a strong anticyclone in the
  Arabian Sea deflected the south-west monsoon southwards before it touched the Kerala coast, so
  **onset over Kerala was delayed by 12 days**; July had several weak or break-monsoon spells and
  **only one monsoon depression**.

### The causal chain — and why evidence matters more here than mechanism

```text
   A THEORY says the air crosses the equator (Lesson 6, Flohn)
        |
        v
   A SECOND THEORY says a circuit closes over the equatorial ocean
   (Lesson 8, Koteswaram)
        |
        v
   NEITHER CAN BE TESTED FROM LAND. The crossing happens over open
   ocean between 10 N and 10 S, where there are no stations.
        |
        v
   SO BUILD AN OBSERVING SYSTEM
   IIOE 1962-65 -> ISMEX 1973 -> Monsoon-77 -> MONEX-1979
        |
        v
   RESULT 1: the crossing is LOCATED - a specific zone off the
   coast of KENYA
        |
        v
   RESULT 2: the crossing FLUCTUATES in intensity
        |
        v
   RESULT 3 - THE ONE THAT MATTERS - those fluctuations "RESULTED IN
   THE FLUCTUATIONS OF RAINFALL OVER MAHARASHTRA"
        |
        v
   A PLANETARY-SCALE FLOW IS NOW TIED TO A DISTRICT-SCALE OUTCOME.
   This is the evidentiary foundation for everything in Lessons 13,
   14 and 18 - breaks, depressions and teleconnections all assume
   that remote circulation changes show up as local rainfall.

  ASSUMPTION: that a correlation observed in 1970s campaign data
  identifies a causal pathway.
  LIMIT 1: Khullar prints the finding without a statistic, a period,
  a sample size or a significance claim. It is a REPORTED FINDING.
  LIMIT 2: MONEX-1979, the largest campaign, observed an ABNORMAL
  SEASON - 12-day delayed onset, break spells, only ONE depression.
  A campaign that measures an atypical year measures atypically.
  LIMIT 3: the modern NAMES for the flow are not in this corpus
  (C-32), so an answer must separate the certified observation from
  the uncertified vocabulary.
```

### The India hook, with its limitation printed beside it

⚠️ **Fluctuations in the intensity of the low-level cross-equatorial flow track fluctuations of
rainfall over Maharashtra.** This is the single most useful evidentiary sentence in the mechanism
half of the topic, because it converts an abstract hemispheric circuit into a statement about a
named Indian state's rainfall — and Maharashtra is precisely where Lesson 11's Western Ghats
transect and Lesson 17's variability data land.

> ⛔ **Limitation that must be printed with it.** It is a **1970s expedition finding**, printed with
> **no statistic, no period, no sample and no significance claim**. And **the modern names for the
> flow are in no certified book in this corpus** (**C-32**, **N-04**), while the map bank carries
> **no row for Mascarene** and no Somali-jet entry (**N-06**). Quote the finding; attribute the names
> to modern usage; claim neither more precisely than the source allows.

### Comparison, objection, reply and the unresolved residual

**Comparison — inference against measurement, and why the chapter needs both.** Flohn and
Koteswaram are **inferential**: they reason from pressure fields and energy budgets to a circulation.
IIOE, ISMEX, Monsoon-77 and MONEX are **observational**: they report where instruments were and what
they recorded. The comparison worth making in an answer is that the observational era did not
**replace** the theories — it **located** one of their shared consequences and attached a measurable
variability to it. ⚠️ Raman and Ramanathan sharpen this further: their finding that the easterlies
become active **after** the rains begin suggests the jet is partly an effect of the monsoon's own
latent heating, which qualifies the clean driver-to-response picture of Lesson 8.

**Objection.** *"MONEX-1979 observed an abnormal season, with a twelve-day delayed onset, break
spells and one depression. Its results are therefore unreliable, and quoting the campaign at length
inflates its importance."*

**Reply.** The objection inverts the methodological lesson. A campaign that observes an abnormal
season is **not** thereby worthless; it is a campaign that happened to sample the **variability**
rather than the mean, which for a system defined by its variability is informative in a different
way. What it cannot support is a claim about the **climatological normal** monsoon. The discipline
this teaches transfers directly to Lesson 19, where a **single season's** forecast verification must
not be generalised into a statement about forecasting skill. ⚠️ **The honest use is to cite
MONEX-1979 for what it located and for its own atypicality, and never as the description of a normal
monsoon.**

**Unresolved residual.** ⇄ **C-32 is not closable from this corpus.** The names *Mascarene High*,
*Somali Jet* and *Findlater Jet* are in general meteorological use and in no certified local book.
This edition will not pretend they are book payload and will not pretend they do not exist; it
labels them as modern nomenclature attached to a certified observation, and ⛔ **declares the
map-bank gap in its source ledger without editing the map bank** (**C-31**). ⇄ A second residual:
Parthasarthy's claim that a **weak north-east trade** predicts a weak monsoon is printed without
evidence, period or mechanism, and **nothing else in the chapter develops it**.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Sourcing "Mascarene High" or "Somali Jet" to Leong, Khullar or Majid.** **C-32, N-04.** The
  phenomenon is certified; the names are not in this corpus.
- ❌ **Citing MONEX-1979 as a description of the normal monsoon.** It observed a season with a
  **12-day delayed onset** and **only one monsoon depression**.
- ❌ **Merging the four programmes.** **IIOE 1962-65** (ICSU, SCOR, UNESCO, WMO) · **ISMEX 1973** ·
  **Monsoon-77** · **MONEX-1979** (GARP/ICSU, WMO, World Weather Watch).
- ❌ **Dropping WAMEX.** MONEX had **three** components, and the third is the **West African Monsoon
  Experiment**, which is what makes the campaign a study of monsoons rather than of India.
- ❌ **Placing the management centres wrongly.** **IMMC at Kuala Lumpur and New Delhi.**
- ❌ **Quoting the Maharashtra finding as a statistic.** It is a reported qualitative link with **no
  number attached in the source**.
- ❌ **Treating the tropical easterly jet as a pure driver.** **Raman and Ramanathan** record that
  the easterlies become very active **after** the rainy season begins.
- ❌ **Re-deriving Somali-coast upwelling or sea-surface temperature.** Those are
  `Geography/basic/12`'s and `Geography/advanced/12`'s (**R-06**).

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I, *"important Geophysical phenomena"* — the observational basis of the monsoon
circulation, which neither owner carries at all.

**PYQ position.** No verified demand is owned here. One **adjacent and dangerous** item is named and
refused: **2020 Prelims Q93**, on **Ocean Mean Temperature as a monsoon-prediction tool**, is routed
to `Geography/basic/12_The-Oceans-Currents-Tides-Salinity` — a monsoon-prediction stem owned by the
ocean topic, and the clearest demonstration in this edition that a question containing the word
*monsoon* need not be a Topic 16 question. ⛔ No answer letter is recorded or inferred.

**Answer use.** Use this lesson to **upgrade an assertion into evidence**. Any sentence of the form
"the monsoon current crosses the equator" is a claim; the same sentence with *"located by the Indian
Ocean expeditions of 1962-65, 1973 and 1977 in a specific zone off the Kenya coast, where
fluctuations in low-level cross-equatorial intensity were found to track rainfall fluctuations over
Maharashtra"* is evidence. One clause converts the register of the whole paragraph, and MONEX-1979's
**45 countries, 52 ships and 104 aircraft missions** give a 20-mark answer the concreteness it
usually lacks.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **Raman and Ramanathan:** upper-tropospheric easterlies become very active **after the rainy
   season begins**; **latent heat from cloud cover** produces *inversion of temperature* and causes
   rainfall.
2. ✅ **Ananth Krishnan:** **subtropical cyclones in the upper troposphere between 20° and 25° N**,
   shifting to **30° N about 5-6 weeks later**; intensive heat between **20° and 40° N**.
3. ✅ **S. Parthasarthy**, *Trying to solve the Monsoon Riddle*: *"a **weak north-east trade wind
   results in a weak monsoon** and leads to drought conditions."*
4. ✅ **IIOE, 1962-1965** — **ICSU, SCOR and UNESCO with WMO**.
5. ✅ **ISMEX 1973** (Indo-Soviet Monsoon Experiment) and **Monsoon-77**; upper-air observations over
   the **Bay of Bengal** in **1977**.
6. ✅ **The located crossing:** *"a **specific zone off the coast of Kenya** where the monsoons from
   the southern hemisphere **crossed the equator** on their way to India."*
7. ✅ **The bridge finding:** *"the **fluctuations in the intensity of low-level across the equator
   resulted in the fluctuations of rainfall over Maharashtra**."*
8. ✅ **MONEX-1979:** **GARP (ICSU)** and **WMO** under **World Weather Watch**; **45 countries**;
   **52 research ships** between **10° N and 10° S**; **104 aircraft missions**; components
   **(i) Winter MONEX 1 Dec 1978 - 5 Mar 1979**, **(ii) Summer MONEX 1 May - 31 Aug 1979** over the
   east coast of Africa, the Arabian Sea and the Bay of Bengal, **(iii) WAMEX** 1 May - 31 Aug 1979;
   **IMMC at Kuala Lumpur and New Delhi**.
9. ✅ **MONEX-1979 was itself abnormal:** no intense cold surge in the China Sea; an Arabian Sea
   anticyclone deflected the monsoon southwards before it reached Kerala; **onset delayed by 12
   days**; July had weak and break spells and **only one monsoon depression**.
10. ✗ **C-32 / N-04 / N-06:** *"Mascarene High"*, *"Somali Jet"* and *"Findlater Jet"* appear in
    **no certified local book**, and the **map bank has no Mascarene row and no Somali-jet entry**.
    The **phenomenon** is certified; the **names** are modern nomenclature and must be labelled as
    such.

### Concept check

**Question:** What exactly did the Indian Ocean observational programmes establish about the
cross-equatorial flow, and what may an answer not claim on their authority?

**Model answer:** They established three things. **A location** — *"a specific zone off the coast of
Kenya where the monsoons from the southern hemisphere crossed the equator on their way to India"*.
**A variability** — the intensity of that low-level crossing fluctuates. **A link** — those
*"fluctuations… resulted in the fluctuations of rainfall over Maharashtra"*, which ties a
hemispheric flow to a named Indian region. The programmes were **IIOE 1962-65**, **ISMEX 1973**,
**Monsoon-77** and **MONEX-1979**. An answer may **not** claim a statistic, a period or a
significance level, because Khullar prints none; may **not** present **MONEX-1979** as describing a
normal monsoon, since that season had a **12-day delayed onset** and **only one depression**; and may
**not** attribute the names *Mascarene High* or *Somali Jet* to these books, which do not contain
them. *(127 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That the familiar coaching vocabulary for the cross-equatorial flow
comes from the standard texts. The texts certify the **crossing zone and its effect**; the names are
later nomenclature, and an answer should mark them as such rather than cite them as book payload.
*(43 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 9

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** Assess the contribution of the international monsoon
observational programmes to the understanding of the Indian monsoon.

**Model:** Their contribution was to convert an inference into a **located, varying, consequential
measurement**. Before them, cross-equatorial flow was a theoretical requirement of Flohn's displaced
planetary winds. **IIOE (1962-65)**, under **ICSU, SCOR and UNESCO with WMO**, then **ISMEX (1973)**
and **Monsoon-77**, identified *"a **specific zone off the coast of Kenya**"* where southern-hemisphere
air crosses the equator, and found that *"fluctuations in the intensity of low-level across the
equator resulted in the **fluctuations of rainfall over Maharashtra**"* — a planetary flow tied to a
regional harvest. **MONEX-1979**, under **GARP** and **WMO**, mobilised **45 countries**, **52 ships**
between 10°N and 10°S and **104 aircraft missions** across Winter MONEX, Summer MONEX and **WAMEX**.
Its limitation is instructive: the season observed was **abnormal** — onset over Kerala **delayed by
12 days**, break spells, **one** depression — so the campaign sampled variability rather than the
mean. **Evidence located the mechanism; it did not normalise it.** *(146 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** **Evidence provenance** — the shift from inference to measurement stated as
the contribution **3** · **Programme chronology** — all four programmes correctly dated with their
sponsoring bodies **2** · **Nomenclature honesty** — no uncertified name attributed to the books
**2** · **Inference restraint** — the Maharashtra link quoted without inventing a statistic, and
MONEX's atypicality disclosed **3**

Progress: 10 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **decision tree of the operational onset gates, with a two-series isochrone map**

### The intuition, before any term

Up to this point the monsoon has been a circulation. From here it is an **operation**. Somebody has
to stand up on a particular morning and say: *it has arrived.* Farmers sow on that statement,
reservoirs are managed on it, and newspapers lead on it.

So think about the problem that person faces. It rains in Kerala in May. It rains in Kerala in
thunderstorms. It rains on one station and not the next. If you declare the monsoon the first time a
Kerala station is wet, you will declare it four times before it actually arrives — which is exactly
what happened often enough that IMD built a test to stop it.

The test has a shape worth understanding before the detail. It asks three independent questions,
and **all three must be satisfied**. Is it *raining widely and persistently*? Is the *wind* actually
the monsoon wind, deep enough and fast enough? And is the *cloud* deep enough to be a monsoon cloud
rather than a shower? Rain alone is not enough, because rain alone is what fools you.

And one more distinction has to be held throughout: a **normal date** is a climatological average,
an **actual date** is what happened, and a **forecast date** is a prediction. Confusing any two is
the commonest error on this material, and in 2026 all three differ.

### The primary visual — the onset test as a decision tree

```text
  IS IT THE MONSOON? THE OPERATIONAL TEST FOR KERALA
  Source: mausam.imd.gov.in/responsive/monsooninformation_onset.php
  RE-FETCHED AND READ IN FULL AT GENERATION, 2026-10-04 IST, HTTP 200.

   START
     |
     v
  +--------------------------------------------------------------+
  | GATE 0 - CALENDAR                                            |
  | Is the date AFTER 10 MAY?                                    |
  +--------------------------------------------------------------+
     | NO -> no declaration possible
     v YES
  +--------------------------------------------------------------+
  | GATE a - RAINFALL                                            |
  | Of the 14 enlisted stations, do 60 % report 2.5 mm OR MORE   |
  | on TWO CONSECUTIVE DAYS?                                     |
  |                                                              |
  | THE FOURTEEN STATIONS, as printed:                           |
  |   Minicoy . Amini . Thiruvananthapuram . Punalur . Kollam    |
  |   Allapuzha . Kottayam . Kochi . Thrissur . Kozhikode        |
  |   Thalassery . Kannur . Kudulu . Mangalore                   |
  |   [note: MINICOY and AMINI are LAKSHADWEEP, and MANGALORE    |
  |    is KARNATAKA - the "Kerala" test is not confined to       |
  |    Kerala]                                                   |
  | If satisfied, onset is declared ON THE SECOND DAY,           |
  | "provided the following criteria are also in concurrence"    |
  +--------------------------------------------------------------+
     | NO -> wait
     v YES
  +--------------------------------------------------------------+
  | GATE b - WIND FIELD                                          |
  | DEPTH   : westerlies maintained up to 600 hPa                |
  |           in the box EQUATOR to 10 N, 55 E to 80 E           |
  | STRENGTH: zonal wind 15-20 KNOTS at 925 hPa                  |
  |           in the box 5-10 N, 70-80 E                         |
  | SOURCE  : "RSMC wind analysis / satellite derived winds"     |
  +--------------------------------------------------------------+
     | NO -> wait
     v YES
  +--------------------------------------------------------------+
  | GATE c - OUTGOING LONGWAVE RADIATION                         |
  | INSAT-derived OLR BELOW 200 W m-2                            |
  | in the box 5-10 N, 70-75 E                                   |
  | [low OLR = cold, high cloud tops = DEEP convection]          |
  +--------------------------------------------------------------+
     | NO -> wait
     v YES
  +--------------------------------------------------------------+
  | DECLARE ONSET OVER KERALA                                    |
  +--------------------------------------------------------------+
     |
     v
  NORTHERN LIMIT OF MONSOON (NLM), as printed:
  "Southwest monsoon normally sets in over Kerala around 1st June.
   It advances northwards, usually IN SURGES, and covers the entire
   country around 15th JULY. The NLM is the NORTHERN MOST LIMIT of
   monsoon upto which it has advanced on any given day."

  WHY THREE GATES AND NOT ONE
   Rain alone  -> fooled by pre-monsoon thunderstorms (Lesson 4's
                  mango showers are exactly this hazard).
   Wind alone  -> fooled by transient westerly surges.
   OLR alone   -> fooled by any deep cloud mass.
   TOGETHER they require widespread persistent rain, IN a deep and
   fast westerly current, WITH deep convection. That conjunction is
   the monsoon and little else is.
```

### Correction C-15 and correction C-24, both printed

⇄ **C-15 — Khullar gives the Andaman normal twice, differently, three folios apart.**

| Location | Printed value |

by correcting it — it replaces both, because it belongs to a different and later normal period.

### The technical content, in operational order

- ✅ **Andaman normal:** **20th May** (folio 141 text) ⇄ **25 May** (Fig. 5.19) · 📰 **22 May**
  (IMD, retrieved 2026-10-04).
- ✅ **The advance asymmetry.** The advance is *"much faster in the Bay of Bengal than in the Arabian
  Sea"*: the current reaches nearly **20° N in the Bay by the third week of May** while still
  **south of Kerala at about 7° N** in the Arabian Sea. ⚠️ This is the single most under-used fact in
  the onset material. The monsoon is **already most of the way up the Bay** when it is declared over
  Kerala, which is why the Bay branch reaches **Kolkata on 7 June**, three days before the Arabian
  branch reaches **Mumbai on 10 June**, despite Kerala being the declared point of entry.
- ✅ **Kerala normal:** **1st June**, *"the first place of entry on the mainland"*.
- ✅ **The burst.** *"The sudden onset accompanied by **thunder, lightning and heavy downpour**"* is
  the ***monsoon burst***. ⚠️ Note that Leong's *"the rains break by the middle of June"* (Lesson 4)
  uses *break* for the same event — and Lesson 14's *break* means the opposite. **Two words, two
  meanings each, and the pairing is a standing trap.**
- ✅ **Onset variability, folio 142.** On **60 % of occasions the onset occurs between 29th May and
  7th June**; **earliest onset 11th May, in 1918 and 1955**; **most delayed 18th June, in 1972**.
  **Satellite imagery** is used to track the advance day by day. ⚠️ The record spread is **11 May to
  18 June — 38 days**, which is the proper context for a three-day delay in any given season.
- 📰 **The 2026 actuals (A-4), from the IMD Salient Features document of 30 September 2026, read in
  full at generation.** Andaman Sea **16 May 2026**, *"nearly six days earlier than the normal
  schedule"*, reaching parts of the south-east Arabian Sea, Bay of Bengal and the Andaman and
  Nicobar Islands including **Sri Vijaya Puram**; further advance on **18 May** over the Comorin
  area; **nearly stationary for a few days**; progress resumed **23 May**; by **30 May** over
  Lakshadweep, the Comorin area and large parts of the Bay. Kerala **4 June 2026**, *"3 days later
  than the climatological normal onset date of 1 June"*. Entire country **9 July 2026** against a
  normal of **8 July**. Delays of **7-10 days** over Maharashtra, Gujarat, Madhya Pradesh,
  Chhattisgarh and Odisha, **5-10 days** over north-east India, and **near normal** over north-west
  India.

> ⚠️ **The 2026 advance is a teaching gift and must be used precisely.** The monsoon was **six days
> early** into the Andamans, then **stalled**, then arrived **three days late** in Kerala, then
> **covered the country one day late** — while being **seven to ten days late** over central India.
> A single season therefore contains an early signal, a stall, a late onset, a near-normal completion
> and a large regional delay. ⛔ **No trend is claimed from it** (**R-03**); it is cited as one
> season's dated record.

### The causal chain, and the three quantities that must never be merged

```text
  WHY THE ADVANCE IS "IN SURGES" AND NOT A STEADY LINE
   The NLM is a LINE ON A MAP, but the physics behind it is a series
   of discrete events:
      a low-level westerly surge strengthens
         -> convergence deepens along the leading edge
         -> deep convection, low OLR, widespread rain
         -> the NLM jumps
      the surge relaxes
         -> the NLM is stationary for days
   The IMD page states it directly: "It advances northwards, USUALLY
   IN SURGES". The 2026 record shows exactly this - advance on 16 May,
   further advance on 18 May, "NEARLY STATIONARY FOR A FEW DAYS",
   resumption from 23 May.
        |
        v
  AND THE SAME SURGE-AND-PAUSE LOGIC, OPERATING AFTER THE MONSOON IS
  ESTABLISHED, IS WHAT LESSON 14 CALLS AN ACTIVE SPELL AND A BREAK.
  ONSET AND BREAK ARE THE SAME PHYSICS AT DIFFERENT STAGES.

  THE THREE QUANTITIES - AND THE RULE
   NORMAL   : a climatological average over a stated base period.
              Revised periodically. Kerala 1 June; Andaman 22 May;
              full country 8 July.
   ACTUAL   : what a given season did. 2026: 16 May / 4 June / 9 July.
   FORECAST : what was predicted before the event. 2026 Kerala onset
              forecast 26 May with a model error of +/- 4 days
              (Lesson 19).
   RULE: an ACTUAL may be compared with a NORMAL to give a DEPARTURE,
   and with a FORECAST to give an ERROR. A NORMAL may NEVER be
   compared with a FORECAST, and NO ARITHMETIC IS DONE ACROSS NORMAL
   PERIODS. Khullar's 15 July and IMD's 8 July are not two
   measurements of one thing.
```

### The India hook, with its limitation printed beside it

⚠️ **The arrival of the Indian monsoon is declared by a fourteen-station, three-gate test, not by
rain.** Sixty per cent of fourteen named stations reporting 2.5 mm on two consecutive days after
10 May, **plus** westerlies to 600 hPa and 15-20 knots at 925 hPa in specified boxes, **plus** INSAT
OLR below 200 W m⁻². Knowing that the test exists, and that it is a conjunction rather than a
threshold, is worth more in an answer than any date.

> ⛔ **Limitation that must be printed with it.** **The test applies to Kerala.** The rest of the
> advance is judged by the **Northern Limit of Monsoon**, a daily line that moves *"usually in
> surges"*, and the IMD page states no equivalent three-gate test for any other region. Two of the
> fourteen stations — **Minicoy and Amini** — are in **Lakshadweep**, and **Mangalore** is in
> **Karnataka**, so the "Kerala" criterion is not geographically confined to Kerala. And the
> declaration is an **operational judgement supported by criteria**, not an automatic computation.

### Comparison, objection, reply and the unresolved residual

**Comparison — Sidi Ali's dates and IMD's gates, four centuries apart.** Lesson 5 recorded that
**Sidi Ali, in 1554**, reported the monsoon's commencement dates. The purpose was identical to IMD's
— somebody had to decide when to act — but the method could not be more different: a navigator's
accumulated experience against a conjunction of a station network, a two-level wind field and a
satellite radiation threshold. ⚠️ **What has changed is not the question but the falsifiability of
the answer.** Sidi Ali's date could be disputed only by another sailor's judgement; IMD's can be
checked against a defined criterion, which is precisely why a **failed onset forecast** can be
recorded as a failure at Lesson 19.

**Objection.** *"The onset criteria are operational trivia. An examiner wants the mechanism, not a
station list."*

**Reply.** The station list is dispensable; **the structure of the test is not**. The three gates are
a compact statement of what the monsoon **is** — persistent widespread rain, inside a deep fast
westerly current, with deep convection — and a candidate who can write that conjunction has defined
the monsoon operationally in one sentence, which is a far stronger move than reciting a date.
Further, the gates **distinguish the monsoon from the pre-monsoon showers of Lesson 4**: mango
showers pass gate (a) in some years and fail (b) and (c) every time. The criteria are the reason the
declaration is not arbitrary.

**Unresolved residual.** ⇄ **C-15 is unresolved in the source**: Khullar's own text and his own
figure give the Andaman normal as 20 May and 25 May, and nothing in the chapter reconciles them. A
second residual is quieter and worth stating: **the IMD page gives no base period for its normals.**
It says the monsoon *"normally sets in over Kerala around 1st June"* and *"covers the entire country
around 15th July"* — while the 2026 end-of-season document uses a full-country normal of **8 July**.
⇄ **The IMD onset page and the IMD end-of-season document print different full-country normals**,
15 July and 8 July, and this edition prints both with their retrieval dates and adopts neither.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Giving one Andaman normal.** **C-15**: Khullar's text **20 May**, his figure **25 May**; the
  current IMD normal is **22 May** (📰 retrieved 2026-10-04).
- ❌ **Quoting Khullar's "15 July" as the current full-country normal.** **C-24**: the 2026
  end-of-season document uses **8 July**. ⚠️ Note that the **IMD onset page itself still says "around
  15th July"** — print both, adopt neither.
- ❌ **Saying the monsoon is declared when it rains in Kerala.** Rain is **gate (a) of three**, and
  the declaration also requires the **wind field** and the **OLR** gates *"in concurrence"*.
- ❌ **Calling the 14 stations Keralan.** **Minicoy** and **Amini** are **Lakshadweep**;
  **Mangalore** is **Karnataka**.
- ❌ **Writing 2.5 cm for the rainfall threshold.** It is **2.5 mm**, on **two consecutive days**, at
  **60 %** of the stations, **after 10 May**.
- ❌ **Confusing the two senses of "break".** Leong's *"the rains break"* and Khullar's ***burst***
  mean **onset**; Lesson 14's ***break*** means a **mid-season dry spell**.
- ❌ **Treating a 3-day delay as remarkable.** The printed record spread is **11 May 1918 and 1955**
  to **18 June 1972** — **38 days** — with 60 % of onsets between **29 May and 7 June**.
- ❌ **Comparing a normal with a forecast.** A normal is an average, a forecast is a prediction, and
  an actual is an observation. **Departure** is actual minus normal; **error** is actual minus
  forecast.
- ❌ **Deriving a trend from 2026.** **R-03.** One season is one season.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I — the onset and advance of the south-west monsoon, which C2 §3 names without
criteria and which C1 correctly warns must be dated.

**PYQ position.** No verified demand is owned here, but this lesson is the **operational backbone**
of the owned **2026 Prelims GS-I** demand at Lesson 20, since the Andaman and Nicobar Islands are
where the monsoon's advance **begins** — and the 2026 actual advance over the islands is dated
**16 May**. ⛔ The locally held 2026 Set-A key is **provisional**; **no answer letter is recorded or
inferred**.

**Answer use.** Replace the date with the **test**, then date the test. *"Onset over Kerala is
declared, after 10 May, when 60 per cent of fourteen enlisted stations report 2.5 mm or more on two
consecutive days, provided westerlies extend to 600 hPa between the equator and 10°N, zonal winds
reach 15-20 knots at 925 hPa, and INSAT outgoing longwave radiation falls below 200 W m⁻² over the
south-eastern Arabian Sea; the normal date is 1 June, and in 2026 onset was declared on 4 June
(IMD, 30 September 2026)."* That is one sentence containing a definition, a threshold set, a normal
and a dated actual — and it is impossible to write without having read the source.

### Revision points

1. 📰 **Gate 0:** the test operates only **after 10 May**.
2. 📰 **Gate (a), rainfall:** **60 %** of the **14** enlisted stations report **2.5 mm or more** on
   **two consecutive days**; onset is declared **on the second day**, *"provided the following
   criteria are also in concurrence"*.
3. 📰 **The fourteen stations:** **Minicoy, Amini, Thiruvananthapuram, Punalur, Kollam, Allapuzha,
   Kottayam, Kochi, Thrissur, Kozhikode, Thalassery, Kannur, Kudulu, Mangalore.** Two are
   **Lakshadweep**; one is **Karnataka**.
4. 📰 **Gate (b), wind field:** westerlies maintained **up to 600 hPa** in **equator-10°N, 55°E-80°E**;
   zonal wind **15-20 knots at 925 hPa** in **5-10°N, 70°-80°E**; source *"RSMC wind analysis /
   satellite derived winds"*.
5. 📰 **Gate (c), OLR:** INSAT-derived OLR **below 200 W m⁻²** in **5-10°N, 70°-75°E**.
6. 📰 **NLM:** the monsoon *"advances northwards, usually **in surges**"* and the **Northern Limit of
   Monsoon** is *"the **northern most limit** of monsoon upto which it has advanced on any given
   day"*.
7. ⇄ **C-15, the Andaman normal:** Khullar text **20 May** · Khullar Fig. 5.19 **25 May** · 📰 IMD
   current normal **22 May** (retrieved 2026-10-04).
8. ✅ **The advance asymmetry:** faster in the **Bay** than in the Arabian Sea — nearly **20° N in
   the Bay by the third week of May** while still **south of Kerala at about 7° N** in the Arabian
   Sea.
9. ✅ **Kerala normal 1 June**, *"the first place of entry on the mainland"*; the **burst** is the
   sudden onset with **thunder, lightning and heavy downpour**.
10. ✅ **Onset variability:** **60 % of onsets between 29 May and 7 June**; **earliest 11 May, 1918
    and 1955**; **latest 18 June, 1972**; the advance is tracked by **satellite imagery**.
11. ✅ **Khullar Fig. 5.19 isochrones:** **25 May · 1 June · 5 June · 10 June · 15 June · 1 July ·
    15 July**.
12. ✗ **C-24:** Khullar's normals predate IMD's revision. 📰 Current normals, retrieved 2026-10-04:
    **Andaman 22 May · Kerala 1 June · full country 8 July · withdrawal from west Rajasthan
    17 September**. ⇄ The IMD **onset page** still prints *"around 15th July"* for full-country
    coverage while the **end-of-season document** uses **8 July**. **Both printed; neither adopted.**
13. 📰 **2026 actuals (A-4):** Andaman Sea **16 May**, *"nearly six days earlier"* than the **22 May**
    normal, reaching **Sri Vijaya Puram**; further advance **18 May**; **nearly stationary for a few
    days**; resumed **23 May**; Lakshadweep and the Comorin area by **30 May**; **Kerala 4 June**,
    **3 days late**; **entire country 9 July** against a normal of **8 July**.
14. 📰 **2026 regional delays:** **7-10 days** over Maharashtra, Gujarat, Madhya Pradesh,
    Chhattisgarh and Odisha; **5-10 days** over north-east India; **near normal** over north-west
    India.
15. ⚠️ **The rule that governs every onset number:** a **departure** is actual minus normal; an
    **error** is actual minus forecast; a normal and a forecast are **never** compared, and **no
    arithmetic is done across normal periods**.

### Concept check

**Question:** It rains heavily across Kerala on 20 and 21 May. Can the monsoon be declared? Set out
what else must be true, and name the three quantities that must be kept apart in any answer about
onset dates.

**Model answer:** **Not on the rainfall alone.** The date clears gate 0, since the test operates
after **10 May**, and the rainfall may clear gate (a) if **60 % of the fourteen enlisted stations**
report **2.5 mm or more on two consecutive days**. But onset is declared *"provided the following
criteria are also in concurrence"*: **westerlies maintained to 600 hPa** between the equator and
10°N, 55°-80°E; **zonal wind of 15-20 knots at 925 hPa** in 5-10°N, 70°-80°E; and **INSAT OLR below
200 W m⁻²** in 5-10°N, 70°-75°E. Heavy May rain without a deep westerly current and deep convection
is pre-monsoon thunderstorm activity. The three quantities are the **normal** (Kerala 1 June), the
**actual** (4 June in 2026) and the **forecast** (26 May ± 4 days in 2026). Actual minus normal is a
**departure**; actual minus forecast is an **error**. *(135 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That the monsoon's arrival is a rainfall event. It is a **circulation
change evidenced by three independent criteria**, and rainfall is only the first of them — which is
why pre-monsoon showers can be heavy without triggering a declaration. *(38 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 10

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** Explain how the onset of the south-west monsoon is
operationally determined, and why the determination requires more than rainfall.

**Model:** IMD declares onset over Kerala only when **three independent criteria concur** after
**10 May**. **Rainfall:** **60 %** of fourteen enlisted stations — Minicoy, Amini,
Thiruvananthapuram, Punalur, Kollam, Allapuzha, Kottayam, Kochi, Thrissur, Kozhikode, Thalassery,
Kannur, Kudulu, Mangalore — must report **2.5 mm or more on two consecutive days**. **Wind field:**
westerlies must extend to **600 hPa** between the equator and 10°N, 55°-80°E, with zonal winds of
**15-20 knots at 925 hPa** over 5-10°N, 70°-80°E. **Radiation:** INSAT **OLR below 200 W m⁻²** over
5-10°N, 70°-75°E. Rainfall alone is insufficient because pre-monsoon thunderstorms — the *mango
showers* of Tamil Nadu and Andhra — produce heavy May rain without a deep westerly current; the wind
and radiation gates test for the **circulation** and the **deep convection** that define the monsoon.
The normal is **1 June**; in **2026** onset was declared on **4 June** (IMD, 30 September 2026).
*(138 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** **Criterion recall** — all three gates named and the calendar condition
retained **3** · **Threshold precision** — 2.5 mm, 60 %, 600 hPa, 15-20 knots, 925 hPa, 200 W m⁻²
reproduced exactly **3** · **Operational sequencing** — concurrence stated, not a list of
alternatives **2** · **Normal-versus-actual separation** — the 1 June normal and the dated 2026
actual kept distinct **2**

Progress: 11 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **orographic transect with a quantified rainfall profile**

### The intuition, before any term

The south-west monsoon arrives at the west coast of India as a wind blowing almost straight at a
wall. The wall is six hundred kilometres long and about a kilometre high, and it stands between the
sea and the rest of the country.

What happens next is the most quantitatively well-documented thing in this entire topic, and it is
worth stating as a surprise rather than a formula. **The wind deposits almost everything it carries
within eighty to a hundred kilometres of the crest.** Not most of it: almost all of it. A station on
the coast takes 190 centimetres. A station fifty kilometres inland and just below the crest takes
60. A station 160 kilometres inland takes 50. The crest itself takes 400 to 500 — and 80 to 100
kilometres behind it, the figure has fallen to 30 to 50.

That is a tenfold collapse across a distance you can drive in two hours. And it explains, without
any further machinery, why peninsular India has a wet strip and a dry interior; why the Deccan's
agriculture is what it is; and why a national rainfall average is nearly meaningless.

But the Arabian branch is not one stream. It is **three**, and the second and third do something
quite different from the first — including the thing a thermal theory of the monsoon can never
explain, which is why **the hottest land in India is also among the driest**.

### The primary visual — the transect, with its numbers

### The three streams, and what each one is for

```text
  *** THE THIRD STREAM IS THE LESSON'S INTELLECTUAL PAYLOAD ***
  A thermal theory predicts that the HOTTEST land draws the STRONGEST
  inflow and therefore the MOST rain. Western Rajasthan is the
  hottest land in the system and one of the driest places in India.
  The explanation is GEOMETRIC, not thermal: the Aravali runs
  PARALLEL to the stream, so there is NOTHING TO LIFT THE AIR.
  Mt Abu proves it by exception - where the range DOES present a
  face, 170 cm falls against 60-80 cm nearby.
  This is Lesson 5's FAILURE (2) - "the shape of the continents, and
  OROGRAPHY" - in a single Indian example.
```

### Why the Arabian branch is the stronger, in the book's own two reasons

✅ **Khullar folio 143 states two reasons, and both must be given:**

1. **the Arabian Sea is larger than the Bay of Bengal**; and
2. **the entire Arabian Sea current advances towards India**, whereas **only part of the Bay current
   enters India**, the remainder proceeding to **Myanmar, Thailand and Malaysia**.

⚠️ The second reason is the better one and is usually omitted. It is a statement about **geometry of
delivery**, not about size: a branch can be large and still deliver little if most of it goes
somewhere else. ⚠️ Note also the tension with Lesson 10 — the **Bay** branch advances **faster** and
reaches Kolkata **before** the Arabian branch reaches Mumbai, yet the Arabian branch is the
**stronger**. **Speed of advance and strength of flow are different properties**, and conflating
them is a standard error.

### The dated anchor, A-3

📰 **The 2026 season gives this lesson its test case, and it is a severe one.** From the IMD
*Salient Features of the 2026 Southwest Monsoon Season*, dated **30 September 2026** and read in full
at generation: **South Peninsula seasonal rainfall was 542.0 mm against an LPA of 716.2 mm = 76 %**,
which the document records as *"**4th lowest since 1901** and **2nd lowest since 2001**"*. The
forecast issued on **29 May 2026** had placed South Peninsular India in the **below normal** category
(< 94 % of LPA), and this is the **one** of the four homogeneous regions whose seasonal forecast the
end-of-season document records as having verified: *"The seasonal forecast issued for homogeneous
regions during the season was within the range of the forecast for South Peninsular India."*

⚠️ **How to use this without overreaching.** The South Peninsula region contains the windward Ghats
and the leeward Deccan, so a regional figure of 76 % tells you the **branch underperformed**; it
does not tell you how the deficit was distributed across the transect, and the document does not
break it down that way. ⛔ **No attribution of the deficit to any cause is made here** — not to El
Niño, not to climate change (**R-02**), and no trend is claimed (**R-03**). The number is cited as
**one season's dated regional total**.

### The causal chain, with its assumptions and its limits

```text
   A moist south-westerly current crosses the Arabian Sea
        |
        v
   It meets the Western Ghats ALMOST PERPENDICULARLY between about
   10 and 20 N  (folio 144)
        |
        v
   FORCED ASCENT -> cooling -> condensation -> "OVER 250 CM" on the
   windward slope; crest 400-500 cm annual
        |
        v
   The air crosses the crest HAVING SHED MOST OF ITS MOISTURE and
   DESCENDS, warming and drying
        |
        v
   A NARROW BELT OF MARKED ARIDITY immediately leeward:
   Khandala 60 cm at ~50 km; Pune 50 cm at ~160 km;
   30-50 cm within 80-100 km of the crest
        |
        v
   FURTHER EAST, "rainfall INCREASES AGAIN eastwards" - because the
   leeward trough is a SUBSIDENCE effect of the immediate lee, not a
   permanent exhaustion of the air
        |
        +-------- MEANWHILE --------+
        |                           |
        v                           v
   STREAM 2 bypasses the wall    STREAM 3 runs PARALLEL to the
   through the NARMADA-TAPI      ARAVALI, which "offers no obstacle"
   TROUGHS -> Nagpur 60 cm       -> no forced ascent -> "the whole
        |                        of Rajasthan is a desert area"
        |                           |
        |                           v
        |                        EXCEPTION PROVING THE RULE: the
        |                        south-eastern EDGE presents a face
        |                        -> MT ABU 170 cm vs 60-80 cm
        v
   The two surviving streams and the Bay branch merge along a line
   through AGRA and FEROZEPUR (folio 143)

  ASSUMPTION: that orographic lifting is the dominant rain-producing
  mechanism for this branch. For the WINDWARD COAST this is well
  supported by the figures. For the INTERIOR it is NOT - Lesson 13
  shows depressions doing most of the inland work, and C2 §2's own
  trap says so: "Monsoon rainfall is not only orographic."
  LIMIT 1: the transect figures are MONSOON-SEASON values from ONE
  BOOK with NO STATED BASE PERIOD - except the crest figure, which
  is explicitly ANNUAL. MIXING THE TWO IS AN ERROR.
  LIMIT 2: "the whole of Rajasthan is a desert area" is the book's
  wording and is too strong - eastern Rajasthan is not desert.
  THE THAR AS A DESERT IS Geography/advanced/07's.
```

### The India hook, with its limitation printed beside it

⚠️ **Pune receives about 50 cm of monsoon rain where Mumbai receives 190 cm, 160 kilometres away.**
Khandala, only about **50 km** from Mumbai and *"a little below the crest"*, already gets **60 cm**.
The crest takes **400-500 cm annually** and the figure falls to **30-50 cm within 80-100 km leeward**.
This single transect explains the entire agricultural, hydrological and settlement contrast between
the Konkan and the Desh.

> ⛔ **Limitation that must be printed with it.** These are **monsoon-season** figures from **one
> book**, with **no stated base period** — **except** the **400-500 cm crest figure, which is
> explicitly annual**. They are therefore **not annual normals**, and they must not be set beside
> Lesson 17's annual classes or beside any IMD figure without that distinction. ⚠️ And the leeward
> profile is **not monotonic**: a narrow arid belt is followed by rainfall *"increasing again
> eastwards"*, so "the Deccan is a rain shadow" is a half-truth.

### Comparison, objection, reply and the unresolved residual

**Comparison — three streams, three different physics.** Stream 1 is **orographic**: a wall, a
perpendicular wind, forced ascent, over 250 cm. Stream 2 is **topographically guided**: the
Narmada-Tapi troughs are low corridors that let moist air inland where the Ghats would have stopped
it, and the result at Nagpur, **60 cm**, is modest because the lifting is weak. Stream 3 is
**unlifted**: it travels along the Aravali rather than across it, so nothing forces ascent at all.
⚠️ The three streams are therefore a natural experiment in what orography does — **a face, a gap and
a parallel** — and the Mount Abu exception, **170 cm against 60-80 cm** on the surrounding plains,
is the control.

**Objection.** *"If the Aravali's orientation explains Rajasthan's dryness, why is the Thar a desert
rather than merely a dry region? Orientation is too slight a cause for an outcome that large."*

**Reply.** It is too slight, and this edition does not claim otherwise. What Khullar's third stream
explains is **why the monsoon current, which passes directly over Rajasthan, does not rain there** —
a specific question about the geometry of lifting, answered by a specific geometric fact and tested
by the Mount Abu exception. ↪ **Why the Thar is a desert** — its full aridity budget, its
continentality, the subsidence inherited from Lesson 8's winter anticyclone, its soils and its
desertification dynamics — is **`Geography/advanced/07`'s question**, and Topic 16 answers only its
own half. Keeping the two apart is what the boundary table requires, and merging them is how a
monsoon answer drifts into a desert answer and loses both.

**Unresolved residual.** **Khullar's transect is internally consistent but externally undated.** He
prints monsoon-season values for three stations and an annual range for the crest, and gives no base
period for any of them. ⚠️ The ratios are robust and the absolute levels are not verifiable from this
corpus. ⇄ A second residual: Khullar's own characterisation — *"consequently the whole of Rajasthan
is a desert area"* — is contradicted by his own folio 152 rainfall classes at Lesson 17, which place
only **western** Rajasthan in the very-low class. **The sentence overstates its own author's data.**

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Treating the Arabian branch as one stream.** It divides into **three**, and the second and
  third do not strike the Ghats at all.
- ❌ **Giving only one reason for the Arabian branch's strength.** Khullar gives **two**: the
  **larger sea**, and that the **entire** Arabian current advances on India while only **part** of
  the Bay current does, the rest going to **Myanmar, Thailand and Malaysia**.
- ❌ **Confusing speed of advance with strength of flow.** The **Bay** branch advances faster
  (Lesson 10); the **Arabian** branch is the stronger.
- ❌ **Mixing the annual crest figure with the monsoon-season station figures.** **400-500 cm** is
  **annual**; **190 / 60 / 50 cm** are **monsoon-season**.
- ❌ **Describing the lee as uniformly dry.** A **narrow belt of marked aridity** is followed by
  rainfall *"increasing again eastwards"*.
- ❌ **Explaining Rajasthan's dryness thermally.** The printed explanation is **geometric**: the
  third stream runs **parallel** to the Aravali, which *"offers no obstacle"*.
- ❌ **Omitting Mount Abu.** **About 170 cm** against **60-80 cm** on the surrounding plains is the
  exception that proves the geometric explanation.
- ❌ **Answering a Thar question from this lesson.** The desert itself is
  `Geography/advanced/07_Thar-Desertification`'s.
- ❌ **Claiming monsoon rainfall is orographic.** C2's own trap line says otherwise, and Lesson 13
  shows the depressions doing the inland work.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I — the distribution of monsoon rainfall over India, which C2 §2 asserts in one
table row and does not quantify.

**PYQ position.** No verified demand is owned here. One **adjacent** item is named and refused:
**2020 Prelims Q87**, on a subtropical crop requiring frost-free days and moderate rainfall, is
routed to `Geography/basic/30_Primary-Economic-Activities-Agriculture` under **R-05**. ⛔ No answer
letter is recorded or inferred. This lesson is the quantitative core of any *"explain the spatial
distribution of monsoon rainfall"* answer.

**Answer use.** Never describe the rain shadow qualitatively when you can draw it. *"The first
Arabian stream strikes the Ghats almost perpendicularly between about 10° and 20° N and yields over
250 cm on the windward slope, with 400-500 cm annually at the crest; Mumbai records about 190 cm in
the season, Khandala 60 cm some 50 km east and just below the crest, and Pune 50 cm at about 160 km
— the crest figure falling to 30-50 cm within 80-100 km leeward, after a narrow belt of marked
aridity beyond which rainfall rises again eastwards."* Six numbers and three named places in one
sentence; the examiner can see the profile.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **Two reasons the Arabian branch is stronger (folio 143):** the Arabian Sea is **larger** than
   the Bay; and the **entire** Arabian current advances on India, while only **part** of the Bay
   current enters, the rest going to **Myanmar, Thailand and Malaysia**.
2. ✅ **The branch divides into THREE distinct streams** (folio 144).
3. ✅ **First stream:** impinges on the west coast, giving **over 250 cm**, particularly between
   **10° and 20° N**, where the impingement is **perpendicular** to the Western Ghats.
4. ✅ **The transect (folio 145):** **Mumbai about 190 cm**; **Khandala about 50 km east, a little
   below the crest, 60 cm**; **Pune about 160 km from Mumbai on the leeward side, 50 cm in the
   monsoon season**.
5. ✅ **The crest and the collapse:** the Western Ghats crest receives about **400-500 cm annual**
   rainfall, *"drastically reduced to about **30-50 cm** within a distance of **80-100 km** leeward
   from the crest"*.
6. ✅ **The lee is not monotonic:** a **narrow belt of marked aridity** lies immediately leeward,
   *"after which rainfall increases again eastwards"*.
7. ✅ **Second stream:** enters the **Narmada-Tapi troughs** and reaches central India, giving
   **Nagpur about 60 cm**.
8. ✅ **Third stream:** moves **north-easterly parallel to the Aravali Range**, which *"offers no
   obstacle"* — *"consequently the whole of Rajasthan is a desert area"*.
9. ✅ **The exception:** the **south-eastern edge** of the Aravali gives **Mount Abu about 170 cm**
   against **60-80 cm** on the surrounding plains.
10. ✅ **The merge (folio 143):** the Arabian and Bay branches meet roughly along a line through
    **Agra and Ferozepur** and merge into a single current; the combined current extends to west
    Uttar Pradesh, Haryana, Punjab, Rajasthan and finally Himachal Pradesh and Kashmir.
11. 📰 **A-3, dated anchor:** **South Peninsula 2026 seasonal rainfall 542.0 mm = 76 % of an LPA of
    716.2 mm**, *"4th lowest since 1901 and 2nd lowest since 2001"* (IMD, **30 September 2026**).
    ⛔ No cause is attributed and **no trend is claimed**.
12. ⚠️ **Unit and vintage discipline:** **400-500 cm is annual**; **190 / 60 / 50 / 60 / 170 /
    60-80 cm are monsoon-season** figures from one book with **no stated base period**. ⇄ And
    *"the whole of Rajasthan is a desert area"* is **contradicted by the same author's folio 152
    classes**, which place only **western** Rajasthan in the very-low band.

### Concept check

**Question:** Western Rajasthan lies directly under the south-west monsoon current and is the
hottest land in the system, yet it is among the driest regions in India. Explain, and state what
part of the explanation is not this topic's.

**Model answer:** The explanation is **geometric, not thermal**. The Arabian Sea branch divides into
**three streams**; the **third** moves north-easterly **parallel to the Aravali Range**, which
therefore *"offers no obstacle"* — there is **no forced ascent**, so the moist current passes over
western Rajasthan without being lifted and without raining. Khullar states the consequence directly:
*"consequently the whole of Rajasthan is a desert area."* The **exception confirms the mechanism**:
where the south-eastern edge of the Aravali does present a face, **Mount Abu receives about 170 cm**
against **60-80 cm** on the surrounding plains. A thermal account predicts the opposite, which is why
this is a textbook instance of the classical theory's second recorded failure — *"the shape of the
continents, and orography"*. ↪ **Why the Thar is a desert**, as against why the monsoon does not rain
on it, belongs to `Geography/advanced/07`. *(137 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That rainfall follows the thermal low. The low determines **where air
converges**; rainfall requires **lifting**, and a current running parallel to a range is never
lifted. *(26 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 11

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** Examine the role of relief in determining the
distribution of rainfall delivered by the Arabian Sea branch of the south-west monsoon.

**Model:** Relief governs it almost completely, and the branch's **three streams** demonstrate three
different relief relations. The **first** strikes the Western Ghats **perpendicularly between about
10° and 20° N**, giving **over 250 cm** windward and **400-500 cm annually** at the crest; the figure
is *"drastically reduced to about **30-50 cm** within **80-100 km** leeward"*, with **Mumbai 190 cm**,
**Khandala 60 cm** at some 50 km, and **Pune 50 cm** at about 160 km in the monsoon season — then a
**narrow arid belt** before rainfall *"increases again eastwards"*. The **second** bypasses the wall
through the **Narmada-Tapi troughs**, lifting weakly: **Nagpur about 60 cm**. The **third** runs
**parallel to the Aravali**, which *"offers no obstacle"*, so the hottest land in India stays dry —
while **Mount Abu**, on the range's south-eastern face, takes **170 cm** against **60-80 cm** nearby.
**A face, a gap and a parallel: three outcomes from one current.** *(147 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** **Transect construction** — the windward-crest-lee sequence built with its
printed stations and distances **3** · **Gradient quantification** — the 400-500 to 30-50 cm collapse
and its 80-100 km scale stated exactly **2** · **Orographic causality** — the three streams
contrasted as three relief relations rather than listed **3** · **Leeward reasoning** — the narrow
arid belt and the renewed eastward increase retained, and the annual/seasonal units kept apart
**2**
