Progress: 12 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **funnel diagram with an east-west decline ladder and a dual-figure column**

### The intuition, before any term

The Arabian branch met a wall and dumped its load. The Bay branch meets something quite different: a
**funnel** and a **deflector**.

The funnel is a V-shaped valley in the Khasi hills whose open end faces the oncoming moist current.
Air entering it cannot spread sideways, so it is squeezed and forced upward, and the result is the
wettest ground on the planet — with the additional oddity that the wettest station is **not** the
famous one. Mawsynram, sixteen kilometres west of Cherrapunji, out-rains it by **119 centimetres a
year**, and a station only **90 kilometres away** takes **161 centimetres**, which is about a seventh
as much.

The deflector is the Himalaya. The Bay current cannot escape northward, so it is turned **westward**
and pushed up the Ganga plain, travelling further and further from its moisture source. Hence the
single most reliable spatial pattern in Indian climatology: **rainfall in the northern plains
declines steadily from east to west.** Kolkata, Patna, Allahabad, Delhi, Bikaner — the figures fall
the whole way.

C1 states the reason for this better than C2 does, and its formulation is worth preserving exactly:
the current is **progressively depleted, not blocked**.

And this lesson is where the book's own numbers start disagreeing with each other, which makes it
the natural place to learn the discipline of printing two figures without choosing.

### The primary visual — the funnel and the ladder

### Correction C-20, printed rather than resolved

⇄ **Leong and Khullar disagree about Cherrapunji's annual rainfall.**

| Source | Printed | Converted | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Leong printed p. 124** | *"Cherrapunji has an average annual rainfall of **425 inches** and a record of **905 inches in 1861**"* | **1,079.5 cm**; record **2,298.7 cm** | no base period stated |
| **Khullar folio 146** | **1,102 cm** | — | no base period stated |

⚠️ **The difference is about 23 cm a year** (1,102 − 1,079.5 = 22.5 cm). ⚠️ The likely reason is that
the two books use **different normal periods**, and **neither states one** — which is exactly the
condition under which a difference cannot be called an error. ⚠️ Note also what only Leong has: the
**905-inch (2,298.7 cm) record of 1861**, which no other source in this corpus carries, and which
is the single most quotable extreme in the topic. **Both annual figures are printed; neither is
adopted.** The safe sentence: *"Cherrapunji's annual normal is printed as 1,102 cm by Khullar and as
425 inches (1,079.5 cm) by Leong, neither stating a base period; Leong additionally records
905 inches in 1861."*

### The technical content, stream by stream

✅ **The Bay of Bengal branch divides into TWO streams** (folio 146) — against the Arabian branch's
three.

**Stream 1 — across the delta into Meghalaya.**

- ✅ Crosses the **Ganga-Brahmaputra delta** and reaches **Meghalaya**.
- ✅ **Cherrapunji (25°15′N, 91°44′E)** at **1,313 m** receives **1,102 cm** annually, *mostly
  June-August*; highest daily record **103.6 cm**.
- ✅ **Mawsynram (25°18′N, 91°35′E)** at **1,329 m**, **16 km to the west**, records **1,221 cm**;
  highest daily record **99 cm**.
- ✅ Both lie on the **southern slopes of the Khasi hills at the head of a funnel-shaped valley**.
- ✅ **Guwahati, only 90 km from Cherrapunji, gets only 161 cm.**

**Stream 2 — the westward deflection.**

- ✅ Reaches the **Himalayan foothills**, is **deflected west**, and brings widespread rain to the
  Ganga plain with a steady east-to-west decline.
- ✅ **Kolkata 119 cm · Patna 105 cm · Allahabad 91 cm · Delhi 51 cm · Bikaner 24 cm** during the
  south-west monsoon period (folio 146). ⇄ **C-13:** folio 148 prints **Allahabad 76 cm** and
  **Delhi 56 cm** for the same quantity.
- ✅ **The advance timeline:** the Bay branch spreads rapidly over **Assam**, with normal arrival at
  **Kolkata on 7 June** (folio 142); the two branches **meet roughly along a line through Agra and
  Ferozepur** (folio 143); **by the end of June the monsoon is usually established over most of the
  country**; **by mid-July it extends into Kashmir and the remaining parts, but only as a feeble
  current** because it has shed most of its moisture.
- ✅ **Tamil Nadu's double shadow (folio 146):** the Tamil Nadu coast stays relatively dry because it
  is in the **rain shadow of the Arabian Sea current** *and* lies **parallel to the Bay current**.
  ⚠️ This is the structural reason Tamil Nadu needs a **different season**, and Lesson 16 supplies it.

### The dated anchor, A-3 — and the conflict it arrived with

📰 **East & Northeast India recorded its lowest seasonal rainfall since 1901 in 2026.** From the IMD
*Salient Features of the 2026 Southwest Monsoon Season*, **30 September 2026**, read in full at
generation: the region received **74 %** of its LPA of **1,367.3 mm**, and the document records it as
*"**lowest since 1901**"*.

⇄ **X-02, found at generation.** The **highlights** of that document print **1,017.1 mm**; **Table 1
of the same document** prints **1,016.3 mm**. ⚠️ Both round to **74 %** of the 1,367.3 mm LPA
(1,016.3 / 1,367.3 = 74.33 %; 1,017.1 / 1,367.3 = 74.39 %), so the conflict changes no category and
no percentage — which is precisely why it is useful. **It is a conflict with no consequence, inside
one official document, and it teaches the habit of checking a headline figure against the table it
came from.** Both are printed here; **neither is adopted**.

⛔ **No cause is attributed for the 2026 north-eastern deficit, and no trend is claimed**
(**R-02**, **R-03**). The figure is one season's dated regional total.

### The causal chain, with its assumptions and its limits

```text
   The Bay branch crosses the Bay of Bengal and the delta
        |
        +---------------------------+
        |                           |
        v                           v
   STREAM 1 into MEGHALAYA      STREAM 2 to the FOOTHILLS
        |                           |
        v                           v
   A FUNNEL-SHAPED VALLEY       The HIMALAYA prevents northward
   at the head of the southern  escape -> the current is DEFLECTED
   Khasi slopes                 WESTWARD and travels up the plain
        |                           |
        v                           v
   Air entering cannot spread   It rains as it goes, and each
   laterally -> CONVERGENCE     rainfall event REMOVES MOISTURE
   PLUS forced ascent -> the        |
   most extreme orographic          v
   rainfall on Earth            "PROGRESSIVELY DEPLETED as it moves
        |                        inland, NOT BLOCKED" (C1 §9.1)
        v                           |
   Mawsynram 1,221 cm               v
   Cherrapunji 1,102 cm         Kolkata 119 -> Patna 105 ->
   Guwahati, 90 km away,        Allahabad 91 [or 76] -> Delhi 51
   only 161 cm                  [or 56] -> Bikaner 24
        |                           |
        v                           v
   WHY GUWAHATI IS DRY: it is   By MID-JULY the current reaches
   in the BRAHMAPUTRA VALLEY,   Kashmir "BUT ONLY AS A FEEBLE
   not on the windward face.    CURRENT because it has shed most
   Ninety kilometres of         of its moisture"
   horizontal distance matters
   far less than WHICH SIDE OF
   THE SLOPE you are on.

  ASSUMPTION: that the east-west decline is a depletion gradient.
  LIMIT 1: it is ALSO a DISTANCE-FROM-SEA effect and a
  SYSTEM-FREQUENCY effect - Lesson 13 shows that most of the
  inland rain is delivered by DEPRESSIONS, whose tracks and
  frequency also decline westward. Depletion is one mechanism in
  a compound gradient, not the whole of it.
  LIMIT 2: the ladder's own numbers are printed TWO WAYS for two of
  its five stations (C-13), and no station figure carries a base
  period.
  LIMIT 3: both branches merge along the Agra-Ferozepur line, so
  the western end of the ladder is not supplied by the Bay branch
  alone.
```

### The India hook, with its limitation printed beside it

⚠️ **Mawsynram out-rains Cherrapunji by 119 cm a year from 16 kilometres away, and Guwahati, 90
kilometres from Cherrapunji, receives 161 cm.** Three stations within a hundred kilometres span
**1,221 cm**, **1,102 cm** and **161 cm**. There is no better single demonstration anywhere in Indian
geography that **rainfall is a property of position on a slope, not of region**.

> ⛔ **Limitation that must be printed with it.** **Leong and Khullar disagree about Cherrapunji's
> normal by about 23 cm** — **425 inches [1,079.5 cm]** against **1,102 cm** — and **neither states a
> base period** (**C-20**). ⚠️ And the famous comparison contains an inversion that must not be
> smoothed: **Mawsynram has the higher annual total but the lower daily record** (99 cm against
> Cherrapunji's 103.6 cm). *Wettest place* and *most intense day* are different superlatives.

### Comparison, objection, reply and the unresolved residual

**Comparison — the wall against the funnel.** The Western Ghats (Lesson 11) are a **linear barrier**
struck perpendicularly: the air is lifted along a front hundreds of kilometres long, and the rainfall
is high and spread along the coast — *over 250 cm*, with 400-500 cm at the crest. The Khasi funnel
is a **converging valley**: the air is lifted *and* laterally compressed at a point, and the rainfall
is extreme and intensely local — **1,221 cm**, with **161 cm** ninety kilometres away. ⚠️ The
comparison yields a transferable principle: **a barrier distributes, a funnel concentrates**, and the
funnel's figure is four to five times the wall's because convergence is added to ascent.

**Objection.** *"If the plains current is progressively depleted, Delhi's figure should be the
lowest before Bikaner, and the gradient should be smooth. But the two printed pairs move in
opposite directions — Allahabad falls 15 cm between folios while Delhi rises 5 cm. The depletion
story is being fitted to numbers that do not support it."*

**Reply.** The objection is a good one and the right response is to **narrow the claim rather than
defend it**. What both printed pairs agree on is the **ordinal pattern**: Kolkata > Patna >
Allahabad > Delhi > Bikaner holds on **either** set of figures, since 119 > 105 > 91 > 51 > 24 and
119 > 105 > 76 > 56 > 24. **The sequence is robust; the two interior values are not.** So the
depletion claim should be made **ordinally** — *"rainfall declines monotonically from Kolkata to
Bikaner"* — and the exact interior figures should be quoted with their folio or not at all. ⚠️ And
the depletion mechanism is anyway **not the only cause**: Lesson 13 shows depression frequency
declining along the same axis, which contributes to the same gradient by a different route.

**Unresolved residual.** Two, and both stand. ⇄ **C-13:** two printed pairs for Allahabad and Delhi,
two folios apart, moving in opposite directions; no reconciliation in the chapter. ⇄ **C-20:**
Cherrapunji at 1,102 cm and at 425 inches [1,079.5 cm], neither with a base period. ⇄ And
📰 **X-02**: the 2026 East & Northeast figure printed as 1,017.1 mm and 1,016.3 mm in one official
document. **Three conflicts in one lesson, across a textbook, a world geography and an official
release — which is the lesson's real subject.**

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Calling Cherrapunji the wettest place.** **Mawsynram, at 1,221 cm, out-rains it by 119 cm**,
  from **16 km** west.
- ❌ **Transferring the daily record to Mawsynram.** **Cherrapunji's daily record is 103.6 cm**;
  Mawsynram's is **99 cm**. The higher annual total has the lower daily maximum.
- ❌ **Giving one Cherrapunji annual figure.** **C-20**: **1,102 cm** (Khullar) and **425 inches
  [1,079.5 cm]** (Leong).
- ❌ **Attributing the 905-inch record to Khullar.** The **905 inches [2,298.7 cm] in 1861** is
  **Leong's**, and no other certified source here carries it.
- ❌ **Giving one Allahabad or Delhi figure.** **C-13**: **91 / 51 cm** at folio 146; **76 / 56 cm**
  at folio 148.
- ❌ **Saying the Himalaya blocks the monsoon.** It **deflects** it westward; the plains current is
  *"progressively depleted… not blocked"* (C1 §9.1).
- ❌ **Giving the Bay branch three streams.** The **Arabian** branch has three; the **Bay** branch
  has **two**.
- ❌ **Explaining Tamil Nadu's dryness with one reason.** It is a **double** shadow: the rain shadow
  of the **Arabian** current, **and** a coastline **parallel** to the **Bay** current.
- ❌ **Treating the mid-July arrival in Kashmir as a full monsoon.** It arrives *"only as a **feeble
  current**"*.
- ❌ **Answering an Assam or Bihar flood question here.** Flood management is
  `Disaster-Management`'s (**R-04**).

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I — the spatial distribution of monsoon rainfall and the role of relief and
distance, which C1 §9.1 states causally and neither owner quantifies.

**PYQ position.** No verified demand is owned here. Two **adjacent** items are named and refused:
**2020 GS-I Q15**, on flooding in million-plus cities, and **2022 GS-III Q8**, on the mechanism and
occurrence of cloudbursts, both routed to `Disaster-Management/basic/08` under **R-04**. ⛔ No answer
letter is recorded or inferred.

**Answer use.** The funnel and the ladder are the two best **evidence blocks** in this topic, and
they work because they are **comparisons, not records**. *"Rainfall in India is a property of
position rather than of region: Mawsynram, at 1,329 m on the southern Khasi slopes, receives
1,221 cm a year and Cherrapunji, 16 km east at 1,313 m, 1,102 cm, while Guwahati, 90 km away in the
Brahmaputra valley, receives 161 cm; along the plains, the deflected Bay current declines
monotonically from Kolkata through Patna, Allahabad and Delhi to Bikaner's 24 cm as it is
progressively depleted rather than blocked."* Two clauses; a vertical contrast and a horizontal one.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **The Bay branch divides into TWO streams** (folio 146) — against the Arabian branch's three.
2. ✅ **Cherrapunji: 25°15′N, 91°44′E, 1,313 m, 1,102 cm annually**, mostly **June-August**; highest
   daily record **103.6 cm**.
3. ✅ **Mawsynram: 25°18′N, 91°35′E, 1,329 m, 16 km west, 1,221 cm**; highest daily record **99 cm**.
   **Difference 119 cm a year, computed at generation.**
4. ✅ **Both are on the southern slopes of the Khasi hills at the head of a funnel-shaped valley.**
5. ✅ **Guwahati, 90 km from Cherrapunji, receives only 161 cm** — a ratio of about **6.8 : 1**.
6. ⇄ **C-20:** Cherrapunji's annual normal is **1,102 cm** (Khullar folio 146) and **425 inches
   [1,079.5 cm]** (Leong p. 124), **about 23 cm apart, neither with a base period**; Leong alone
   records **905 inches [2,298.7 cm] in 1861**.
7. ✅ **The decline ladder (folio 146), south-west monsoon period:** **Kolkata 119 · Patna 105 ·
   Allahabad 91 · Delhi 51 · Bikaner 24 cm**.
8. ⇄ **C-13:** folio 148 prints **Allahabad 76** and **Delhi 56** for the same quantity. The two
   values move in **opposite directions**. ⚠️ **The ordinal sequence is robust on either pair; the
   interior values are not.**
9. ✅ **Advance:** Bay branch spreads rapidly over **Assam**; normal arrival at **Kolkata 7 June**;
   deflected **westward** at the Himalayan foothills.
10. ✅ **The merge:** the branches meet roughly along a line through **Agra and Ferozepur**; **by end
    June** the monsoon is established over most of the country; **by mid-July** it reaches Kashmir
    *"but only as a **feeble current**"*.
11. ✅ **C1 §9.1, the owner's best sentence, preserved:** the plains current is **"progressively
    depleted as it moves inland, not blocked"**.
12. ✅ **Tamil Nadu's double shadow:** the **rain shadow of the Arabian current** *and* a coast
    **parallel to the Bay current**. → Lesson 16.
13. 📰 **A-3, dated anchor:** **East & Northeast India 2026 = 74 % of an LPA of 1,367.3 mm**,
    *"lowest since 1901"* (IMD, **30 September 2026**). ⇄ **X-02:** the total is printed as
    **1,017.1 mm** in the highlights and **1,016.3 mm** in Table 1 of the same document; **both round
    to 74 %**. ⛔ No cause attributed; no trend claimed.
14. ⚠️ **The transferable principle:** **a barrier distributes, a funnel concentrates.** The Ghats
    give *over 250 cm* along a long front; the Khasi funnel gives **1,221 cm** at a point and
    **161 cm** ninety kilometres away.

### Concept check

**Question:** Three stations lie within a hundred kilometres of one another and record 1,221 cm,
1,102 cm and 161 cm a year. Identify them, explain the spread, and state the two source conflicts
that any answer using these figures must disclose.

**Model answer:** They are **Mawsynram (1,329 m, 1,221 cm)**, **Cherrapunji (1,313 m, 1,102 cm,
16 km east)** and **Guwahati (161 cm, 90 km away)**. The spread is explained by **position on a
slope**: the first two sit on the **southern slopes of the Khasi hills at the head of a funnel-shaped
valley**, where the moist Bay current is **laterally compressed as well as lifted**, while Guwahati
lies in the **Brahmaputra valley**, off the windward face. The two conflicts are, first, **C-20** —
Cherrapunji's annual normal is printed as **1,102 cm** by Khullar and **425 inches [1,079.5 cm]** by
Leong, **neither stating a base period** — and second, the **inversion**: Mawsynram has the higher
annual total but the **lower daily record**, **99 cm** against Cherrapunji's **103.6 cm**. *(122 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That Cherrapunji is the wettest place in India. It is the **best-known**
one. Mawsynram's annual total is higher by **119 cm**, and the two superlatives — greatest annual
total and greatest daily fall — belong to **different stations**. *(36 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 12

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** Account for the rainfall distribution produced by the
Bay of Bengal branch of the south-west monsoon.

**Model:** The branch divides into **two** streams with contrasting geometries. The **first** crosses
the Ganga-Brahmaputra delta into the **southern slopes of the Khasi hills**, where a **funnel-shaped
valley** compresses the current laterally as well as lifting it: **Mawsynram (1,329 m) 1,221 cm** and
**Cherrapunji (1,313 m, 16 km east) 1,102 cm** annually, against **Guwahati's 161 cm** only 90 km
away. ⇄ Leong prints Cherrapunji at **425 inches [1,079.5 cm]** with a record of **905 inches in
1861**; neither book states a base period. The **second** stream is **deflected westward** by the
Himalaya and travels up the Ganga plain, **"progressively depleted… not blocked"**, declining
monotonically: **Kolkata 119, Patna 105, Allahabad 91, Delhi 51, Bikaner 24 cm** — ⇄ though folio 148
prints **Allahabad 76** and **Delhi 56**. By **mid-July** the current reaches Kashmir *"only as a
feeble current"*. **A funnel concentrates; a deflector distributes and exhausts.** *(141 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** **Site specificity** — coordinates, altitudes and separations used, not just
station names **2** · **Funnel mechanism** — lateral compression distinguished from simple
orographic lifting **3** · **Decline quantification** — the full five-station ladder with the
depletion reason **3** · **Cross-source reconciliation** — C-13 and C-20 both disclosed and neither
silently resolved **2**

## Lesson 13 — The monsoon trough and the depressions that actually make the rain

Progress: 13 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **depression-track rail with monthly genesis boxes and a delta-funnel strip**

### The intuition, before any term

By now a picture has formed that is wrong in an important way. The monsoon current strikes the
Ghats; it funnels into the Khasi hills; it is deflected up the plain. All true — and all of it is
about **relief**. But look at where India's population and cropland actually are: the Gangetic plain,
central India, the Deccan. **There is no mountain there.** So what lifts the air?

The answer is that the rain over interior India is not made by the current flowing over terrain. It
is made by **weather systems** — discrete low-pressure vortices that form over the warm Bay of
Bengal, come ashore through the river deltas, and track inland along a corridor, raining as they go.
There are three or four of them a month. When they come, it rains across whole states. When they
fail to form, it does not rain, no matter how strongly the monsoon current is blowing.

The corridor they travel along is the **monsoon trough** of Lesson 6 — the displaced convergence
zone, now given a pressure field and a job.

And the 2026 season supplied a controlled demonstration of exactly this, which is why this lesson
has the sharpest live anchor in the edition. **In June 2026 not a single low-pressure area formed
over India. June rainfall was 65 per cent of normal.** In July the number of systems was exactly
normal — but they **lasted nearly twice as long**, and central India had a normal-to-excess July.
Count and duration are different variables, and 2026 separated them.

### The primary visual — the rail, the boxes and the funnels

```text
  THE MONSOON TROUGH AND THE SYSTEMS THAT RUN ALONG IT
  Sources: Khullar folios 140, 147-148; IMD 30 September 2026.

  PART A - THE TROUGH'S PRESSURE FIELD  (Khullar folio 140)

    NORTH INDIA                                       SOUTH INDIA
    below 1,000 mb  <---------------------------->  1,008-1,010 mb
         |                                                 |
         |   an ELONGATED TROUGH across the GANGA BASIN    |
         |   to the HEAD OF THE BAY OF BENGAL              |
         |                                                 |
         +--- the 1,009 mb ISOBAR crosses KERALA and ------+
              TAMIL NADU
    The pressure difference across the whole country in the rainy
    season is about 10 mb. THE INDIAN MONSOON IS A LOW-GRADIENT
    SYSTEM. Its rain is not made by the gradient; it is made by the
    SYSTEMS that form along the trough.

  *** C-28, A PRINTED UNIT ERROR ***
    Khullar Fig. 5.18 legend (folio 143) reads
    "ISOBARS (IN MILLIMETRES)".
    Isobars are contoured in MILLIBARS / hPa. Millimetres measure
    LENGTH. The folio-140 text uses mb correctly throughout, so this
    is a FIGURE-LEGEND error, not a conceptual one - and it is
    exactly the same KIND of error that IMD's own 29 May 2026 press
    release makes with "165.4 cm" for a June rainfall normal that
    is 165.3 mm (X-03, Lesson 19). TWO SOURCES, ONE CLASS OF ERROR,
```

### Correction C-30 — the taxonomy, printed as two systems and never merged

⚠️ **Khullar's "3-4 depressions per month" and IMD's modern low-pressure-system counts are not the
same quantity, and no arithmetic may be performed across them.**

| | ✅ **Khullar folio 147** | 📰 **IMD, 2026 season, 30 September 2026** |
|---|---|---|
| **Unit counted** | *"depressions"*, undifferentiated | a graded **low-pressure system (LPS)** taxonomy: Low → Well Marked Low → **Depression** → **Deep Depression** → Cyclonic Storm |
| **Count** | *"about **3-4 depressions** are formed **per month** from June to September"* | **14 low-pressure systems** in the season, including **six monsoon depressions**, of which **three** intensified into **deep depressions** |
| **Duration measure** | none | **77 LPS days** against a normal of **57** |
| **Period** | no base period stated | the **2026** season, dated |

⚠️ Four months at 3-4 a month implies **12-16 systems** on Khullar's count, and 2026 recorded **14**
low-pressure systems — a coincidence of magnitude that is **not** a verification, because the two are
counting **different objects**: Khullar's "depression" is the undifferentiated system, while IMD's
"14" includes every category down to a low, of which only **six** were depressions. ⛔ **No ratio,
difference or trend is computed across them** (**R-07**, **R-03**). Both are printed; the taxonomy
difference is the lesson.

### The dated anchor, A-5 and X-12 — the 2026 natural experiment

📰 **From the IMD *Salient Features of the 2026 Southwest Monsoon Season*, 30 September 2026, read in
full at generation:**

- **Season totals:** *"There were **14 low pressure systems**, including **six Monsoon Depressions**
  during the season, out of that **three intensified in to Deep Depression**. There were **77 Low
  Pressure System Days** against its normal **57** days."*
- **June 2026:** *"Climatologically, June witnesses an average of **three low-pressure systems
  (LPSs)**, accounting for about **11 LPS days**. However, **no low-pressure area (LPA) formed during
  June 2026**, representing a significant departure from the climatological average and
  **contributing to the large rainfall deficiency during the month**."* June rainfall was **65 % of
  LPA**.
- **July 2026:** **four** low-pressure systems, *"equal to the climatological average of four
  systems"* — one intensifying into a **Deep Depression during 26 July-1 August** and another into a
  **Depression during 5-7 July** — but *"although the number of low-pressure systems was near normal,
  their **persistence was significantly higher** than the climatological average, with **24 LPS days
  recorded against the normal of 13.56 days**"*, and *"the prolonged persistence of these systems,
  particularly over Central India, led to enhanced rainfall activity over the region and contributed
  to **normal to excess rainfall over many parts of Central India**"*. July rainfall was **101 % of
  LPA**.
- **August 2026:** **seven** LPSs; the month opened with the weakening of a depression formed the
  previous month over south-east Rajasthan; six developed over the Bay of Bengal and adjoining areas,
  **two intensifying into depressions** and moving **west-north-westwards** across central and
  northern India.
- **September 2026:** **two deep depressions** over the north Bay of Bengal, **21-27 September** and
  **28-30 September**, plus a low-pressure area over the north Bay **10-17 September**.

> ⚠️ **Why this is the strongest evidence block in the edition.** C2 asserts that depressions make a
> major part of the rain. The 2026 season supplies the **two complementary tests** of that assertion,
> both printed by IMD and both attributed by IMD itself to the systems: **June had no system and the
> rain failed**; **July had a normal number of systems that lasted nearly twice as long, and central
> India did well.** ⚠️ The analytical point to carry is that **the operative variable is
> system-days, not system-count** — which is exactly the distinction Khullar's "3-4 per month" cannot
> express. ⛔ No trend is claimed from a single season (**R-03**), and no cause beyond IMD's own
> stated attribution is asserted.

### The causal chain, with its assumptions and its limits

```text
   The displaced convergence zone lies across north India as the
   MONSOON TROUGH (Lesson 6)
        |
        v
   It is a low-gradient feature: below 1,000 mb in the north,
   1,008-1,010 mb in the south, the 1,009 mb isobar over Kerala and
   Tamil Nadu  (folio 140)
        |
        v
   Over the WARM BAY OF BENGAL, at the eastern end of the trough,
   vortices form - "3-4 per month, June to September"
        |
        v
   They are "SUCKED INWARD THROUGH THE DELTAS of the great rivers" -
   GANGA, MAHANADI, GODAVARI, KRISHNA, CAUVERY
        |
        v
   They track ALONG the trough, WEST-NORTH-WESTWARDS, and MERGE WITH
   THE SEASONAL LOW OVER NORTH-WEST INDIA
        |
        v
   Each system lifts air over a wide area -> widespread rain over
   terrain with NO RELIEF. Yields of "AS MUCH AS 60 CM IN A SINGLE
   DAY".
        |
        v
   SO: INTERIOR INDIA'S RAINFALL IS A FUNCTION OF SYSTEM FREQUENCY
   AND PERSISTENCE, NOT OF CURRENT STRENGTH.
        |
        v
   FAILURE MODE, stated by the book: "their ABSENCE or a CHANGE IN
   THEIR TRACKS results in DEFICIT OR NO RAIN"
        |
        v
   2026 TESTED BOTH LIMBS:
     JUNE - NO system formed    -> 65 % of LPA
     JULY - normal COUNT but    -> 101 % of LPA, central India
            24 LPS days vs 13.56   normal to excess

  ASSUMPTION: that the depression is the rain-bearing unit for
  interior India. Supported by Khullar's statement, by the 2026
  June and July records, and by the physical requirement that
  something must lift air over a plain.
  LIMIT 1: Khullar's counts have NO BASE PERIOD and use a taxonomy
  superseded by IMD's graded LPS classification (C-30).
  LIMIT 2: ONE SEASON IS NOT A CLIMATOLOGY. June 2026 is a
  demonstration, not a proof, and R-03 forbids any trend claim.
  LIMIT 3: the same IMD document reports WESTERN DISTURBANCE spells
  in every month of the 2026 monsoon, including SEVEN in August.
  THOSE ARE NOT MONSOON SYSTEMS and are routed to
  Geography/advanced/13 under R-06. They are named here only so that
  the LPS counts are not confused with them.
```

### The India hook, with its limitation printed beside it

⚠️ **Most of central and north India's monsoon rain is delivered by Bay of Bengal depressions
entering through the deltas, not by uplift over the Ghats.** Khullar folio 146 states it as a
principle — *"A major part of the monsoon rainfall is generated by depressions originating in the
Arabian Sea but **more so in the Bay of Bengal**. Some depressions develop over land also"* — and
folio 147 supplies the climatology: **3-4 per month**, entering through the **Ganga, Mahanadi,
Godavari, Krishna and Cauvery** deltas, tracking along the trough, yielding **as much as 60 cm in a
single day**.

> ⛔ **Limitation that must be printed with it.** **The "3-4 per month" figure predates IMD's current
> low-pressure-system taxonomy** (**C-30**), carries **no base period**, and counts an
> undifferentiated "depression" where IMD now distinguishes **Low, Well Marked Low, Depression, Deep
> Depression and Cyclonic Storm**. 2026 recorded **14 LPS including six monsoon depressions**, which
> is **not** a comparable number. ⛔ **No arithmetic is performed across the two taxonomies.**

### Comparison, objection, reply and the unresolved residual

**Comparison — the two rain-making regimes of the Indian monsoon.** The **orographic** regime
(Lessons 11-12) is **continuous while the current blows**, spatially fixed by terrain, and extremely
intense in small areas: 250-500 cm on a windward slope, 1,221 cm in a funnel. The **synoptic** regime
is **episodic**, spatially mobile, and moderate but widespread: 3-4 systems a month, each raining
over whole states, with occasional 60 cm days. ⚠️ The consequence for an answer is that **the two
regimes fail differently**: the orographic regime fails when the current weakens, the synoptic regime
fails when systems do not form — and **2026 shows that a season can have an adequate current and
still fail in June for want of a single low**.

**Objection.** *"The trough's pressure field is trivial — about ten millibars across the whole
country. A gradient that weak cannot drive a monsoon, so the pressure-based account of the monsoon is
overstated."*

**Reply.** The objection is correct and is the lesson's own point. Khullar's folio-140 numbers —
**below 1,000 mb** in north India, **1,008-1,010 mb** in the south, the **1,009 mb isobar** across
Kerala and Tamil Nadu — do describe a **low-gradient** field, and a mid-latitude depression can show
a larger gradient across a few hundred kilometres. ⚠️ The resolution is that the trough's function is
**not** to drive the wind by its gradient; it is to be a **locus of convergence** along which
vortices form and travel. The energy is supplied by **latent heat release** over a warm ocean, which
is exactly what Raman and Ramanathan described at Lesson 9. **The monsoon is a moist-convective
system organised along a weak trough, not a pressure-gradient-driven flow** — and C2's own trap line,
*"monsoon rainfall is not only orographic"*, points at the same conclusion from the other side.

**Unresolved residual.** ⇄ **C-30 is not closable**: Khullar's undated, undifferentiated "3-4 per
month" cannot be reconciled with IMD's graded taxonomy, and this edition prints both without a
bridge. ⚠️ A second residual: Khullar states that depressions originate in the Arabian Sea *"but
more so in the Bay of Bengal"* and gives the Arabian Sea a **June zone of 17°-20° N** — but gives
**no Arabian Sea count** at all, for any month. **The asymmetry is asserted and never quantified.**

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Attributing interior rainfall to orographic uplift.** C2's own trap line and Khullar folio 146
  both say otherwise; the **depressions** do the inland work.
- ❌ **Merging Khullar's "3-4 per month" with IMD's "14 LPS".** **C-30**: different taxonomies,
  different objects, **no arithmetic across them**.
- ❌ **Counting systems instead of system-days.** 2026: **July had a normal count and 24 LPS days
  against a normal of 13.56**, and that is what produced the rain.
- ❌ **Writing "isobars in millimetres".** **C-28**: Khullar's **Fig. 5.18 legend** prints it; isobars
  are in **millibars / hPa**, and his own folio-140 text uses mb correctly.
- ❌ **Giving one genesis zone for the whole season.** **June 16°-21° N, west of 92° E** · **July-August
  north of 18° N, west of 90° E** · **September north of 15° N, west of 90° E**.
- ❌ **Omitting the deltas.** *"Almost all of them are sucked inward through the deltas"* of the
  **Ganga, Mahanadi, Godavari, Krishna and Cauvery**.
- ❌ **Treating a monsoon depression as a cyclone.** Structure, intensity scales and landfall
  warnings are `Geography/basic/13`'s; **cyclone and flood management** is `Disaster-Management`'s
  (**R-04**).
- ❌ **Importing the 2026 western-disturbance spells into a monsoon answer.** They are reported in
  the same IMD document and are **not monsoon systems** (**R-06**).
- ❌ **Claiming a trend from June 2026.** **R-03.** It is one month of one season, with IMD's own
  attribution quoted and nothing added.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I — the rain-generating mechanism of the Indian monsoon over the interior, which
C2 §2 asserts without evidence.

**PYQ position.** No verified demand is owned here. **2022 GS-I Q5**, on IMD colour-coded weather
warnings for cyclone-prone areas, is **adjacent and refused**, routed to
`Disaster-Management/basic/07` under **R-04**. ⛔ No answer letter is recorded or inferred. This
lesson supplies the evidence that converts C2's bare assertion into an argued claim, which is
precisely what a 15-mark *"monsoon variability and its consequences"* answer needs.

**Answer use.** Make the **frequency-and-persistence** point explicitly; it is the strongest single
move available on monsoon variability. *"Interior India's monsoon rain is delivered episodically by
low-pressure systems forming over the Bay of Bengal — Khullar records three to four a month from
June to September, entering through the Ganga, Mahanadi, Godavari, Krishna and Cauvery deltas and
tracking west-north-westwards along the monsoon trough, with falls of as much as 60 cm in a day —
so that their absence or a change in their tracks produces deficit regardless of the current's
strength: in June 2026 no low-pressure area formed at all and the month closed at 65 per cent of
normal, while July 2026 had a normal count of four systems but 24 system-days against a normal of
13.56 and closed at 101 per cent (IMD, 30 September 2026)."*

### Revision points

1. ✅ **The trough's pressure field (folio 140):** an elongated trough **across the Ganga basin to
   the head of the Bay**; **north India below 1,000 mb**, rising southwards to **1,008-1,010 mb**,
   with the **1,009 mb isobar crossing Kerala and Tamil Nadu**.
2. ✗ **C-28, unit error:** Khullar **Fig. 5.18 legend (folio 143)** reads *"**ISOBARS (IN
   MILLIMETRES)**"*. Isobars are contoured in **millibars / hPa**; the folio-140 text uses mb
   correctly.
3. ✅ **The principle (folio 146):** *"A major part of the monsoon rainfall is generated by
   depressions originating in the Arabian Sea but **more so in the Bay of Bengal**. Some depressions
   develop over land also."*
4. ✅ **Climatology (folio 147):** *"about **3-4 depressions** are formed per month from **June to
   September**"*.
5. ✅ **Landfall:** *"almost all of them are **sucked inward through the deltas** of the great
   rivers, the **Ganga, the Mahanadi, the Godavari, the Krishna and the Cauvery**"*.
6. ✅ **Monthly genesis zones:** **June — 16°-21° N, west of 92° E**, mostly moving north-west ·
   **July-August — north of 18° N, west of 90° E**, moving **west-north-westwards** · **September —
   north of 15° N, west of 90° E**. **Arabian Sea June zone: 17°-20° N.**
7. ✅ **Track and fate:** the majority move **along the monsoon trough** and **merge with the
   seasonal low over north-west India**; **Fig. 5.21** is *Normal dates of withdrawal of Monsoon*.
8. ✅ **Yield and failure (folio 148):** *"as much as **60 cm in a single day**"*; *"their **absence**
   or a **change in their tracks** results in **deficit or no rain**"*; storms in August-September
   are rare and form close to the **Maharashtra-Gujarat coast**.
9. 📰 **A-5, 2026 season:** **14 low-pressure systems**, including **six monsoon depressions**, of
   which **three** became **deep depressions**; **77 LPS days against a normal of 57**.
10. 📰 **X-12, the natural experiment:** **June 2026 — no low-pressure area formed at all**, against a
    climatological **three systems and about 11 LPS days**; June closed at **65 % of LPA**.
    **July 2026 — four systems, equal to climatology, but 24 LPS days against a normal of 13.56**,
    with *"prolonged persistence… particularly over Central India"* and **normal to excess rainfall
    over many parts of Central India**; July closed at **101 % of LPA**.
11. ✗ **C-30:** Khullar's undated, undifferentiated *"depressions"* are **not** IMD's graded
    **Low / Well Marked Low / Depression / Deep Depression / Cyclonic Storm**. ⛔ **No arithmetic
    across the two taxonomies, and no trend claimed.**

### Concept check

**Question:** The monsoon current was blowing normally over India through June 2026, yet the month
closed at 65 per cent of its long-period average. Explain, and state the general principle the case
illustrates.

**Model answer:** Because **the current does not make the rain over interior India; the systems do**.
Khullar records that *"a major part of the monsoon rainfall is generated by depressions… more so in
the Bay of Bengal"*, at **three to four a month from June to September**, entering through the
**Ganga, Mahanadi, Godavari, Krishna and Cauvery** deltas and tracking along the monsoon trough —
and that *"their **absence** or a change in their tracks results in deficit or no rain"*. IMD records
that in **June 2026 no low-pressure area formed at all**, against a climatological **three systems
and about 11 LPS days**, and attributes the month's **65 % of LPA** partly to that absence. The
principle: over a plain with no relief, **rainfall is a function of synoptic-system frequency and
persistence**, not of current strength — which is why July 2026, with a normal count but **24 system
days against 13.56**, closed at **101 %**. *(149 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That a strong monsoon current guarantees rain. It guarantees **moisture
supply**; rain requires **lifting**, and over the interior the lifting is supplied by discrete
low-pressure systems whose formation is not determined by the current's strength. *(35 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 13

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** "The monsoon trough, not the monsoon current, determines
where it rains in interior India." Examine.

**Model:** Substantially correct, with one refinement. The trough is a
**low-gradient** feature — Khullar prints **below 1,000 mb** in the north against **1,008-1,010 mb**
in the south, the **1,009 mb isobar** crossing Kerala and Tamil Nadu — so it cannot drive rain by its
gradient. It is instead a **locus** along which low-pressure systems form and travel:
**three to four a month, June to September**, *"sucked inward through the deltas"* of the **Ganga,
Mahanadi, Godavari, Krishna and Cauvery**, tracking **west-north-westwards** and merging with the
seasonal low over north-west India, with falls of **as much as 60 cm in a single day**. *"Their
absence or a change in their tracks results in deficit or no rain."* **June 2026** proves it:
**no low-pressure area formed**, and the month closed at **65 % of LPA**. The operative variable is
**system-days** — July 2026 had a **normal count** but **24 days against
13.56**, and closed at **101 %**. *(148 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** **Genesis-zone accuracy** — monthly genesis boxes and the delta entries used
precisely **2** · **Track reasoning** — the trough treated as a locus of formation and travel rather
than as a driving gradient **3** · **Taxonomy currency** — Khullar's counts and IMD's LPS categories
both given, with no arithmetic across them **2** · **Rainfall attribution** — the 2026 June and July
contrast used to separate count from persistence **3**

Progress: 14 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **three-column adjudication matrix, source against source against criterion**

### The intuition, before any term

A break is the monsoon's most frightening behaviour, and the reason is agricultural rather than
meteorological. A season can deliver its full seasonal total and still destroy a crop, if the rain
stops for a fortnight at the wrong moment. **Timing beats quantity**, and the break is where timing
lives.

Physically, a break is simple to picture. The monsoon trough, which normally lies across the plains,
**shifts north to the foot of the Himalaya**. The plains, left on the dry side of it, stop raining.
The foothills, now directly under it, are deluged. So a break is not an absence of monsoon — it is a
**relocation** of it, and the same event that parches Uttar Pradesh floods the sub-Himalayan belt.

What makes this the hardest lesson in the topic is that **four different sources give four different
accounts of the same phenomenon, and two of them are the same author on two folios**. One says a
break lasts about a week; one says three to five days over 500 to 1,000 kilometres; one supplies a
modern objective threshold; and the book contradicts itself about what causes it.

So this lesson is an adjudication. It prints all four, it says which half of the owner's claim is
defensible and which is refused outright, and it leaves unresolved what the sources leave unresolved.

### Correction C-11, split into its defensible and its refused halves

> ✅ **The duration half is defensible.** C2's *"at least 3-5 days"* is consistent with the objective
> criterion's **≥ 3 consecutive days**, and the phrase *"at least"* correctly marks it as a
> **threshold** rather than a typical value. Khullar's *"about a week"* is a **normal**, and the two
> are not in conflict: a distribution with a 3-day floor can easily have a 7-day mean. **Teach all
> three statistics together** — floor, normal and rare maximum — and the apparent conflict dissolves.

> ✗ **The spatial half is refused.** *"Over 500-1000 km"* appears in **no certified book span** and
> in **no official source retrieved on 2026-10-04 or at generation**. Khullar's folio 146 describes
> the **region** where rain ceases — *"over the country outside the **Himalayan belt and southeast
> peninsula**"* — and gives **no length figure at all**. The modern criterion is defined over the
> **core monsoon zone**, a **fixed region**, not a variable length. ⛔ **The figure is not taught as
> payload, not repeated as a plausible approximation, and not softened into "a few hundred
> kilometres".** It is named, located in the owner, and refused (**R-07**).

### Correction C-14 — one author, two causes, two folios apart

⇄ **Khullar gives two incompatible causes for the break within one chapter.**

| Folio | Printed cause | What it implies |
|---|---|---|
| **146** | *"The breaks are believed to be brought about by the **collapse of the Tibetan High**. This results in the northward shifting of the **monsoon trough**. The axis of the trough lies at the **foothills of the Himalayas** during the break period"* | an **upper-air, remote** cause acting through the Lesson-8 heat engine, with the trough's displacement as the **consequence** |
| **148 (iv)** | rain comes in wet spells separated by rainless intervals called *breaks*, which *"are **related to tropical cyclones which originate in the Bay of Bengal**"* | a **lower-level, local, synoptic** cause tied to the Lesson-13 systems |

⚠️ **These are not two aspects of one explanation.** One locates the cause in the collapse of an
upper-tropospheric anticyclone over Tibet; the other locates it in the behaviour of Bay systems. The
first makes the trough's northward shift a **result**; the second makes system behaviour the
operative variable. ⚠️ A charitable reconstruction is available — a weakened Tibetan High could alter
the environment in which Bay systems form — but **Khullar does not make it**, and this edition will
not make it for him. **Both are printed; neither is adopted.**

### The technical content, as printed

- ✅ **The passage, folio 146:** *"During the rainy season, particularly in **July and August**,
  there are certain periods when the monsoons become weak… rainfall practically ceases over the
  country **outside the Himalayan belt and southeast peninsula**… The latest studies have revealed
  that the breaks are likely to occur **more frequently during the second week of August**. The
  **normal duration of the break is about a week** but on some occasions this could be longer. The
  **longest breaks have been known to persist for two to three weeks**, but such occasions are rare.
  The breaks are believed to be brought about by the **collapse of the Tibetan High**. This results
  in the **northward shifting of the monsoon trough**. The axis of the trough lies at the **foothills
  of the Himalayas** during the break period… **heavy rainfall occurs over the sub-Himalayan regions
  and the southern slopes of the Himalayas**… On an average **one or two breaks** do occur during the
  rainy season. Statistical studies of the monsoon show that **in 85 out of 100 years there is a
  break in the monsoons**."*
- 📰 **The modern criterion, reported and marked as such (U-05).** A **break** over the core monsoon
  zone is identified when the **standardized rainfall anomaly** averaged over that zone falls
  **below −1.0 for at least three consecutive days**; an **active** spell is identified when it
  **exceeds +1.0** for at least three consecutive days. ⛔ **This is search-mediated at generation to
  the Rajeevan active/break literature and a CLIVAR monsoon-research document. The IMD Pune
  operational page failed at transport level on 2026-10-04 and again at generation.** It is
  therefore **reported, not certified**.
- 📰 **What *is* certified, and it matters.** The IMD *Salient Features of the 2026 Southwest Monsoon
  Season*, **30 September 2026**, read in full at generation, uses exactly this apparatus:
  *"The **standardized rainfall anomalies over the core monsoon zone** region for 2026 are shown in
  Fig. 9"*, and describes 2026 as showing *"considerable temporal variability, with **alternating wet
  and dry spells** rather than a sustained rainfall regime"* — with *"several days recording positive
  anomalies, including values **exceeding +1**"* in early July, *"another **dry spell** during
  mid-July, with anomalies **falling below −1**"*, intermittent dry spells in the third and fourth
  weeks of August, and a marked late-September wet spell *"reaching **above +4**"*. ⚠️ **The method
  and the ±1 thresholds are therefore certified from a primary IMD document; only the
  three-consecutive-day duration rule is not.**
- ✅ **C2's own gap-closer supplies the intra-seasonal mechanism.** C2's **Madden-Julian Oscillation**
  block identifies the MJO as an **eastward-moving pulse of tropical cloudiness, convection and
  rainfall**, with a convective phase over the Indian Ocean favouring **active** spells and a
  suppressed phase supporting **breaks**; and its own trap line states the timescale discipline —
  *"MJO is **intra-seasonal** variability; ENSO is **inter-annual**. Do not treat them as the same
  timescale."* ⚠️ C2 marks this material as standard geography rather than book payload, and this
  edition keeps that marking. ↪ The **ocean mechanics** of the oscillation are
  `Geography/advanced/12`'s.
- ✅ **C1's agricultural formulation, which is the best sentence in either owner on this topic and is
  preserved:** *"a break that coincides with a crop's critical growth stage causes damage that the
  same rainfall deficit spread evenly would not. Agricultural risk is therefore about **timing**."*
  ↪ The agricultural consequence itself routes to `Geography/basic/30` (**R-05**); the **climatic**
  statement is Topic 16's.

### The causal chain — and the honest admission inside it

```text
   ACTIVE SPELL
   The monsoon trough lies over the PLAINS; low-pressure systems form
   over the Bay and track inland (Lesson 13) -> widespread rain
        |
        v
   SOMETHING CHANGES
        |
        +--- ACCOUNT 1 (Khullar folio 146): the TIBETAN HIGH
        |    COLLAPSES -> the trough shifts NORTH
        |
        +--- ACCOUNT 2 (Khullar folio 148 iv): the behaviour of
        |    TROPICAL CYCLONES ORIGINATING IN THE BAY changes
        |
        +--- ACCOUNT 3 (C2's gap-closer, marked as standard):
             the MJO's SUPPRESSED PHASE moves over the region
        |
        v   *** THE THREE ARE NOT RECONCILED BY ANY CERTIFIED
            SOURCE. THIS EDITION PRINTS ALL THREE. ***
        |
        v
   THE TROUGH'S AXIS LIES AT THE FOOTHILLS OF THE HIMALAYAS
        |
        +-------------------------+
        |                         |
        v                         v
   THE PLAINS GO DRY         THE SUB-HIMALAYAN REGIONS AND THE
   "rainfall practically     SOUTHERN SLOPES OF THE HIMALAYAS GET
   ceases over the country   HEAVY RAINFALL
   outside the Himalayan          |
   belt and southeast             v
   peninsula"                A BREAK IS A RELOCATION, NOT AN
        |                    ABSENCE. The same event that parches
        v                    the plains floods the foothills.
   DURATION
     floor   : >= 3 consecutive days (reported criterion, U-05)
     normal  : "about a week"        (Khullar f.146)
     rare max: "two to three weeks"  (Khullar f.146)
   FREQUENCY
     "one or two" per season; "in 85 out of 100 years there is a
     break in the monsoons"  -> so a BREAK-FREE SEASON occurs in
     roughly 15 years in 100. THE BREAK IS THE NORM, NOT THE
     ANOMALY.
   EXTENT
     *** REFUSED: no source supports "500-1000 km" (C-11). ***

  LIMIT, STATED PLAINLY: this edition can tell you WHEN a break
  happens, HOW LONG it normally lasts, HOW OFTEN it happens, WHERE
  the rain goes instead, and HOW IT IS DETECTED. It cannot tell you
  WHAT CAUSES IT, because the controlling book gives two
  incompatible answers and nothing in the certified corpus decides
  between them.
```

### The India hook, with its limitation printed beside it

⚠️ **A break in the monsoon floods the Himalayan foothills while the plains go dry.** Khullar folio
146: during a break the trough's axis lies at the **foothills of the Himalayas**, and *"heavy
rainfall occurs over the **sub-Himalayan regions and the southern slopes of the Himalayas**"* while
rainfall *"practically ceases over the country **outside the Himalayan belt and southeast
peninsula**"*. ⚠️ And the frequency figure should be read carefully: **in 85 of 100 years there is a
break** — so a break-free season happens about **15 times in a century**, which makes the break a
**normal feature of the season**, not an aberration.

> ⛔ **Limitation that must be printed with it.** **The book gives two incompatible causes for the
> same phenomenon, two folios apart** (**C-14**) — the collapse of the Tibetan High at folio 146 and
> Bay-origin tropical cyclones at folio 148 — and **neither this edition nor any certified source
> adjudicates**. The **duration rule is reported, not certified** (**U-05**), and the owner's
> **spatial figure is refused outright** (**C-11**).

### Comparison, objection, reply and the unresolved residual

**Comparison — break, withdrawal and drought are three different objects.** C2's must-know list and
trap list both insist on the first distinction, correctly: *"Break monsoon is **not** withdrawal."*
Set out the three together and the difference is structural: a **break** is an **intra-seasonal
relocation** of the trough, lasting days to weeks, with the monsoon still established and the
foothills still raining; a **withdrawal** (Lesson 15) is the **end** of the season, progressive,
irreversible within the year, and takes about three months; a **drought** is a **seasonal-total
outcome**, not a circulation state at all, and its management is `Disaster-Management`'s. ⚠️ A season
can have two breaks and no drought, or a drought with no unusual break.

**Objection.** *"Refusing the 500-1000 km figure is excessive caution. It is a plausible order of
magnitude, it appears in teaching materials, and refusing it leaves the answer with no spatial
statement at all."*

**Reply.** The refusal is the point, and the answer is not left empty. **What a source has not
printed, an answer may not cite** — and this figure appears in no book span and no official endpoint
retrieved on 2026-10-04 or at generation. More importantly, **a better spatial statement is
available and is certified**: the break's extent is defined not by a length but by a **region** —
Khullar's *"outside the Himalayan belt and southeast peninsula"*, and the modern criterion's **core
monsoon zone**, which the IMD 2026 document uses explicitly. **A region is a stronger spatial
statement than a length**, because a length begs the question *along which axis?* So the refusal
costs nothing and gains precision. ⚠️ A candidate who writes *"the break is defined over the core
monsoon zone, where rainfall practically ceases outside the Himalayan belt and the south-east
peninsula"* has said more, and more defensibly, than one who writes *"500-1000 km"*.

**Unresolved residual.** Three, all live. ⇄ **C-14:** two causes, one author, two folios, no
reconciliation. ⛔ **U-05:** the ≥3-consecutive-day rule is **reported**; the IMD Pune page failed at
transport level on 2026-10-04 and again at generation, and the IMD end-of-season document certifies
the **method and the ±1 thresholds** but **not the duration rule**. ✗ **C-11:** the 500-1000 km
figure is **refused and remains unsourced**.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Repeating "500-1000 km".** **C-11.** It is in **no** certified source. Give the **region**
  instead.
- ❌ **Treating "3-5 days" and "about a week" as a contradiction.** A **threshold**, a **normal** and
  a **rare maximum** are three statistics of one distribution: **≥ 3 days**, **about a week**, **two
  to three weeks**.
- ❌ **Giving one cause for the break.** **C-14**: **collapse of the Tibetan High** (folio 146)
  *against* **Bay-origin tropical cyclones** (folio 148).
- ❌ **Saying it stops raining during a break.** It **relocates**: *"heavy rainfall occurs over the
  sub-Himalayan regions and the southern slopes of the Himalayas"*.
- ❌ **Confusing break with withdrawal.** C2's own must-know line says it directly, and Lesson 15
  measures the difference.
- ❌ **Confusing the two senses of "break".** Leong's *"the rains break"* and Khullar's ***burst***
  mean **onset** (Lesson 10). **This** break is a mid-season dry spell.
- ❌ **Treating a break as rare.** *"In **85 out of 100 years** there is a break"*, with **one or
  two** per season.
- ❌ **Citing the ≥3-day criterion as IMD-certified.** **U-05**: reported, not certified. The IMD
  **method** — core-zone standardized anomalies at **±1** — **is** certified from the 2026
  end-of-season document.
- ❌ **Merging MJO with ENSO.** C2's own trap: **MJO is intra-seasonal; ENSO is inter-annual.**
- ❌ **Writing drought management or crop-contingency planning.** **R-04** and **R-05**.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I — intra-seasonal variability of the monsoon, which C2 §3 asserts with a figure it
cannot support and C2's own must-know line states correctly.

**PYQ position.** No verified demand is owned here. **2025 Prelims Q64**, on wet-bulb temperature
crossing 35 °C, is **adjacent and refused**, routed to `Disaster-Management/basic/09` under **R-04**.
⛔ No answer letter is recorded or inferred. This lesson is the core of any *"monsoon variability"*
answer, and the **timing-over-quantity** argument is where the marks are.

**Answer use.** State the **three durations**, the **region**, the **frequency** and the **two
causes** — and refuse the length. *"A break is an intra-seasonal relocation of the monsoon trough to
the Himalayan foothills, so that rainfall practically ceases outside the Himalayan belt and the
south-east peninsula while the sub-Himalayan slopes are deluged. It is detected over the core
monsoon zone by a standardized rainfall anomaly below −1 sustained for at least three consecutive
days; its normal duration is about a week, with rare maxima of two to three weeks; one or two occur
per season, and in 85 of 100 years a break occurs. Khullar attributes it at folio 146 to the collapse
of the Tibetan High and at folio 148 to Bay-origin tropical cyclones, and does not reconcile the
two."*

### Revision points

1. ✅ **When:** *"during the rainy season, particularly in **July and August**"*, and *"more
   frequently during the **second week of August**"*.
2. ✅ **Where the rain stops:** *"rainfall practically ceases over the country **outside the
   Himalayan belt and southeast peninsula**"*.
3. ✅ **Where the rain goes:** the trough's axis lies at the **foothills of the Himalayas**, and
   *"heavy rainfall occurs over the **sub-Himalayan regions and the southern slopes of the
   Himalayas**"*. **A break is a relocation, not an absence.**
4. ✅ **Normal duration: "about a week"**; **longest two to three weeks**, *"but such occasions are
   rare"*.
5. 📰 **Reported objective criterion (U-05):** standardized rainfall anomaly over the **core monsoon
   zone** **below −1.0 for ≥ 3 consecutive days** = break; **above +1.0 for ≥ 3 consecutive days** =
   active. **Search-mediated at generation; the IMD Pune page failed at transport level.**
6. 📰 **Certified at generation:** the IMD end-of-season document of **30 September 2026** uses
   *"standardized rainfall anomalies over the core monsoon zone"* with explicit **+1** and **−1**
   thresholds, describing 2026 as *"alternating wet and dry spells"* with anomalies **above +1** in
   early July, **below −1** in mid-July, dry spells in the third and fourth weeks of August, and a
   late-September wet spell **above +4**. **The method is certified; the duration rule is not.**
7. ✅ **Frequency:** *"on an average **one or two** breaks"* per season; *"in **85 out of 100 years**
   there is a break in the monsoons"* — a break-free season occurs about **15 times in a century**.
8. ⇄ **C-14, two causes:** folio 146, *"the **collapse of the Tibetan High**"*, with the trough's
   northward shift as the result; folio 148 (iv), breaks *"are **related to tropical cyclones which
   originate in the Bay of Bengal**"*. **Neither adopted.**
9. ✗ **C-11, refused:** *"over **500-1000 km** length"* appears in **no certified book span** and in
   **no official source** retrieved on 2026-10-04 or at generation. ✅ The **duration** half,
   *"at least 3-5 days"*, is **defensible** as a threshold.
10. ✅ **C2's own must-know line, preserved as the better formulation:** breaks are intra-seasonal
    dry spells associated with monsoon-trough and convection shifts, and *"**duration and spatial
    extent vary**"*.
11. ⚠️ **MJO (C2's gap-closer, marked standard):** an **eastward-moving pulse** of tropical
    cloudiness and convection; the Indian Ocean convective phase favours **active** spells, the
    suppressed phase supports **breaks**. ⚠️ **MJO is intra-seasonal; ENSO is inter-annual.**
12. ⚠️ **C1's agricultural formulation, preserved:** a break at a crop's critical growth stage does
    damage that the same deficit spread evenly would not — **risk is about timing**. ↪ The
    agricultural consequence is `Geography/basic/30`'s.
13. ⚠️ **Three distinct objects:** **break** = intra-seasonal relocation, days to weeks;
    **withdrawal** = the season's end, about three months (Lesson 15); **drought** = a seasonal-total
    outcome, managed by `Disaster-Management`.

### Concept check

**Question:** An owner file states that breaks last "at least 3-5 days over 500-1000 km". Assess each
half of that claim, and state what should replace the part that fails.

**Model answer:** **The duration half is defensible.** *"At least 3-5 days"* reads as a
**threshold**, and the modern objective criterion identifies a break when the **standardized rainfall
anomaly over the core monsoon zone falls below −1.0 for at least three consecutive days** — though
that duration rule is **reported, not certified**, because the IMD operational page could not be
reached. It sits consistently beside Khullar's **normal duration of "about a week"** and his rare
maximum of **two to three weeks**: a floor, a mean and a tail. **The spatial half fails.**
*"500-1000 km"* appears in **no certified book span** and **no official source**; Khullar gives no
length at all. It should be replaced by a **region**: rainfall ceases *"outside the Himalayan belt
and southeast peninsula"*, and the criterion is defined over the **core monsoon zone**. *(132 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That a minimum threshold and a typical duration are rival figures. They
describe different features of one distribution, and an answer that gives the floor, the normal and
the rare maximum together is more precise than one that picks a single number. *(42 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 14

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** "A break in the monsoon is a relocation rather than an
interruption." Examine, and comment on the state of the evidence.

**Model:** The formulation is exact. During a break the **monsoon trough shifts north**, its axis
lying at the **foothills of the Himalayas**, so that *"rainfall practically ceases over the country
outside the Himalayan belt and southeast peninsula"* while *"heavy rainfall occurs over the
sub-Himalayan regions and the southern slopes of the Himalayas"*. The monsoon has moved, not
stopped. It is **normal**: *"one or two"* per season, and *"in **85 out of 100 years**
there is a break"*. Its **normal duration is about a week**, rarely **two to three weeks**; the
modern criterion detects a core-monsoon-zone standardized anomaly **below −1 for at least three
consecutive days** — a rule **reported, not certified here**, though IMD's 2026 analysis uses the
same **±1** apparatus. Evidence is weakest on **cause**: Khullar attributes it to the **collapse
of the Tibetan High** at folio 146 and to **Bay-origin tropical cyclones** at folio 148, without
reconciling them. *(148 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** **Definition triangulation** — book, owner and modern criterion set against
one another rather than merged **3** · **Duration evidence** — floor, normal and rare maximum given
as three statistics of one distribution **2** · **Causal adjudication** — both printed causes given
and neither adopted **3** · **Refusal discipline** — the 500-1000 km figure refused and replaced by
a region, and U-05 disclosed **2**
