
Progress: 15 / 20 · Stage: Core · Primary visual grammar: **advance-and-withdrawal mirror map, with durations measured on one scale**

### The intuition, before any term

Here is a question that sounds trivial and is not. The monsoon arrives in Kerala first and reaches
Punjab last. **So where does it leave from first?**

The intuitive answer — *it leaves in the order it arrived, so Kerala first* — is wrong, and it is
wrong in a way worth understanding, because the right answer is a general property of systems that
advance by occupying territory. The monsoon reaches the north-west **last**, by which time it is
already a *"feeble current"* that has shed most of its moisture over 1,500 kilometres of travel.
That tenuous northern extremity is the **first thing to fail** when the forcing weakens. Meanwhile
the south-eastern coast, which the south-west monsoon barely served at all, is only beginning its own
rainy season from the reversed flow.

So the rule is: **last in, first out.** Punjab gets about two and a half months of monsoon. The
Coromandel coast gets more than six.

And this is the one place in the topic where a standard textbook states a fact **backwards**. Leong's
Chapter 16 says the opposite — in a single clear sentence — and Khullar's Chapter 5 states the
correct rule explicitly, with durations attached, so the correction is **cross-book adjudicable**
rather than a matter of opinion. That is unusual and valuable, and it is why this lesson exists.

### The primary visual — the mirror map

```text
  M-7  ADVANCE AND WITHDRAWAL, MIRRORED, WITH DURATIONS ON ONE SCALE
  Sources: Khullar folios 144 (Fig. 5.19), 147 (Fig. 5.21), 148;
  current normals from IMD, retrieved 2026-10-04.

   ADVANCE (Fig. 5.19)              WITHDRAWAL (Fig. 5.21)
   isochrones, as printed           isochrones, as printed
   -------------------------        -------------------------
   25 MAY  ...... Andaman           1 SEPT  ... NW extremity  <-- C-24
    1 JUNE ...... Kerala            15 SEPT                       superseded
    5 JUNE                           1 OCT                        by current
   10 JUNE ...... Mumbai            15 OCT                        normal of
   15 JUNE ...... Saurashtra-       1 NOV                         17 SEPT
                  Kachchh, central  15 NOV
    1 JULY         India             1 DEC
   15 JULY ...... whole country     15 DEC  ... extreme SE tip

   READ THE TWO COLUMNS TOGETHER AND THE RULE IS VISIBLE:
   the LAST place to be reached is the FIRST to be left.

  THE DURATION SCALE  (Khullar folio 148, exact figures)

   PUNJAB      |======|                     2.5 MONTHS
               ^      ^
        SW monsoon    withdraws
        reaches in    in the SECOND
        the FIRST     WEEK OF
        WEEK OF JULY  SEPTEMBER

   COROMANDEL  |=============================|  MORE THAN 6 MONTHS
               ^                             ^
        reaches in the            withdraws only in the
        FIRST WEEK OF JUNE        MIDDLE OF DECEMBER

   J   F   M   A   M   J   J   A   S   O   N   D
   |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

  KHULLAR'S OWN SENTENCE, folio 148, the one to quote:
   "A simple comparison of figure 5.19 with 5.21 will show that the
    SOUTH-WEST MONSOONS REACH NORTH-WEST INDIA LAST OF ALL AND
    WITHDRAW FROM THERE FIRST OF ALL."

  LEONG printed p.124, THE SENTENCE THAT IS WRONG:
   "The PUNJAB PLAINS WHICH RECEIVE THE SOUTH-WEST MONSOON EARLIEST
    ARE THE FIRST TO SEE THE WITHDRAWAL OF THE MONSOON."
    ^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^
    The CONCLUSION (Punjab withdraws first) is CORRECT.
    The PREMISE (Punjab receives the monsoon earliest) is FALSE.
    Punjab receives it in the FIRST WEEK OF JULY - LAST of all.
    *** C-08: a true conclusion resting on a false premise, which
        is the most dangerous kind of textbook error, because the
        answer looks right. ***

  THE ASYMMETRY THAT COMPLETES THE PICTURE
   ADVANCE    : a BURST - "sudden", with thunder and lightning
   WITHDRAWAL : "rather GRADUAL and takes ABOUT THREE MONTHS"
   The monsoon arrives in a day and leaves over a season.
```

### Correction C-08 — the headline, stated exactly

✗ **Leong printed p. 124:** *"The **Punjab plains which receive the south-west monsoon earliest** are
the first to see the withdrawal of the monsoon."*

✅ **Khullar folio 148:** *"the **south-west monsoons reach north-west India last of all and withdraw
from there first of all**… In **Punjab** the south-west monsoons reach in the **first week of July**
and withdraw in the **second week of September**, giving only **two and a half months**… the
south-west monsoons reach the **Coromandel coast** in the **first week of June** and withdraw from
there only in the **middle of December**… more than **six months**."*

📰 **Current IMD practice agrees with Khullar:** the 2026 withdrawal *"commenced on 19 September"*
**from west Rajasthan**, against a **normal date of 17 September** — i.e. withdrawal begins in the
**north-west**, which the advance reaches last.

⚠️ **Why this error is worth a paragraph rather than a footnote.** Leong's **conclusion** is right:
Punjab does see the withdrawal first. Only his **premise** is wrong. An error of this shape is far
more dangerous than a plain mistake, because a candidate who reproduces the whole sentence will get
the withdrawal order right and the advance order **backwards**, and will have contradicted Lesson
10's own advance sequence — in which the monsoon reaches Kerala on 1 June and the merged current
reaches Punjab only after the Agra-Ferozepur merge. ⛔ **The sentence is quoted in this edition only
inside a correction frame and is never used as fact.**

### The technical content, as printed

- ✅ **Leong p. 124:** the monsoon retreats southwards after **mid-September**. *(The Punjab sentence
  follows, and is corrected above.)*
- ✅ **Khullar folio 148, the withdrawal sequence:** *"The monsoons withdraw from the extreme
  north-west end of the country in **September**, from the peninsula by **October** and from the
  extreme south-eastern tip by **December**."* The season is also called the *season of retreating
  monsoon* or the *post monsoon season*, and the **cool season** begins with the withdrawal in
  mid-September and continues to November.
- ✅ **The durations:** **Punjab — reached first week of July, withdraws second week of September,
  only two and a half months.** **Coromandel coast — reached first week of June, withdraws middle of
  December, more than six months.**
- ✅ **The character:** *"Unlike the **sudden burst** of the advancing monsoons, the **withdrawal is
  rather gradual and takes about three months**."*
- ✅ **Fig. 5.21**, *Normal dates of withdrawal of Monsoon*, isochrones **1 Sept · 15 Sept · 1 Oct ·
  15 Oct · 1 Nov · 15 Nov · 1 Dec · 15 Dec**.
- ✅ **October temperatures (folio 148):** day temperature over larger parts of **Rajasthan 37 °C**,
  falling to **33 °C** in the peninsula and **30 °C** in north-east India; night minima **20°-25 °C**.
  ⚠️ These are the numbers behind the popular name *October heat*: the rain has gone, the sun has not,
  and humidity remains.
- 📰 **Current normals and the 2026 actual (A-4):** normal withdrawal commencement from **west
  Rajasthan is 17 September**; in 2026 it **commenced on 19 September**, *"2 days delayed"*. ✗
  **C-24:** Khullar's **1 September** first isochrone is therefore **superseded**.

### The causal chain, with its assumptions and its limits

```text
   By mid-July the monsoon has reached its northern limit, arriving
   in Kashmir "only as a FEEBLE CURRENT because it has shed most of
   its moisture" (folio 143)
        |
        v
   The north-western extremity is therefore the WEAKEST PART OF THE
   SYSTEM - furthest from the source, driest, most marginal
        |
        v
   SEPTEMBER: solar declination moves south; the continental thermal
   low weakens; the convergence zone begins its return
        |
        v
   THE MARGINAL EXTREMITY FAILS FIRST
   -> withdrawal commences from WEST RAJASTHAN (normal 17 Sept;
      2026 actual 19 Sept) - the LAST place reached
        |
        v
   The withdrawal line moves SOUTH-EASTWARD, mirroring the advance
   -> peninsula by OCTOBER
        |
        v
   The flow over the Bay REVERSES. Air now crossing the Bay from the
   north-east PICKS UP MOISTURE and delivers it to the SOUTH-EASTERN
   COAST
        |
        v
   -> the extreme south-eastern tip is not left until DECEMBER,
      because for that coast the retreat IS the rainy season
      (Lesson 16)
        |
        v
   TOTALS: Punjab 2.5 months. Coromandel more than 6.
   CHARACTER: "rather GRADUAL and takes about THREE MONTHS", against
   a BURST of a single day.

  WHY ADVANCE IS ABRUPT AND RETREAT IS GRADUAL - the asymmetry
  explained, as an inference from the taught mechanism:
   ONSET is gated by an UPPER-AIR TRANSITION (Lesson 7) - the jet
   shifts, the restraint is released, and a system that was held
   back arrives all at once.
   WITHDRAWAL is governed by the SLOW DECAY of a thermal field and
   the southward march of solar declination - a continuous forcing,
   with no gate to release.
   A GATED PROCESS IS ABRUPT; A DECAY PROCESS IS GRADUAL.
   *** This inference is NOT PRINTED in the certified corpus. It is
       marked as an inference and is consistent with every printed
       element used above. ***

  LIMIT 1: Khullar's withdrawal isochrones PREDATE IMD's revision
  (C-24); the current normal commencement is 17 SEPTEMBER, not
  1 September.
  LIMIT 2: the Punjab and Coromandel durations are KHULLAR'S, with
  no base period stated.
  LIMIT 3: ONE SEASON'S actual (2026: 19 Sept, 2 days late) is NOT a
  normal and NO TREND is claimed from it (R-03).
```

### The India hook, with its limitation printed beside it

⚠️ **Punjab gets about two and a half months of south-west monsoon; the Coromandel coast gets more
than six.** Punjab is reached in the **first week of July** and left in the **second week of
September**; the Coromandel coast is reached in the **first week of June** and left only in the
**middle of December**. The same national "monsoon season" means something completely different at
the two ends of the country — which is the strongest single argument against reasoning about India's
monsoon from a national figure.

> ⛔ **Limitation that must be printed with it.** **Leong states the Punjab rule backwards**
> (**C-08**), and his sentence is quoted here only inside a correction frame. Khullar's durations
> carry **no base period**. And **Khullar's withdrawal isochrones are superseded** (**C-24**): the
> current normal commencement from west Rajasthan is **17 September**, not his **1 September**.

### Comparison, objection, reply and the unresolved residual

**Comparison — the advance and the withdrawal are not mirror images, despite the mirror map.** They
are mirrored in **order** (last in, first out) and opposed in **character**: the advance is a
**burst**, gated by an upper-air transition and arriving in a day with thunder and lightning; the
withdrawal is *"rather gradual and takes about **three months**"*, governed by the decay of a thermal
field. ⚠️ And they differ in **product**: the advance delivers rain to the places it reaches,
whereas the withdrawal **creates a new rainy season** for the south-east, because the reversed flow
crosses the Bay and picks up moisture. **A retreating monsoon is not a monsoon doing less; it is a
monsoon doing something else.**

**Objection.** *"Leong is a standard text. Calling one of its sentences simply wrong is
presumptuous, and an examiner might have learned it from him."*

**Reply.** The correction is not a matter of judgement, and that is precisely why it is safe to make.
It is **cross-book adjudicable**: Khullar's folio 148 states the opposite rule **explicitly**, gives
**durations** on both sides, and derives it from a comparison of his **own two figures**; and current
IMD practice begins withdrawal from **west Rajasthan**, which the advance reaches **last**. ⚠️
Further, Leong's premise contradicts **his own chapter's logic** — he places the summer low over the
**Punjab** and describes the rains breaking over the subcontinent from the **south**. ⚠️ The
examination-safe move is also the honest one: **state the rule, give Khullar's durations, and note
the printed error** — which demonstrates source-handling rather than mere recall, and cannot be
marked down.

**Unresolved residual.** ⇄ **Khullar's own cold-season boundary is ambiguous in his own text.** At
folio 148 the **cool season** *"begins with the withdrawal in mid-September and continues to
November"*, yet the same folio has the monsoon withdrawing from the extreme south-eastern tip only
**by December** — so the cool season begins while the monsoon is still withdrawing from part of the
country, and ends before it has finished. ⚠️ **This is a consequence of applying one national
calendar to a country whose monsoon duration varies from 2.5 to 6+ months**, and it is the clearest
single vindication of Lesson 4's refusal to adopt a seasonal calendar. ⛔ The **cold-weather-season
folios 149-151 were not certified in this lane** (**U-03**), so no further claim is made about the
cool season here.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Writing that Punjab receives the monsoon earliest.** **C-08.** It is reached in the **first
  week of July**, **last of all**.
- ❌ **Reasoning that the monsoon withdraws in the order it arrived.** **Last in, first out.**
- ❌ **Confusing withdrawal with a break.** C2's own table says it: a break is an **intra-seasonal
  relocation**; withdrawal is the **end of the season**, taking **about three months**.
- ❌ **Using Khullar's 1 September withdrawal isochrone as current.** **C-24**: the current normal
  commencement from **west Rajasthan is 17 September**.
- ❌ **Treating the 2026 two-day delay as significant.** **R-03.** It is one season's departure from
  a normal, dated **30 September 2026**.
- ❌ **Describing the withdrawal as sudden.** *"Unlike the sudden burst of the advancing monsoons,
  the withdrawal is rather **gradual** and takes about **three months**."*
- ❌ **Giving one withdrawal date for India.** **North-west in September, peninsula by October,
  extreme south-eastern tip by December.**
- ❌ **Treating the retreat as a rainless period everywhere.** For the Coromandel coast it is the
  **main rainy season** (Lesson 16).
- ❌ **Importing western disturbances as the retreating monsoon.** They take over the north-western
  rainfall role **after** withdrawal and are `Geography/advanced/13`'s (**R-06**).

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I — the withdrawal of the south-west monsoon, which C2 §3 identifies as a separate
process without a single date or duration.

**PYQ position.** No verified demand is owned here, but this lesson is **structurally required** by
the owned **2026 Prelims GS-I** demand at Lesson 20 on **seasonal precipitation** in the Andaman and
Nicobar Islands, since the islands' rainfall spans both the advance and the retreat. ⛔ The locally
held 2026 Set-A key is **provisional**; **no answer letter is recorded or inferred**.

**Answer use.** Lead with the **rule**, support it with the **two durations**, and close with the
**character asymmetry**. *"The south-west monsoon reaches north-west India last and withdraws from
there first: Punjab is reached in the first week of July and left in the second week of September —
two and a half months — while the Coromandel coast is reached in the first week of June and left
only in mid-December, more than six. Withdrawal begins from west Rajasthan, normally on 17 September
and on 19 September in 2026 (IMD, 30 September 2026), and unlike the sudden burst of the advance it
is gradual, taking about three months."* ⚠️ If the printed error is relevant, add one clause: *"Leong
states the Punjab rule in reverse at printed p. 124."*

### Revision points

1. ✅ **The rule, Khullar folio 148:** *"the **south-west monsoons reach north-west India last of all
   and withdraw from there first of all**."*
2. ✗ **C-08, Leong printed p. 124:** *"The Punjab plains which receive the south-west monsoon
   **earliest** are the first to see the withdrawal."* **The conclusion is right; the premise is
   false.** Quoted only inside a correction frame.
3. ✅ **Punjab:** reached **first week of July**, withdraws **second week of September** — **two and
   a half months**.
4. ✅ **Coromandel coast:** reached **first week of June**, withdraws **middle of December** — **more
   than six months**.
5. ✅ **The sequence:** withdrawal from the **extreme north-west in September**, from the
   **peninsula by October**, from the **extreme south-eastern tip by December**.
6. ✅ **The character:** *"Unlike the **sudden burst** of the advancing monsoons, the withdrawal is
   rather **gradual** and takes about **three months**."*
7. ✅ **Leong p. 124:** the monsoon retreats southwards **after mid-September**.
8. ✅ **Fig. 5.21 isochrones:** **1 Sept · 15 Sept · 1 Oct · 15 Oct · 1 Nov · 15 Nov · 1 Dec ·
   15 Dec**.
9. ✗ **C-24:** Khullar's **1 September** first isochrone is **superseded**. 📰 **Current normal
   commencement from west Rajasthan: 17 September** (IMD, retrieved 2026-10-04).
10. 📰 **A-4, 2026 actual:** withdrawal *"commenced on **19 September**, **2 days delayed** by its
    normal date of September 17"* (IMD, **30 September 2026**). ⛔ **No trend claimed.**
11. ✅ **October temperatures (folio 148):** day **37 °C** over larger parts of **Rajasthan**,
    **33 °C** in the peninsula, **30 °C** in north-east India; night minima **20°-25 °C**.
12. ⚠️ **The asymmetry, as an inference:** onset is **gated** by an upper-air transition and is
    therefore abrupt; withdrawal is a **decay** of a thermal field and is therefore gradual. **Not
    printed in the corpus; marked as an inference.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Which part of India sees the south-west monsoon withdraw first, and why? Identify the
printed error that this question is designed to expose.

**Model answer:** **The extreme north-west** — withdrawal commences from **west Rajasthan**, normally
on **17 September** (IMD), and in 2026 on **19 September**. The reason is that the north-west is
reached **last**: Khullar states that *"the south-west monsoons reach north-west India **last of
all** and withdraw from there **first of all**"*, and the current arriving there in mid-July does so
*"only as a **feeble current** because it has shed most of its moisture"*. That marginal extremity
fails first when the forcing weakens. **Punjab** therefore gets only **two and a half months** —
first week of July to second week of September — against the **Coromandel coast's more than six**,
first week of June to mid-December. The printed error is **Leong printed p. 124**, which states that
*"the Punjab plains… **receive the south-west monsoon earliest**"* — **a true conclusion on a false
premise**. *(135 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That a system retreats in the order in which it advanced. The monsoon is
weakest where it has travelled furthest, so its **last-occupied margin is its first casualty** —
last in, first out. *(32 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 15

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** Explain the pattern and character of the withdrawal of
the south-west monsoon from India.

**Model:** The governing rule is **last in, first out**. Khullar states that *"the south-west
monsoons reach north-west India last of all and withdraw from there first of all"*, because the
current reaching the north-west in mid-July arrives *"only as a **feeble current**"*, having shed its
moisture. Withdrawal therefore **commences from west Rajasthan** — normal date **17 September**,
actual **19 September** in 2026 (IMD, 30 September 2026) — then clears the **peninsula by October**
and the **extreme south-eastern tip by December**. The durations are unequal: **Punjab**,
reached in the first week of July and left in the second week of September, has **two and a half
months**; the **Coromandel coast**, reached in the first week of June and left in mid-December, has
**more than six**. *"Unlike the sudden burst of the advancing monsoons, the withdrawal
is rather **gradual** and takes about **three months**"* — and for the south-east it inaugurates a
**new rainy season**. *(149 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** **Directional logic** — last-in-first-out stated and justified by the feeble
northern current **3** · **Duration arithmetic** — both the Punjab and Coromandel spans given with
their start and end **2** · **Error correction** — Leong's reversed premise identified, or at
minimum the correct premise stated **2** · **Normal-date currency** — the superseded isochrone
avoided and the 17 September normal used with its dated 2026 actual **3**

### The intuition, before any term

Everything so far has described the Indian monsoon as a June-to-September affair. For most of the
country that is right. For one stretch of coast it is almost exactly wrong, and the reason is
geometric rather than meteorological.

Recall Lesson 12's **double shadow**. The Tamil Nadu coast sits in the rain shadow of the Arabian Sea
current, which has already given up its moisture on the Ghats — and it lies **parallel** to the Bay
current, which therefore runs along it rather than into it. Two branches, and neither one rains on
it. So the south-west monsoon, which defines the Indian year, effectively **skips** the Coromandel
coast.

What saves it is the retreat. When the flow reverses in October, air moving from the north-east
**crosses the Bay of Bengal** — a warm sea, at the end of a long heating season — picks up moisture,
and strikes a coast that is now, for the first time all year, **windward**. The Coromandel's rainy
season therefore begins when the rest of India's is ending.

Two consequences follow that an answer should state. First, **Kerala has two rainy seasons** — the
south-west monsoon and a secondary one in the retreat — a **bi-modal** regime. Second, any statement
of the form "India's rainy season is June to September" is false for a populous and agriculturally
important part of the country.

### The primary visual — the two ladders

### The technical content, as printed

- ✅ **The season and its region (folio 152):** **October-November is the main rainy season in Tamil
  Nadu** and adjoining **Andhra Pradesh south of the Krishna delta**, and a **secondary rainy period
  for Kerala**, which therefore has a **bi-modal** rainfall pattern. ⚠️ The **Krishna delta** is the
  printed northern boundary, and it is the kind of precise limit that distinguishes a read answer
  from a remembered one.
- ✅ **The statistics (folio 152):** *"According to a study conducted by **Krishna Rao and
  Jagannathan** the average rainfall in Tamil Nadu in October and November is **38.25 cm** which is
  about **38.73 per cent** of the annual rainfall received here. However, the **coastal areas receive
  about 50 %** of their annual rainfall during this season."* ⛔ **U-08: the study carries no date in
  the printed folio, and none is supplied here.**
- ✅ **Leong's independent station figure (p. 123):** *"In **Madras 50 inches** of rain falls during
  **October and November**, accounting for **half** its annual rainfall."* [50 in = **127.0 cm**, a
  conversion.] ⚠️ Note that Leong places this inside his **cool dry** season, because his three-season
  scheme has **no retreat season** (Lesson 4) — which is exactly the calendar problem this lesson
  resolves.
- ✅ **The north-western counterpart, named and routed.** On the same folio, Khullar records that in
  north-west India **western disturbances** produce clouding and light rainfall, with an **average
  incidence of 1 to 3** and an **increasing trend as the season advances**, and that precipitation
  falls as **snow** in the higher reaches of **Jammu and Kashmir, Himachal Pradesh and the Kumaon
  Hills**. ↪ **Western disturbances are `Geography/advanced/13`'s** (**R-06**). They are named here
  because they appear on the same folio and because a candidate must not confuse them with the
  north-east monsoon; they are **not taught** in this edition.

### The causal chain, with its assumptions and its limits

```text
   JUNE-SEPTEMBER: the Coromandel coast is in a DOUBLE SHADOW
     - rain shadow of the ARABIAN SEA current, which has unloaded on
       the Western Ghats (Lesson 11)
     - PARALLEL to the BAY current, which runs along it, not into it
       (Lesson 12)
        |
        v
   So the SW monsoon reaches the coast in the first week of June and
   gives it relatively little
        |
        v
   OCTOBER: the withdrawal reverses the flow (Lesson 15)
        |
        v
   Air moves from the NORTH-EAST and CROSSES THE BAY OF BENGAL - a
   warm sea at the end of a long heating season
        |
        v
   It PICKS UP MOISTURE over that fetch
        |
        v
   It strikes the Coromandel coast, which is now - for the first time
   in the year - WINDWARD
        |
        v
   MAIN RAINY SEASON for TAMIL NADU and ANDHRA SOUTH OF THE KRISHNA
   DELTA: 38.25 cm = 38.73 % of the annual for the state; about 50 %
   for the COASTAL areas; Madras 50 inches = HALF its annual
        |
        v
   KERALA, which ALREADY had a SW monsoon maximum, now gets a
   SECONDARY maximum -> BI-MODAL
        |
        v
   AND THE MONSOON DOES NOT LEAVE THE EXTREME SOUTH-EAST UNTIL
   MID-DECEMBER (Lesson 15) - because for that coast the retreat IS
   the season

  ASSUMPTION: that the Bay fetch is what supplies the moisture.
  Leong states exactly this at p.123: the NE Monsoon "crossing the
  BAY OF BENGAL rains on the south-eastern tip".
  LIMIT 1: the Krishna Rao and Jagannathan study is UNDATED (U-08).
  LIMIT 2: the "38.73 %" and the "half" are DIFFERENT DENOMINATORS -
  a STATE average against a STATION and a COASTAL figure. They are
  not interchangeable and no arithmetic is done across them.
  LIMIT 3: the post-monsoon Bay CYCLONE season overlaps this season
  exactly. C1 §3 and §5 correctly identify October-November as a
  major Bay cyclone season, but the CYCLONES THEMSELVES are
  Geography/basic/13's, and their MANAGEMENT is Disaster
  Management's (R-04).
  LIMIT 4: no 2026 outcome can be reported, because the NE monsoon
  season had not occurred when the latest official document
  available at generation was issued (30 September 2026).
```

### The India hook, with its limitation printed beside it

⚠️ **Tamil Nadu's main rainy season is October-November, not June-September**, and the coast takes
about **50 per cent** of its annual rainfall in those two months, with Madras at **50 inches
[127.0 cm] — half its annual total**. **Kerala**, next door, has a **bi-modal** year: a south-west
monsoon maximum and a secondary retreat-season maximum.

> ⛔ **Limitation that must be printed with it.** The **38.73 per cent** figure comes from an
> **undated study** — *Krishna Rao and Jagannathan*, with **no year printed on the folio**
> (**U-08**) — and it is a **state** average, not a coastal one. The **coastal 50 per cent** and
> Leong's **Madras "half"** are different denominators from the state figure, and **no arithmetic is
> done across them**.

### Comparison, objection, reply and the unresolved residual

**Comparison — the north-east monsoon against the south-west monsoon, as systems.** The south-west
monsoon is a **deep, cross-equatorial, hemispheric** current, 600 hPa deep by the onset criterion,
delivering three-quarters of the national total. The north-east monsoon is a **shallow, continental
outflow** that acquires its moisture over a **limited Bay fetch** and delivers rain to a narrow
coastal belt. ⚠️ They are not symmetrical halves of a double system in **magnitude**, though Lesson
1's definition makes them symmetrical halves in **structure** — and the asymmetry is the point: the
same reversal that is a world-scale rain event in one direction is a regional one in the other,
because the return flow has a far shorter oceanic path.

**Objection.** *"Calling this a 'monsoon' at all is loose. It is the winter half of the same double
system, which Lesson 1 already defined, so treating it as a separate season inflates it."*

**Reply.** The objection is right about the physics and wrong about the consequence. It **is** the
winter limb of the double system — Lesson 1's definition and Majid's two-monsoon scheme both say so,
and Majid's top-level unit is precisely *"the season of the north-east monsoon"*. But the **rainfall
consequence** is not a mirror image: this limb produces a **main rainy season** for an entire region,
with **38.73 per cent** of Tamil Nadu's annual total and about **half** of its coast's in two months.
⚠️ A structure that is one system can still require two seasonal treatments if its two limbs produce
different rainfall regimes in different places — which is exactly why Leong's three-season calendar,
with **no retreat season**, has to park Madras' October-November rainfall inside his *cool dry*
season (Lesson 4). **The objection is a classification argument; the lesson is a rainfall argument.**

**Unresolved residual.** ⛔ **U-08:** the **Krishna Rao and Jagannathan** study is **undated** in the
printed folio, so its vintage cannot be assessed and the **38.25 cm / 38.73 %** pair cannot be placed
against any modern normal period. ⚠️ And a second, quieter residual: Khullar states that
October-November is the main season for Tamil Nadu and Andhra **south of the Krishna delta** and a
**secondary** season for Kerala, but gives **no figure at all for Kerala's secondary peak**. **The
bi-modality is asserted and never quantified.**

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Saying Tamil Nadu gets most of its rain from the south-west monsoon.** Both owners' trap lines
  say otherwise, and this lesson quantifies it.
- ❌ **Extending the north-east monsoon's main-season status beyond its printed limit.** It is **Tamil
  Nadu and adjoining Andhra Pradesh south of the Krishna delta**.
- ❌ **Calling Kerala's retreat rainfall its main season.** It is a **secondary** rainy period;
  Kerala is **bi-modal**.
- ❌ **Mixing the three percentages.** **38.73 %** is a **state** figure; **about 50 %** is a
  **coastal** figure; Leong's *"half"* is a **station** figure for Madras. **Three denominators.**
- ❌ **Dating the Krishna Rao and Jagannathan study.** **U-08**: the folio prints **no date**.
- ❌ **Converting Leong's 50 inches silently.** **127.0 cm**, marked as a conversion (**R-07**).
- ❌ **Teaching western disturbances here.** Khullar prints their **incidence of 1 to 3** on the same
  folio; they are `Geography/advanced/13`'s and are **named and routed, not taught** (**R-06**).
- ❌ **Writing the post-monsoon cyclone season as monsoon content.** The **storms** are
  `Geography/basic/13`'s; their **management** is `Disaster-Management`'s (**R-04**).

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I — the north-east monsoon and the retreat-season rainfall of the south-eastern
coast; the discharge of route-in **I-5** from Topic 13's released edition.

**PYQ position.** No verified demand is owned here. ⚠️ The lesson is nonetheless **directly relevant
to the owned 2026 Prelims GS-I demand** at Lesson 20, which concerns **seasonal precipitation** in
the Andaman and Nicobar Islands — a territory in the Bay of Bengal whose rainfall year is not the
mainland's. ⛔ The locally held 2026 Set-A key is **provisional**; **no answer letter is recorded or
inferred**.

**Answer use.** Use this lesson whenever a stem invites a national generalisation, because it is the
cleanest available **counter-example**. *"The statement that India's rainy season runs from June to
September is false for the Coromandel coast: Tamil Nadu and adjoining Andhra south of the Krishna
delta take their main rainfall in October and November — 38.25 cm, about 38.73 per cent of the state
annual on the Krishna Rao and Jagannathan figures, and about 50 per cent for the coastal areas, with
Leong recording 50 inches [127.0 cm] at Madras, half its annual total — while Kerala, with a
secondary maximum in the same season, is bi-modal."* One sentence, three denominators handled
correctly, and a national generalisation destroyed.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **The season (folio 152):** **October-November is the main rainy season in Tamil Nadu** and
   adjoining **Andhra Pradesh south of the Krishna delta**, and a **secondary rainy period for
   Kerala**, which is therefore **bi-modal**.
2. ✅ **Krishna Rao and Jagannathan:** Tamil Nadu's October-November average is **38.25 cm**, about
   **38.73 per cent** of the annual; **coastal areas receive about 50 %** of their annual in this
   season. ⛔ **U-08: the study is undated in the printed folio.**
3. ⚠️ **Arithmetic verified at generation:** 38.25 / 0.3873 = **98.8 cm**, an implied Tamil Nadu
   annual of about **99 cm**, consistent with Khullar's own **low (50-100 cm)** annual class at
   Lesson 17. **The two printed figures are mutually consistent.**
4. ✅ **Leong p. 123:** *"In **Madras 50 inches** of rain falls during **October and November**,
   accounting for **half** its annual rainfall"* [50 in = **127.0 cm**] — printed inside his **cool
   dry** season, because his scheme has **no retreat season**.
5. ⚠️ **Three denominators, never merged:** **38.73 %** = state · **about 50 %** = coastal ·
   **"half"** = one station, Madras.
6. ⚠️ **The structural reason:** the **double shadow** of Lesson 12 — rain shadow of the Arabian
   current, and **parallel** to the Bay current — means the south-west monsoon effectively skips this
   coast; the reversed flow **crosses the Bay**, picks up moisture, and makes the coast **windward**
   for the first time in the year.
7. ✅ **The withdrawal link:** the monsoon leaves the extreme south-eastern tip only in **mid-December**
   (Lesson 15), because for that coast the retreat **is** the season.
8. ✅ **Named and routed, same folio:** **western disturbances** in north-west India, **average
   incidence 1 to 3**, with an increasing trend as the season advances, and **snow** in the higher
   reaches of **Jammu and Kashmir, Himachal Pradesh and the Kumaon Hills**. ↪
   `Geography/advanced/13` (**R-06**) — **named, not taught**.
9. ⚠️ **Route-in I-5 discharged:** Topic 13's released edition transfers the **north-east monsoon**
   and **Tamil Nadu's October-December rainfall** to Topic 16, and this lesson carries them.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does Tamil Nadu's rainfall year differ from the rest of India's, and what exactly
do the figures 38.73 per cent, about 50 per cent and "half" each refer to?

**Model answer:** Because the coast is in a **double shadow** during the south-west monsoon: it lies
in the **rain shadow of the Arabian Sea current**, which unloads on the Western Ghats, and
**parallel** to the **Bay current**, which runs along it rather than into it. When the flow reverses
in October, air from the north-east **crosses the Bay**, gathers moisture, and strikes a coast that
is now windward — so **October-November is the main rainy season** for Tamil Nadu and adjoining
Andhra **south of the Krishna delta**. The three figures have **three different denominators**:
**38.73 per cent** is the share of **Tamil Nadu's state annual** (38.25 cm, after the undated Krishna
Rao and Jagannathan study); **about 50 per cent** is the share for the **coastal areas**; and
**"half"** is **Leong's figure for Madras station**, where **50 inches [127.0 cm]** falls in those two
months. *(141 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That a percentage can be moved between a state, a coastal belt and a
station. These are three populations, and the apparent disagreement between "38.73 per cent" and
"half" is a **denominator difference, not a conflict**. *(36 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 16

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** Account for the distinctive rainfall regime of the
south-eastern coast of India.

**Model:** It is distinctive because the **south-west monsoon effectively bypasses it**. The coast
lies in the **rain shadow of the Arabian Sea current**, which has unloaded on the Western Ghats, and
runs **parallel** to the **Bay of Bengal current**, so neither branch delivers to it — a **double
shadow**. The retreat supplies what the advance did not: from **October**, the reversed flow crosses
the warm **Bay of Bengal**, gathers moisture, and meets a coast that is for the first time
**windward**. Hence **October-November is the main rainy season** in **Tamil Nadu and adjoining
Andhra Pradesh south of the Krishna delta**: **38.25 cm**, about **38.73 per cent** of the state
annual on the **Krishna Rao and Jagannathan** figures — the study **undated** in the source — with
**about 50 per cent** for coastal areas and, at **Madras**, **50 inches [127.0 cm]**, *"half its
annual rainfall"*. **Kerala**, with a secondary maximum here, is **bi-modal**. *(148 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** **Seasonal reassignment** — the main-season status established, not merely
mentioned **3** · **Regional specificity** — the Krishna-delta limit and Kerala's bi-modality both
stated **2** · **Statistical citation** — all three figures used with their correct denominators
**3** · **Dating caution** — the undated study flagged and the conversion marked **2**

Progress: 17 / 20 · Stage: Synthesis · Primary visual grammar: **isohyet trace plus a coefficient-of-variability matrix rendered as a text choropleth**

### The intuition, before any term

Every mechanism taught so far converges here. The Arabian branch built a wet strip and a dry
interior; the Bay branch built a funnel and an east-west gradient; the depressions scattered rain
across the plains; the breaks removed it for a week at a time. The result, laid on a map, is the
**most unequal rainfall distribution of any large country**, and this lesson measures it.

Two ideas govern the measurement, and the second is the one that earns marks.

**The first is the amount.** India's national average, **about 118 cm**, is high — Khullar calls it
*"the highest for a country of such vast dimensions anywhere in the world"*. Parts of Meghalaya get
**1,000 cm**; parts of south-west Rajasthan get **15**. A single national figure is nearly
meaningless.

**The second is the reliability**, and here there is a cruel regularity. The places that get the
least rain are also the places where the amount varies most from year to year. The **coefficient of
variability** — standard deviation over mean, as a percentage — runs **15-35 per cent** nationally,
but reaches **80 per cent** in the extreme west of Rajasthan, which is also where rainfall is
minimum. Khullar states the corollary flatly: rainfall amount and reliability are **inversely
proportional to the coefficient of variability**. **The driest places are also the least
predictable.**

And this lesson is where the controlling book contradicts itself the most: three internal conflicts
and one statistic attributed to the **1951 Census**.

### The four conflicts in this chapter, printed and not resolved

⇄ **C-16 — the western Rajasthan minimum, two printed values.**

| Folio | Printed |
|---|---|
| **148**, characteristic (vii) | *"from about **12 cm** in western Rajasthan to over 250 cm in the west coastal plains"* |
| **152** | *"some districts in south-west Rajasthan hardly **15 cm**"* |

⚠️ **Three centimetres apart, four folios apart, in one chapter.** Folio 155 then prints a third
related figure — **Sri Ganganagar receives 12 cm** of rain in 10-12 rainy days — which is consistent
with the folio-148 value but is a **station**, not a regional minimum. **All are printed; none is
adopted.**

⇄ **C-19 — the national range is not a national range.** Folio 148 (vii) gives *"large-scale spatial
variation, from about 12 cm in western Rajasthan to **over 250 cm** in the west coastal plains"*,
while folios 146 and 152 print **1,102 cm** and **1,221 cm** for Cherrapunji and Mawsynram. ⚠️ The
resolution is a **category error, not a conflict of fact**: *"over 250 cm"* is the **floor of the
very-high class**, not the national maximum. ⛔ **An answer that quotes "12 cm to over 250 cm" as
India's rainfall range is reproducing a class boundary as an extreme**, and understates the true
maximum by a factor of nearly five.

⇄ **C-18 — three rainy-season shares, measuring two different populations.**

| Printed | Folio | What it measures |
|---|---|---|
| *"India's **three fourths** of the total annual rainfall is received during this season"* | 140 | the **national** share, June-September |
| the plains average about **92 cm**, *"or about **87 per cent**"*, against only **14 cm** in the rest of the year | 140, same paragraph | the **plains** share |
| *"about **75 per cent** is received in the monsoon months from June to September"* | 155 | the **national** share again |

⚠️ **Verified at generation:** 92 / (92 + 14) = **86.8 %**, consistent with the printed *"about 87
per cent"*. ⚠️ So **the 75 % and the 87 % are not rivals**; the first is national and the second is
the **plains**, and the *"three fourths"* at folio 140 agrees with the 75 % at folio 155. **The
apparent contradiction inside one paragraph dissolves once the populations are separated — but the
paragraph does not separate them, and a reader who does not do the arithmetic will see a
contradiction.**

✗ **C-25 — Table 5.3 is a 1951 Census statistic.**

| **TABLE 5.3 — Distribution of Rainfall** (folio 155) | |
|---|---|
| **% of total land area** | **Annual rainfall (cm)** |
| 11 | above 200 |
| 21 | 125 to 200 |
| 37 | 75 to 125 |
| 24 | 35 to 75 |
| 7 | below 35 |

✅ Attributed on the folio to *"the calculations made by **census of India in 1951**… only **11 per
cent** area of India gets over 200 cm of annual rainfall while about **one third** of the total land
area has to content with a mere 75 cm annually"*. ⚠️ **Verified at generation:** the column sums to
**11 + 21 + 37 + 24 + 7 = 100** exactly, and the two sub-75 cm classes sum to **31 %**, consistent
with *"about one third"*. ⛔ **The table is internally sound and its provenance is 1951. It must be
quoted with that date attached and never as a current distribution.**

### The remaining printed payload

- ✅ **National average (folio 152):** *"The average annual rainfall of India is about **118 cm**
  which is the **highest for a country of such vast dimensions anywhere in the world**."*
- ✅ **Extremes:** large parts of **Meghalaya** receive about **1,000 cm**; **Kanniyakumari and
  Nellai Kattabomman** districts get **less than 30 cm** during the south-west monsoon while the
  nearby **Kerala coast gets 200 cm or more**. ⚠️ That last pair is a superb micro-example: two
  adjacent stretches of the same peninsular tip, differing by a factor of seven in the same season.
- ✅ **The nine chief characteristics of monsoonal rainfall (folio 148):** (i) **seasonal,
  June-September**; (ii) largely **orographic**, governed by relief, with the windward Western Ghats
  above **250 cm** and most of the leeward side **below 60 cm**; (iii) rainfall **decreases with
  distance from the sea** — Kolkata 119, Patna 105, Allahabad 76, Delhi 56 cm (⇄ **C-13**, Lesson
  12); (iv) rain comes in **wet spells separated by rainless intervals called breaks** (⇄ **C-14**,
  Lesson 14); (v) **heavy downpour** producing large-scale run-off and soil erosion; (vi) **vital for
  the agrarian economy**; (vii) **large-scale spatial variation** (⇄ **C-16**, **C-19**); (viii)
  monsoons **often fail to keep date**; (ix) they sometimes **withdraw before the scheduled time**,
  damaging crops.
- ✅ **Torrentiality (folio 155):** *"Indian rainfall is basically **torrential** in nature… the
  actual **rainy days are 40-45** only… A rainfall of **50-60 cm in a rainy day** is not uncommon.
  The highest record… is **103.6 cm in 24 hours at Cherrapunji**. This place gets rainfall of
  **1,102 cm in 180 rainy days**. **Sri Ganganagar** receives **12 cm** of rain in **10-12 rainy
  days**. Hence the statement, *it pours, it never rains in India*, is true whether it be Meghalaya
  or Rajasthan."*
- ✅ **Reliability (folio 155):** actual rainfall stays within **10 to 15 per cent** of normal in
  **Assam and West Bengal**, but may vary by **thirty to fifty per cent or even more** in
  **Rajasthan** and adjoining north-west India.

### The dated anchor, A-3 — and the arithmetic residue X-06

📰 **The 2026 season is a textbook demonstration of C1's own thesis that a national figure conceals
regional deficit.** From the IMD *Salient Features*, **30 September 2026**, read in full at
generation: of **36 meteorological subdivisions**, **eighteen** — constituting **53 % of the area** —
received **normal** rainfall, **seventeen**, representing **42 %**, recorded **deficient** rainfall,
and **one (Odisha)**, covering **5 %**, experienced **excess**. ⚠️ **Verified at generation:**
18 + 17 + 1 = **36** ✓ and 53 + 42 + 5 = **100** ✓.

⚠️ **The reading that earns the mark:** a season recorded nationally as **87 % of LPA — deficient**
nonetheless had **a majority of its subdivisions, and a majority of its area, in the normal
category**, and one subdivision in excess. **The national figure and the subdivision distribution
tell different stories, and both are true.** That is C1 §10.3's sentence, demonstrated.

⇄ **X-06, found at generation.** The same document's **district-wise** breakdown states **741
meteorological districts** and then distributes them as **282 below normal (38 %)**, **34
significantly below normal (5 %)**, **312 normal (42 %)**, **90 excess (12 %)** and **16 substantially
above normal (2 %)**. ⚠️ **Verified at generation:** the five counts sum to **734**, seven short of
the stated 741, and the five percentages sum to **99**. ⛔ **Both residues are disclosed. Neither is
corrected, and no count is adjusted to make the total close.**

### The causal chain, with its assumptions and its limits

```text
   SPATIAL DISTRIBUTION (Lessons 11-13 combined)
        |
        v
   A NATIONAL AVERAGE of about 118 cm, which is HIGH - and which
   describes almost nowhere
        |
        +----------------------+----------------------+
        v                      v                      v
   VERY HIGH >= 200 cm    HIGH 100-200 cm      VERY LOW < 50 cm
   west coast,            bounded by the       western Rajasthan,
   Meghalaya              100 cm ISOHYET       Kachchh, most of
   (about 1,000 cm)                            Ladakh (15 cm, or
        |                                      12 cm - C-16)
        |                                            |
        v                                            v
   TORRENTIALITY: 40-45 RAINY DAYS only; 50-60 cm in a day "not
   uncommon"; Cherrapunji 1,102 cm in 180 RAINY DAYS; Sri Ganganagar
   12 cm in 10-12 RAINY DAYS
        |
        v
   VARIABILITY: CV = (SD / Mean) x 100
        |
        v
   CV IS INVERSE TO RAINFALL
     wet west coast and sub-Himalaya -> CV 15-20 %
     dry extreme western Rajasthan   -> CV 80 %
        |
        v
   THE COROLLARY, PRINTED: "amount of rainfall and RELIABILITY of
   rainfall is INVERSELY PROPORTIONAL to the coefficient of
   variability"
        |
        v
   IN PLAIN FIGURES: Assam and West Bengal stay within 10-15 % of
   normal; Rajasthan varies by 30-50 % OR MORE
        |
        v
   *** THE DOUBLE PENALTY: THE DRIEST REGIONS ARE ALSO THE LEAST
       RELIABLE. They receive least AND can least predict what
       they will receive. ***

  ASSUMPTION: that CV is an adequate measure of reliability. It is
  standard, and Khullar prints the formula.
  LIMIT 1: CV is INFLATED BY A SMALL MEAN. A region with a 15 cm
  mean will show a high CV for an absolute variation that would be
  trivial on the west coast. The STATISTIC PARTLY MANUFACTURES THE
  PATTERN IT REPORTS - and no certified source says so. This is an
  INFERENCE, and it is marked as one.
  LIMIT 2: Table 5.3's areal distribution is a 1951 CENSUS
  calculation (C-25).
  LIMIT 3: the national minimum is printed TWO WAYS (C-16) and the
  national range quotes a CLASS FLOOR as a maximum (C-19).
  LIMIT 4: NO TREND IS CLAIMED about variability, in any direction
  (R-03).
```

### The India hook, with its limitation printed beside it

⚠️ **The driest districts of India are also the least reliable.** Extreme western Rajasthan receives
the minimum — **12 cm** at folio 148, **15 cm** at folio 152 — and carries the maximum coefficient
of variability, **80 per cent**; Khullar notes the coincidence himself: *"incidentally these are the
areas which receive minimum rainfall"*. The west coast and the sub-Himalayan belt, which receive
most, vary least, at **15-20 per cent**. **Assam and West Bengal stay within 10-15 per cent of
normal; Rajasthan may vary by thirty to fifty per cent or more.**

> ⛔ **Limitation that must be printed with it.** The **areal table is a 1951 Census calculation**
> (**C-25**); the **western Rajasthan minimum is printed two ways** (**C-16**); the *"12 cm to over
> 250 cm"* range quotes a **class floor as a maximum** (**C-19**); and ⚠️ **CV is arithmetically
> inflated by a small mean**, so part of the inverse pattern is a property of the statistic rather
> than of the climate — **an inference, not a printed claim**. ⛔ **No trend in variability is
> asserted** (**R-03**).

### Comparison, objection, reply and the unresolved residual

**Comparison — the two ways a rainfall figure can mislead.** A **national average** misleads by
**aggregation**: 118 cm describes a country containing both 1,221 cm and 15 cm. A **seasonal share**
misleads by **denominator**: 87 per cent and 75 per cent are both correct for different populations
(**C-18**). ⚠️ The transferable skill is to ask, of every rainfall number, **two** questions — *over
what area?* and *out of what total?* — and the 2026 season answers both at once: a **deficient**
national figure with **53 per cent of the area normal**.

**Objection.** *"The inverse relation between rainfall and variability is an artefact. CV has the
mean in its denominator, so a dry region is bound to show a high CV. The 'double penalty' is a
statistical illusion."*

**Reply.** The objection is partly right and the right response is to **concede the arithmetic and
keep the conclusion**, because Khullar supplies an **independent** check that does not use CV at
all. Folio 155 states the reliability directly in **absolute** terms: actual rainfall stays within
**10 to 15 per cent of normal** in Assam and West Bengal but varies by **thirty to fifty per cent or
even more** in Rajasthan. ⚠️ That is a statement about **departure from normal**, and it reaches the
same conclusion as CV by a different route. ⚠️ Moreover the **consequence** is real regardless of the
statistic: a 50 per cent shortfall on a 15 cm mean is agriculturally decisive in a way that a 15 per
cent shortfall on 300 cm is not. **The artefact argument modifies the measure, not the outcome.**

**Unresolved residual.** Four stand. ⇄ **C-16:** 12 cm against 15 cm. ⇄ **C-19:** a class floor
quoted as a national maximum. ⇄ **C-18:** three shares in one chapter, reconcilable only by
separating populations that the source does not separate. ⛔ **C-25:** the areal table's **1951**
provenance, with **no current replacement in the certified corpus**. ⚠️ And **Fig. 5.25's printed
class list skips 60-80 cm**, which is reproduced as printed and not corrected.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Quoting "12 cm to over 250 cm" as India's rainfall range.** **C-19**: *"over 250 cm"* is the
  **floor of the very-high class**; the maximum is **1,221 cm** at Mawsynram.
- ❌ **Giving one western Rajasthan minimum.** **C-16**: **12 cm** (folio 148) and **15 cm** (folio
  152).
- ❌ **Treating 75 % and 87 % as a contradiction.** **C-18**: **75 %** is **national**; **87 %** is
  the **plains** share, from 92 cm against 14 cm, verified at 86.8 %.
- ❌ **Using Table 5.3 as a current distribution.** **C-25**: it is a **1951 Census** calculation.
- ❌ **Writing the CV formula without the multiplier.** **CV = (Standard Deviation / Mean) × 100.**
- ❌ **Saying variability is high where rainfall is high.** It is the **inverse**: **15-20 %** on the
  wet west coast, **80 %** in the driest part of Rajasthan.
- ❌ **Forgetting the rainy-day count.** **40-45 rainy days** nationally; Cherrapunji takes
  **1,102 cm in 180 rainy days**; Sri Ganganagar **12 cm in 10-12 rainy days**.
- ❌ **Reading a deficient national season as a uniformly deficient one.** 2026: **87 % nationally**,
  yet **18 of 36 subdivisions normal (53 % of area)** and **Odisha in excess**.
- ❌ **Correcting the 2026 district arithmetic.** **X-06**: the printed classes sum to **734 of 741**
  and **99 %**. Disclose; do not adjust.
- ❌ **Deriving a Köppen or Thornthwaite class from these figures.** `Geography/basic/14`'s.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I — the distribution and variability of rainfall over India, which C1 §10.3 frames
correctly and quantifies nowhere.

**PYQ position.** No verified demand is owned here. This lesson is the **statistical backbone of
every 15-mark variability answer** and supplies the evidence for C1's reusable spine. ⛔ No answer
letter is recorded or inferred.

**Answer use.** Build the variability paragraph from **four moves**: the average, the spread, the
measure, the consequence. *"India's average annual rainfall of about 118 cm — the highest for a
country of such size — conceals a range from under 50 cm in western Rajasthan, Kachchh and most of
Ladakh to above 200 cm along the west coast and about 1,000 cm in parts of Meghalaya; the
coefficient of variability, standard deviation over mean, runs 15-35 per cent nationally but exceeds
60 per cent in the extreme west and reaches 80 per cent in western Rajasthan — where rainfall is
minimum — so that Assam and West Bengal stay within 10-15 per cent of normal while Rajasthan varies
by thirty to fifty per cent or more. In 2026 a nationally deficient season of 87 per cent of LPA
still left 18 of 36 subdivisions, and 53 per cent of the area, in the normal category."*

### Revision points

1. ✅ **National average:** *"about **118 cm**… the **highest for a country of such vast dimensions
   anywhere in the world**."*
2. ✅ **The four annual classes:** **very high ≥ 200 cm** (west coast Thiruvananthapuram to Mumbai,
   belt average **200-400 cm**, localised **400-800 cm**; **Meghalaya** the wettest, with
   **Mawsynram 1,221 cm** and **Cherrapunji 1,102 cm**) · **high 100-200 cm** · **low 50-100 cm** ·
   **very low < 50 cm** (western Rajasthan, Kachchh, most of Ladakh).
3. ✅ **The 100 cm isohyet:** south from the **Gujarat coast** roughly parallel to the Western Ghats
   crest to **Kanniyakumari**, then east over southern **Jammu and Kashmir, Himachal Pradesh and
   northern Uttar Pradesh**, bending **west east of Allahabad** over **Bundelkhand**, then
   **west-south-west** over western **Madhya Pradesh, eastern Maharashtra, northern Andhra and
   Telangana**.
4. ✅ **Micro-extremes:** **Meghalaya about 1,000 cm**; **Kanniyakumari and Nellai Kattabomman less
   than 30 cm** in the south-west monsoon against the nearby **Kerala coast's 200 cm or more**.
5. ⇄ **C-16:** western Rajasthan's minimum is **about 12 cm** (folio 148) and **hardly 15 cm**
   (folio 152).
6. ⇄ **C-19:** *"12 cm… to **over 250 cm**"* is a **class floor**, not a national maximum — which is
   **1,221 cm**.
7. ⇄ **C-18:** **three fourths** and **about 87 per cent** in one paragraph (folio 140), and **75 %**
   at folio 155. ⚠️ **87 %** is the **plains** share (92 cm against 14 cm, **verified at 86.8 %**);
   **75 %** is the **national** share. **Different populations, not rival figures.**
8. ✅ **CV = (Standard Deviation / Mean) × 100**; **15-35 %** nationally; **over 40 %** in much of
   Rajasthan, Gujarat and parts of Haryana and Punjab; **over 60 %** in the extreme west; **80 %** in
   extreme western Rajasthan — *"incidentally these are the areas which receive minimum rainfall"*;
   **15-20 %** on the west coast, the sub-Himalayan belt including Sikkim, Arunachal Pradesh, Assam
   and West Bengal, and in Nagaland, Manipur and Mizoram; also high in **Leh and Ladakh** and
   **Rayalaseema**.
9. ✅ **The corollary (folio 155):** *"amount of rainfall and **reliability** of rainfall is
   **inversely proportional to the coefficient of variability**."* In figures: **10-15 %** departure
   in Assam and West Bengal, **thirty to fifty per cent or more** in Rajasthan.
10. ✅ **The nine chief characteristics (folio 148):** seasonal · largely orographic (windward Ghats
    above **250 cm**, most of the leeward below **60 cm**) · decreasing with distance from the sea ·
    wet spells separated by **breaks** · heavy downpour with run-off and soil erosion · vital for
    the agrarian economy · large spatial variation · often fails to keep date · sometimes withdraws
    early.
11. ✅ **Torrentiality (folio 155):** **40-45 rainy days**; **50-60 cm in a rainy day** not uncommon;
    **103.6 cm in 24 hours at Cherrapunji**; **1,102 cm in 180 rainy days** there; **Sri Ganganagar
    12 cm in 10-12 rainy days**; *"**it pours, it never rains in India**"*.
12. ✗ **C-25, Table 5.3**, attributed to the **1951 Census**: **11 %** of land area above 200 cm ·
    **21 %** 125-200 · **37 %** 75-125 · **24 %** 35-75 · **7 %** below 35. ⚠️ **Verified at
    generation: sums to 100; the two sub-75 cm classes sum to 31 %, matching "about one third".**
13. ✅ **Fig. 5.25** classes as printed: **0-20, 20-40, 40-60, 80-100, 100-200, 200-300, 300-400,
    over 400 cm** — ⚠️ the printed list **skips 60-80 cm**, reproduced as printed.
14. 📰 **A-3, 2026 subdivisions:** **18 of 36 normal (53 % of area)**, **17 deficient (42 %)**, **1
    (Odisha) excess (5 %)** — in a season recorded nationally as **87 % of LPA, deficient**.
    **Verified: 18 + 17 + 1 = 36; 53 + 42 + 5 = 100.**
15. ⇄ **X-06, disclosed:** the 2026 **district** classes — **282 (38 %) below normal, 34 (5 %)
    significantly below, 312 (42 %) normal, 90 (12 %) excess, 16 (2 %) substantially above** — sum to
    **734 of a stated 741** districts and to **99 %**. ⛔ **Disclosed, not corrected.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Why are the driest parts of India also the least reliable, and what is the strongest
objection to the way that relationship is usually measured?

**Model answer:** Because the **coefficient of variability**, **(SD / Mean) × 100**, runs **15-20 per
cent** on the wet west coast and sub-Himalayan belt but **over 60 per cent** in the extreme west of
Rajasthan and Gujarat, **reaching 80 per cent** in extreme western Rajasthan — which Khullar notes
are *"incidentally… the areas which receive minimum rainfall"*. The corollary is printed: rainfall
amount and reliability are *"inversely proportional to the coefficient of variability"*. The
**objection** is that CV carries the **mean in its denominator**, so a dry region is arithmetically
predisposed to a high CV, and part of the pattern is an artefact of the statistic. The reply is that
Khullar supplies an **independent absolute check**: actual rainfall stays within **10-15 per cent**
of normal in Assam and West Bengal but varies by **thirty to fifty per cent or more** in Rajasthan.
**Same conclusion, different measure.** *(141 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That a nationally deficient season is deficient everywhere. In 2026
India recorded **87 per cent of LPA**, yet **18 of 36 subdivisions — 53 per cent of the area — were
normal** and Odisha was in excess. *(35 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 17

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** "In India, the reliability of rainfall matters more than
its quantity." Examine.

**Model:** It holds because the two are **inversely distributed**. India averages about
**118 cm** annually, *"the highest for a country of such vast dimensions anywhere in the world"*, yet
ranges from **under 50 cm** in western Rajasthan, Kachchh and most of Ladakh to **over 200 cm** on
the west coast and about **1,000 cm** in parts of Meghalaya. The **coefficient of variability**,
*(SD / Mean) × 100*, is **15-20 per cent** where rainfall is highest and **80 per cent** in extreme
western Rajasthan, where it is lowest; Khullar's corollary is that reliability is *"inversely
proportional to the coefficient of variability"*, so Assam and West Bengal stay within **10-15 per
cent** of normal while Rajasthan varies by **thirty to fifty per cent or more**. Torrentiality
compounds it: **40-45 rainy days**, with **50-60 cm** days not uncommon. ⚠️ Caveat: CV is
inflated by a small mean; **Table 5.3 is a 1951 Census calculation**. *(147 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** **Classification accuracy** — the four annual classes and the national
average used correctly **2** · **Isohyet reasoning** — spatial structure given, not just a list of
extremes **2** · **Variability computation** — the CV formula, the national band and the regional
extremes stated exactly **3** · **Provenance disclosure** — the 1951 attribution and the CV artefact
both flagged **3**

Progress: 18 / 20 · Stage: Synthesis · Primary visual grammar: **Walker-circulation loop in two states, with a Southern Oscillation Index sign strip**

### The intuition, before any term

A monsoon season is decided partly by things happening **outside India**, and in one case eleven
thousand kilometres outside it. That is the claim of teleconnection, and it sounds like astrology
until the mechanism is stated.

Here it is in ordinary language. Across the equatorial Pacific there is normally a great east-west
loop of air: it rises over the warm western Pacific and the Indonesian region, travels east aloft,
sinks over the cool eastern Pacific off South America, and returns west along the surface. That loop
is anchored by a **temperature contrast between the two ends of an ocean**.

When the eastern Pacific warms — an **El Niño** — the contrast weakens, and the rising limb slides
**eastward**, away from the Indian Ocean. The ascending branch that favours Indian rainfall moves
away from India. That is the whole teleconnection, and it is why a warm patch of sea off Peru is a
fact about Indian agriculture.

The crucial discipline, which both owners state correctly and most candidates ignore, is that this
is a **probability shift, not a determination**. Khullar's own statistics make the point with
unusual force: of forty-three deficient monsoon years in a century, **nineteen** were El Niño years
— fewer than half — and **six El Niño years produced good monsoon rain**. His conclusion is printed
and should be quoted: *"there is no one-to-one correspondence."*

And the 2026 season delivered the sharpest possible test of that discipline, because it had a **weak
El Niño that strengthened to strong**, a **neutral IOD throughout**, and a **deficient season of 87
per cent** — three facts that have to be stated together and in that order.

### The primary visual — the loop, in two states, with the index
