**Coverage audit of the twenty checks.** Every one of the **thirty-two corrections** is policed by at
least one check or by an explicit refusal; every one of the **eleven uncertainties** is policed by a
check or carried as a declared gap; and **both owned PYQ demands** are served — O2's capability by
checks 3, 10, 15 and 16, and O1 by check 20. **No check repeats another's demand, correction set or
capability.**

## Five cumulative checks

**Cumulative check 1 — the number whose sources disagree.**

**Demand.** A stem requires you to state the summer displacement of the intertropical convergence
zone, the annual rainfall of Cherrapunji, and the duration of a break. Write all three in a way that
survives any marker.

**Model.** *"The convergence zone is displaced to roughly 20°-30° N in July — Khullar gives 20°-25°,
Majid 25-30 and Flohn about 30, and the sources do not reconcile. Cherrapunji's annual normal is
printed as 1,102 cm by Khullar and 425 inches, about 1,079.5 cm, by Leong, neither stating a base
period; Leong additionally records 905 inches in 1861. A break normally lasts about a week, with rare
maxima of two to three weeks, and is identified objectively when the standardized rainfall anomaly
over the core monsoon zone falls below −1 for at least three consecutive days."* ⚠️ **The pattern is
constant: give the band, attribute the ends, and refuse to adopt.** *(108 words)*

**Cumulative check 2 — the four timescales.**

**Demand.** Place MJO, ENSO, IOD and PDO on their timescales and state what each governs, in four
lines.

**Model.** **MJO — intra-seasonal (weeks), Indian Ocean to Pacific**: governs **active and break
spells** within a season. **ENSO — inter-annual, equatorial Pacific, period 2-5 years and not
fixed**: governs whether a season tends to **surplus or deficit**. **IOD — seasonal to inter-annual,
Indian Ocean**: can **offset or reinforce** ENSO; in 2026 it was **neutral throughout**, so no offset
was available. **PDO — decadal, North Pacific**: modulates the **background** on which ENSO acts.
⚠️ Modoki is **not** a weak El Niño but a **central-Pacific** spatial pattern. *(79 words)*

**Cumulative check 3 — the three meanings of "break", and the two of "burst".**

**Demand.** Distinguish every sense in which the word *break* is used in this topic, and the word
*burst*.

**Model.** **Leong's *"the rains break by the middle of June"*** means **onset** — the same event
Khullar calls the ***burst***, *"accompanied by thunder, lightning and heavy downpour"*. **Khullar's
*break in the monsoon*** means a **mid-season dry spell**, an intra-seasonal **relocation** of the
trough to the Himalayan foothills, lasting about a week. ⚠️ A third sense also appears: **Majid's
*"the jet stream breaks down"***, which describes the **disintegration** of the subtropical westerly
jet and is a **different claim** from Pedelaborde's **northward shift**. ⛔ **One word, three
referents; conflating them is the standing trap of this topic.** *(91 words)*

**Cumulative check 4 — what Topic 16 does not own.**

**Demand.** A stem reads: "Discuss the impact of the monsoon on Indian agriculture and the measures
needed to manage drought." How much is Topic 16's?

**Model.** The **climatic half** only. Topic 16 owns the **onset, distribution, breaks, withdrawal
and variability** that create agricultural risk — the timing-over-quantity argument, the coefficient
of variability, the depression dependence, the core-monsoon-zone figure. ⛔ It owns **none** of the
**agricultural-economic** content — MSP, cropping patterns, irrigation coverage, kharif area, food
security (**R-05**, `Geography/basic/30`) — and **none** of the **drought management** — declaration,
relief, contingency planning, early warning (**R-04**, `Disaster-Management/basic/08`). ⚠️ The correct
answer supplies the mechanism with precision, **names the two owners**, and does not write their
content. *(81 words)*

**Cumulative check 5 — the whole mechanism in one paragraph.**

**Demand.** State the complete mechanism of the Indian summer monsoon at 20-mark depth, in one
paragraph, with attribution.

**Model.** Differential heating of the Asian landmass against the Indian Ocean reverses the seasonal
pressure gradient — **Halley, 1686** — but cannot explain unequal development, orographic control or
upper-air behaviour. **Flohn** reframes the monsoon as *"a modification of the planetary winds of the
tropics"*: the convergence zone is displaced to roughly **20°-30° N**, lying across the Indo-Gangetic
plain as the **monsoon trough**, and the **south-east trades cross the equator** and are turned by
the Coriolis effect into the south-west monsoon. **Koteswaram (1952)** adds an **elevated** driver —
Tibet, **4.5 million sq km** at about **4,000 m**, **2-3 °C** warmer at level, generating an
anticyclonic outflow at **300-200 mb** that subsides over the equatorial Indian Ocean and returns as
moist **equatorial westerlies**, with a **tropical easterly jet** of over **100 knots** beneath it.
**Yin (1949)** and **Pedelaborde (1963)** supply the timing: the subtropical westerly jet's northward
shift to the far side of Tibet is *"the first indication of the onset"*. The expeditions of
**1962-1979** located the crossing **off the Kenya coast** and tied its fluctuations to **rainfall
over Maharashtra**. *(174 words)*

---

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

Seven original questions with complete model answers and **seven paper-level rubric dimension-sets**,
pairwise distinct from one another and from all twenty lesson-local sets. **Word caps are hard:
10-mark ≤ 150 words; 15- and 20-mark ≤ 250 words.** ⛔ **None of these is a past question. The two
owned PYQs are linked and routed in the section above and are never solved in this file.**

## Q1 — 10 marks, 150 words — rubric set: THESIS CONTROL

**Question.** "The Indian monsoon is better described as a displaced planetary wind system than as a
giant sea breeze." Comment.

**Model.** The displacement account is stronger, and the sea-breeze account is not merely weaker but
is an identifiable position with named holders. **Köppen (1923), Hann (1932) and Angot (1943)** held
monsoons to be *"simply land and sea breezes on a large scale"*, and Khullar glosses **Halley's 1686**
thermal explanation as the same mechanism with *"day and night… replaced by summer and winter"*.
Three failures defeat it: monsoons *"do not develop equally everywhere"*; development is governed by
*"the shape of the continents, and orography"*; and by *"the conditions of air circulation in the
upper troposphere"*. **Flohn** instead treats the Asian monsoon as *"a modification of the planetary
winds of the tropics"*, with the convergence zone drawn to about **30° N** and the **south-east
trades crossing the equator** to arrive as the south-west monsoon. **A sea breeze does not import
air from another hemisphere.** *(141 words)*

**Rubric — THESIS CONTROL (10):** proposition answered directly in the first sentence rather than
after a survey **3** · both positions attributed to named holders with dates **3** · the three printed
failures used as the pivot **2** · a single decisive closing discriminator **2**

## Q2 — 10 marks, 150 words — rubric set: PROBLEM FRAMING

**Question.** Why does the Indian monsoon "burst" rather than arrive gradually?

**Model.** Because the arrival is **gated**, not forced. A convergence zone migrating with the sun
should arrive gradually; the Indian onset does not — Leong records that the rains *"break"* by
mid-June and Khullar names the event the ***burst***, *"accompanied by thunder, lightning and heavy
downpour"*. The gate is in the upper air. **Pedelaborde (1963)** describes the western jet flowing
along *"the southern slopes of the Himalayas"* in winter and shifting *"rather dramatically"* to the
**northern edge of the Tibet Plateau** in summer; **Yin (1949)** adds that the low-latitude upper-air
trough shifts *"from **90°E to 80°E** longitude"* in response. While the jet sits south of the
mountains the low-level circulation is restrained; when it jumps, the restraint is released at once.
⚠️ **Khullar is careful to call this an *indication* of onset, not its cause**, and the distinction
should be preserved. *(138 words)*

**Rubric — PROBLEM FRAMING (10):** the puzzle stated before the answer, so the mechanism has
something to explain **3** · gate-and-release logic made explicit **3** · both authorities dated and
their separate claims kept apart **2** · the indicator qualification retained **2**

## Q3 — 15 marks, 250 words — rubric set: THEORY SEQUENCING

**Question.** Critically compare the classical and modern explanations of the Indian monsoon.

**Model.** The classical explanation is **Halley's (1686)**: thermal contrast between a heated
continent and a cooler ocean reverses the pressure gradient seasonally, in two parts — a summer and a
winter monsoon. It is not naive. It gets the direction, the seasonality and the energetics right, and
Khullar records that it *"dominated the scene for about **three centuries**"*. Khullar also records
its three failures: monsoons *"do not develop equally everywhere"*; development is governed by *"the
**shape of the continents**, and **orography**"*; and by *"the conditions of air circulation in the
**upper troposphere**"*.

Each modern theory repairs one. **Flohn** answers the first: the Asian monsoon is *"a modification of
the planetary winds of the tropics"*, with the NITCZ drawn to about **30°** and the main current the
**expanded equatorial westerlies** — so the air is the southern hemisphere's trades, deflected after
crossing the equator. **Koteswaram (1952)** answers the second with an **elevated** heat source:
Tibet, about **4.5 million sq km** at **4,000 m**, in summer **2-3 °C** warmer at level, driving an
anticyclonic outflow at **300-200 mb** that subsides over the equatorial Indian Ocean and returns as
moist equatorial westerlies, beneath a **tropical easterly jet** exceeding **100 knots**. **Yin
(1949)** and **Pedelaborde (1963)** answer the third, supplying the burst's timing.

⚠️ **The modern theories supplement rather than replace.** Khullar still writes that *"the origin of
monsoons is still shrouded in mystery"*, records that **Flohn's** concept is *"widely accepted"*
without endorsing it over Koteswaram's, and leaves surface and elevated heating unreconciled.
*(245 words)*

**Rubric — THEORY SEQUENCING (15):** chronological spine with each theory answering a stated prior
failure **5** · every theory named, dated and given its actual claim rather than a label **4** ·
Halley treated as a strong theory with limits, not an error **3** · the unresolved
surface-versus-elevated question disclosed in the conclusion **3**

## Q4 — 15 marks, 250 words — rubric set: BRANCH ANATOMY

**Question.** Examine the spatial pattern of south-west monsoon rainfall over India and account for
its principal anomalies.

**Model.** The pattern is produced by **two branches and five streams**. The **Arabian branch**, the
stronger for two stated reasons — the sea is larger, and *the entire* current advances on India while
only part of the Bay current does, the rest going to **Myanmar, Thailand and Malaysia** — divides
into **three**. Its **first** stream strikes the Ghats almost perpendicularly between about **10° and
20° N**, giving **over 250 cm** windward and **400-500 cm annually** at the crest, collapsing to
**30-50 cm within 80-100 km** leeward: **Mumbai 190 cm**, **Khandala 60 cm** at some 50 km, **Pune
50 cm** at about 160 km in the season. Its **second** passes through the **Narmada-Tapi troughs** to
**Nagpur, about 60 cm**. Its **third** runs **parallel to the Aravali**, which *"offers no
obstacle"* — the first anomaly: the hottest land in India stays dry, while **Mount Abu**, on the
range's south-eastern face, takes **170 cm** against **60-80 cm** nearby.

The **Bay branch** divides into **two**. One enters the **Khasi funnel**: **Mawsynram 1,221 cm**,
**Cherrapunji 1,102 cm**, and **Guwahati 161 cm** only 90 km away — the second anomaly, and one of
slope position rather than region. The other is **deflected westward** at the Himalayan foothills and
declines **Kolkata 119 → Patna 105 → Allahabad 91 → Delhi 51 → Bikaner 24 cm**, *"progressively
depleted… not blocked"*.

⚠️ Two cautions: folio 148 prints **Allahabad 76** and **Delhi 56**, and the **crest figure is
annual** while the station figures are **seasonal**. *(235 words)*

**Rubric — BRANCH ANATOMY (15):** both branches and all five streams distinguished, not merged into
"two branches" **4** · the transect quantified with distances **4** · both anomalies — Rajasthan and
the funnel — explained by geometry rather than asserted **4** · the dual figures and the annual/
seasonal distinction disclosed **3**

## Q5 — 15 marks, 250 words — rubric set: TIMESCALE SEPARATION

**Question.** "Monsoon variability matters more than monsoon quantity." Critically examine.

**Model.** The proposition holds across four timescales, and the evidence is strongest at the two
shortest. **Intra-seasonally**, a **break** relocates the monsoon trough to the Himalayan foothills:
rain *"practically ceases over the country outside the Himalayan belt and southeast peninsula"* while
the sub-Himalayan slopes are deluged. Breaks last **about a week** normally, rarely **two to three
weeks**, occur **one or two** times a season, and happen in **85 of 100 years** — the norm, not the
anomaly. **Synoptically**, interior rainfall depends on low-pressure systems, *"3-4 per month"*
entering through the deltas; **June 2026 produced none at all** and closed at **65 % of LPA**, while
**July 2026 had a normal count of four but 24 system-days against a normal of 13.56** and closed at
**101 %**.

**Spatially**, the **coefficient of variability** is **15-20 %** where rainfall is highest and
**80 %** in extreme western Rajasthan where it is lowest, so the driest regions are also the least
predictable; Assam and West Bengal stay within **10-15 %** of normal while Rajasthan varies by
**thirty to fifty per cent or more**. **Inter-annually**, ENSO is a probability modifier only: of
**43 deficient years, 19** were El Niño years and **six El Niño years were good**.

⚠️ The qualification matters: a **nationally deficient** 2026, at **87 % of LPA**, still left **18 of
36 subdivisions and 53 % of the area normal**. ⛔ No trend of any kind is claimed. *(225 words)*

**Rubric — TIMESCALE SEPARATION (15):** four distinct timescales named and kept apart **4** ·
each supported by its own quantitative evidence **4** · the national-versus-subdivision contrast used
as the decisive qualification **4** · probabilistic language sustained and no trend asserted **3**

## Q6 — 20 marks, 250 words — rubric set: FULL-SYSTEM ARCHITECTURE

**Question.** Explain the complete mechanism of the Indian summer monsoon, and assess how far it is
understood.

**Model.** **Four accounts, each repairing the last.** **Halley (1686)**: differential heating
reverses the seasonal pressure gradient, a double system of summer and winter winds. **Flohn**: the
monsoon is *"a modification of the planetary winds of the tropics"* — the convergence zone is
displaced to roughly **20°-30° N** (Khullar 20°-25°, Majid 25-30, Flohn about 30, unreconciled),
lying over the Indo-Gangetic plain as the **monsoon trough**; the **south-east trades cross the
equator** and are turned by the Coriolis effect, so *"these displaced trades **are** the south-west
monsoons"*. **Koteswaram (1952)**: Tibet, about **4.5 million sq km** at **4,000 m**, **2-3 °C**
warmer at level, drives an anticyclonic outflow at **300-200 mb** that subsides over the equatorial
Indian Ocean and returns as moist **equatorial westerlies**, with a **tropical easterly jet** above
**100 knots**. **Yin (1949)** and **Pedelaborde (1963)**: the subtropical westerly jet's shift to the
far side of Tibet, and a trough moving **90°E to 80°E**, time the **burst**.

**Evidence**, not inference: **IIOE (1962-65)**, **ISMEX (1973)**, **Monsoon-77** and **MONEX-1979**
located the crossing *"off the coast of Kenya"* and tied its fluctuations to **rainfall over
Maharashtra**.

**How far understood?** Khullar still writes that *"the origin of monsoons is still shrouded in
mystery"*. Surface and elevated heating are **not reconciled**; the easterly jet's height is printed
**two ways**; the break has **two incompatible causes** in one chapter; and the 2026 onset forecast
**failed**. ⚠️ **The mechanism is well described and incompletely explained.** *(233 words)*

**Rubric — FULL-SYSTEM ARCHITECTURE (20):** all four accounts present, in dependency order, each with
its own repair **6** · quantitative specificity carried throughout — latitudes, altitudes, pressure
levels, speeds **5** · observational evidence distinguished from theoretical inference **4** · the
verdict genuinely assessed rather than asserted, using named open questions **5**

## Q7 — 20 marks, 250 words — rubric set: OPERATIONAL PRECISION

**Question.** How is the Indian monsoon season monitored, forecast and verified? Illustrate with a
recent season.

**Model.** **Monitoring begins with a definition.** Onset over Kerala is declared only after
**10 May**, when **60 %** of **14 enlisted stations** report **2.5 mm or more on two consecutive
days**, *provided* westerlies extend to **600 hPa** between the equator and 10°N, 55°-80°E, zonal
winds reach **15-20 knots at 925 hPa** over 5-10°N, 70°-80°E, and INSAT **OLR falls below 200 W m⁻²**
over 5-10°N, 70°-75°E. The advance is then tracked by the **Northern Limit of Monsoon**, moving
*"usually in surges"*. Within the season, active and break spells are assessed from **standardized
rainfall anomalies over the core monsoon zone**, with **±1** thresholds.

**Forecasting** uses, since **2021**, a **Multi-Model Ensemble** of coupled global models including
IMD's **MMCFS** — replacing the post-1987 **parametric and power regression** scheme that drew on
**15 parameters** in four categories: temperature, pressure, wind pattern and **snow cover**.

**Verification, 2026.** Forecast **92 % ± 5 %** in mid-April and **90 % ± 4 %** on **29 May**;
realised **87 % of LPA, 759.4 mm**, on **30 September** — inside the band, with the probabilistic
forecast placing **60 %** on the realised *deficient* category. ⚠️ In the same season the **Kerala
onset forecast of 26 May ± 4 days failed** against **4 June**, *"only the second instance (after
2015) since… 2005"*; **three of four regional** forecasts and **two of four monthly** outlooks did not
verify. ⛔ **Accuracy is a property of a forecast at a stated scale.** *(222 words)*

**Rubric — OPERATIONAL PRECISION (20):** every threshold reproduced exactly, with its box or level
**6** · the three monitoring layers — onset, advance, intra-seasonal — distinguished **4** ·
forecasting architecture correctly dated across both eras **4** · verification reported product by
product, including the failures **6**

# REMEDIATION

Three parts. **Part A** repairs the defects a learner will import from the owner files. **Part B**
repairs the failure modes specific to this topic's material. **Part C** states the eight
discriminations on which this topic is actually tested.

## Part A — defects inherited from the owner pair

| # | What the owner says | What the evidence says | Correction |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | C1 §2 prints a **four-season** table marked *from the source book* | Leong prints **three** seasons with different boundaries; Majid prints **two** monsoon seasons subdivided four ways, retreat **Oct-Dec** | **C-01** — use the four-season division as a **convention**, never as Leong's |
| 2 | C1 §1 and §5 attribute **10°-30° N/S** to the monsoon chapter | The band is on **Leong printed p. 114**, a classification table; **Chapter 16 prints no band** | **C-02** — re-attribute, or state both facts |
| 3 | C1 §3 gives tropical-marine rainfall as *"around **150 cm+**"* | **70 in = 177.8 cm** (regime) and **88 in = 223.5 cm** (Cairns) | **C-03** — unsourced and low |
| 4 | Neither owner gives a **monsoon-type** rainfall figure | Leong p. 114 prints **60 in = 152.4 cm**, making the **marine** type wetter | **C-04** |
| 5 | C1 §4 covers monsoon vegetation in four sentences | Leong prints **deciduous** habit, *"far fewer species"*, **teak / Burma's three-quarters**, sal, acacia, eucalyptus, bamboo, the thorn gradient | **C-05** |
| 6 | Neither owner names a single theorist | **Al Masudi, Sidi Ali, Halley, Dobby, Conrad, Ramage, Flohn, Yin, Pedelaborde, Koteswaram** and fourteen others are printed | **C-06** — C2 §8 sets a theory-comparison angle its body cannot support |
| 7 | C2 §3 gives breaks as *"3-5 days over **500-1000 km**"* | The duration half is defensible; **the spatial figure is in no source** | **C-11** — refuse the length, give the **region** |
| 8 | C1 §7 carries the **forecast** of 90 % ± 4 % | The season closed at **87 %, 759.4 mm** on 30 September 2026 | **C-09** — pair them; never merge |
| 9 | C1 §7 cites a **PIB URL** | **HTTP 403** on 2026-10-04 and again at generation | **C-10** — cite the IMD PDFs |
| 10 | C2 §7 gives Mission Mausam as *"₹2,000 crore over two years"* | Correct, but needs **Cabinet 11 September 2024** and the **initial phase to March 2026** | **C-12** |
| 11 | C1 claims **Mascarene High** and **Somali jet** as owned core | Neither name is in **any** certified local book; the **phenomenon** is certified at Khullar folios 128-129 | **C-32** — label as modern nomenclature |
| 12 | C2 §1 merges Tibet's **barrier** and **heating** roles in one cell | They operate in different seasons, at different levels, by different physics; Topic 13 owns the barrier, Topic 16 the heat source | **I-6** — separate in two clauses |

## Part B — failure modes specific to this topic

| # | Failure mode | Why it happens | The repair |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Resolving a conflict by choosing a number** | Two printed figures feel like an error to be fixed | Print both, attribute both, adopt neither. **Seven conflicts in Khullar; four in the IMD record** |
| 2 | **Quoting a class floor as an extreme** | *"over 250 cm"* looks like a maximum | **C-19** — the maximum is **1,221 cm** |
| 3 | **Merging percentages with different denominators** | 75 %, 87 %, 38.73 %, "half" all sound comparable | **C-18**, Lesson 16 — ask *out of what total?* |
| 4 | **Treating a threshold as a typical value** | *"at least 3-5 days"* against *"about a week"* | Floor, normal and rare maximum are three statistics of one distribution |
| 5 | **Promoting an indicator to a cause** | The jet "indicates" onset; indication reads as causation | Preserve the printed word. **Lesson 7** |
| 6 | **Comparing across normal periods** | Khullar's 15 July and IMD's 8 July look like the same quantity | **No arithmetic across normal periods.** Lesson 10 |
| 7 | **Comparing a normal with a forecast** | Both are "expected" values | Departure = actual − normal; error = actual − forecast. **Never normal − forecast** |
| 8 | **Counting systems rather than system-days** | A count is easier to remember | 2026: normal July **count**, **24 system-days against 13.56**. Lesson 13 |
| 9 | **Reading a deficient national season as uniformly deficient** | A single headline figure | 2026: **87 % nationally**, **18 of 36 subdivisions normal** |
| 10 | **Generalising from one season** | A vivid recent case feels like evidence | **R-03.** The century of statistics at Lesson 18 governs |
| 11 | **Letting a stem's keyword decide ownership** | "monsoon", "rainfall", "Andaman" appear in other topics' questions | The **quantity asked about** decides. Three adjacency traps, above |
| 12 | **Silently converting units** | Imperial figures feel like an obstacle | **R-07.** Printed unit leads; conversion follows in brackets, marked |
| 13 | **Inheriting a true conclusion on a false premise** | Leong's Punjab sentence reaches the right verdict | **C-08.** The most dangerous error shape there is |
| 14 | **Attributing a season to climate change** | The inference feels natural | **R-02.** Report IMD's own language and stop |

## Part C — the eight discriminations this topic is actually tested on

1. **Monsoon as a wind definition against monsoon as a rainfall meaning** — four printed criteria,
   all of them wind tests, and a public usage that is entirely about rain.
2. **Tropical monsoon against tropical marine** — same latitudes, same hot zone; separated by
   **seasonality with a dry season** against **seasonality without one**, and the marine type is the
   **wetter** on Leong's own table.
3. **Burst against break against withdrawal** — onset, intra-seasonal relocation, and the end of the
   season; three events, two of them sharing a word.
4. **Orographic rainfall against synoptic rainfall** — the Ghats and the Khasi funnel against the
   depressions that make interior India's rain.
5. **Barrier against heat source** — the plateau's two roles, in two seasons, at two levels, owned
   by two topics.
6. **Indicator against cause** — the jet's northward shift, and what the sources actually say.
7. **Normal against actual against forecast** — three quantities, two legitimate subtractions.
8. **Probability modifier against deterministic cause** — ENSO, with **19 of 43** and **six good El
   Niño monsoons** behind the refusal.

## Map 3 — argument tree: "is the Indian monsoon a true monsoon?"

```text
                    IS THE INDIAN MONSOON A TRUE MONSOON?
                                    |
          +-------------------------+-------------------------+
          |                         |                         |
     BY DOBBY                  BY CONRAD                 BY RAMAGE 1971
     "reversal is the          "about 180 deg"           four criteria
      key-note"                      |                         |
          |                          |              +----------+----------+
       YES, trivially         MENON measures        |          |          |
       - no threshold         "NEARLY 180"      (i) >=120  (ii) >40 %  (iii)
       exists to fail              |             CLEARS     CLEARS    >3 m/s
          |                     YES, but the        |          |      CLEARS
          |                     margin sits in      +----------+----------+
          |                     the word "nearly"              |
          |                          |                    (iv) <1 cyclone-
          |                          |                    anticyclone
          |                          |                    alternation per
          |                          |                    2 yr per 5 deg grid
          |                          |                         |
          |                          |                    *** THE STRAIN ***
          |                          |                    Khullar f.147: the
          |                          |                    Bay makes 3-4
          |                          |                    DEPRESSIONS PER
          |                          |                    MONTH, Jun-Sep
          |                          |                         |
          +-------------+------------+-------------------------+
                        |
                   BUT BY KOPPEN / HANN / ANGOT the question does not
                   arise: monsoons are "simply land and sea breezes on a
                   large scale", so there is no separate category to
                   qualify for.  Leong p.127 SETS THIS AS AN EXERCISE.
                        |
                        v
            THE ANSWER THAT SURVIVES EVERY MARKER
            India clears every REVERSAL test and strains only against a
            DISTURBANCE test - and it strains against it for the most
            Indian of reasons. Every one of the four criteria is a WIND
            test; the word, in Indian use, is about RAIN; and no
            certified source bridges the two.
```

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

⚠️ **These are compressed recall notes, not a second teaching pass.** Headings are topic-specific.
Every figure is traceable to a folio or a dated endpoint in the source ledger below.

## Defining the object

- ***Mausin* / *monsin*** (Khullar 122) ⇄ ***Mausim*** (Majid 3.21) — Arabic/Malayan for *season*.
  **C-23.**
- A monsoon is a **double system of seasonal winds** = summer + winter winds. Majid: **~6 months NE
  + ~6 months SW** over the Arabian Sea, complete reversal every six months.
- **Dobby** — reversal is *"the key-note"*, no threshold. **Conrad** — *"about **180°**"*; **Menon**
  measures *"nearly 180°"*. **Köppen 1923 / Hann 1932 / Angot 1943** — *"simply land and sea breezes
  on a large scale"*; **Leong p. 127 sets this as an exercise**.
- **Ramage 1971** — (i) ≥ **120°** shift Jan-Jul · (ii) prevailing-direction frequency > **40 %** ·
  (iii) resultant velocity > **3 m/s** · (iv) < **1 cyclone-anticyclone alternation per 2 years per
  5° grid**. **Rectangle ≈ 35°N-25°S, 30°W-173°E.** Also **A.A. Rama Sastry**.
- ⚠️ (i)-(iii) test **reversal**; **(iv) tests absence of disturbances** — India's only strain, caused
  by the Bay depressions. **Every criterion is a wind test; the word is used about rain.**

## Distribution of the two hot-zone types

- **Monsoon (a block):** Indian sub-continent, Burma, Thailand, Laos, Cambodia, parts of Vietnam,
  south China, northern Australia.
- **Marine (a scatter):** Central America, West Indies, north-eastern Australia, Philippines, parts
  of East Africa, Madagascar, Guinea Coast, eastern Brazil.
- **10°-30° N/S is Leong p. 114, a classification table. Chapter 16 prints no band.** **C-02, N-02.**
- Leong p. 122: Central Asia in summer *"more than **15 °F**"* [8.3 °C] above normal; Fig. 125 —
  **Asiatic Low, Punjab Low, S.W. Monsoon, S.E. Monsoon (Trades)**; the **SE Monsoon crosses the
  equator** to become the SW Monsoon, and the **NE Monsoon** crosses to become northern Australia's
  **NW Monsoon**. **C-07.**
- ✗ **N-03:** Leong Ch. 16 names **no Indian station** but Bombay, Madras, Punjab, Sind, Cherrapunji
  and the Ganges-Brahmaputra delta.

## The two types, measured

| | Monsoon | Marine |
|---|---|---|
| Regime (p. 114) | **60 in** [152.4 cm] | **70 in** [177.8 cm] — **the wetter** |
| Station | **Bombay** 18°55′N 73°E, 37 ft [11.3 m] | **Cairns** 17°S 145°42′E, SE Trades |
| Annual | **72 in** [182.9 cm] | **88 in** [223.5 cm] |
| Range | **10 °F** [5.6 °C], 86→76 °F | **12 °F** [6.7 °C], 82 °F Jan → 70 °F Jul |
| Shape | Jun 19.9 / Jul 24 / Aug 14.5 / Sep 10.6 in = **95.8 %** in four months | Jan 15.8 / Feb 16.4 / Mar 17.7 / Apr 12.1 = **70.5 %**, but *"no month without any rainfall"* |

⚠️ **Discrimination: seasonal *with* a dry season against seasonal *without* one.** Sudan row for
scale: **30 in [76.2 cm]**. **C-03, C-04, N-07.**

## Seasonal calendars — three schemes, none adopted

- **Leong p. 123 — three:** cool dry **Oct-Feb** · hot dry **Mar-mid-Jun** · rainy **mid-Jun-Sep**.
- **Majid 3.21 — two monsoon seasons, four sub-seasons:** NE monsoon (Jan-Feb winter; Mar-May hot) ·
  SW monsoon (Jun-Sep general rains; **Oct-Dec retreating**).
- **C1 — four**, retreat Oct-Nov. ✗ **C-01: no certified book prints it.**
- Payload: **Bombay 76 °F** [24.4 °C] · **Punjab 50 °F** [10.0 °C] · **Madras 50 in [127.0 cm] in
  Oct-Nov = half its annual** · **Bombay May 86 °F** [30.0 °C] · central India **95 °F** [35.0 °C] ·
  **Sind 110 °F** [43.3 °C] · the rains *"break"* by **mid-June** · rain lowers Bombay **86→81 °F**,
  the north by *"as much as **13 °F**"* [7.2 °C].
- **Khullar 140 pre-monsoon names:** *spring storm showers* (nor'wester) · *tea showers* (Assam, May
  ≈ two-thirds of June) · *mango showers* (TN, AP) · *cherry blossoms* (Karnataka); coastal
  Kerala-Karnataka **~25 cm**, interior south India **~10 cm**.
- ⛔ **U-03:** *Loo* and *Kalbaisakhi* sit on **uncertified folios** — nothing quoted; routed to
  Topic 13.

## Theory lineage, with its failures

- **Mention:** Rig Veda, Greek and Buddhist scholars. **Study:** credited to the **Arabs**.
- **c. 10th C — Al Masudi of Baghdad:** reversal of **ocean currents and monsoon winds**.
  **1554 — Sidi Ali:** **commencement dates**. **1686 — Halley:** thermal contrast, **(a) summer,
  (b) winter**; *"day and night… replaced by summer and winter"*; **dominated ~three centuries**.
- **The three failures:** (1) monsoons *"do not develop equally everywhere"*; (2) governed by *"the
  **shape of the continents**, and **orography**"*; (3) by *"the conditions of air circulation in the
  **upper troposphere**"*.
- **Repairers:** Flohn, Thompson, Stephenson, Frost, **M.T. Yin**, Hwang, Takahashi, E. Palmen,
  C. Newton; Indian — **P. Koteswaram**, Krishnan, Raman, Ramanathan, Krishna Murti, Rama.

## Air-mass repair

- SE + NE trades meet at the **ITCZ**; in summer the **SE trades cross the equator** and are turned
  right — *"these displaced trades **are** the south-west monsoons"*. **Monsoon Front** where they
  meet the NE trades.
- **July: the ITCZ over the Indo-Gangetic plain = the Monsoon Trough.**
- ⇄ **C-21:** displacement **20°-25°** (Khullar) ⇄ **25-30** (Majid) ⇄ **about 30°** (Flohn).
- **Flohn:** the Asian monsoon is *"simply a **modification of the planetary winds of the
  tropics**"*; current = **expanded equatorial westerlies**; **NITCZ** (July) / **SITCZ** (January).
- **Majid:** rain *"primarily **convectional**… randomly distributed"* plus *"travelling cyclones
  associated with the ITCZ"*; the peak **moves northward**.

## Jet-stream repair

- Jet at **~12 km**; westerlies **150-300 km/h**, extreme **400** ⇄ Majid **375 kmph** (**C-22**).
- **Majid 3.35:** west-to-east near the tropopause (**~12,000 m**); **160-480 km wide × 900-2,150 m
  thick**; core **> 300 kmph**; means **110** (summer) to **190 kmph** (winter); types
  **sub-tropical / polar front / Arctic-Antarctic**.
- **Yin 1949:** the low-latitude upper-air trough shifts **90°E → 80°E** (a **zonal** claim).
- **Pedelaborde 1963**, *The Monsoon*, Fig. 5.4 after him: winter along the **southern slopes of the
  Himalayas**, summer *"rather dramatically"* to the **northern edge of Tibet**.
- **Khullar 126:** *"northward movement of the subtropical jet is the **first indication** of the
  onset"* — ⚠️ **indication, not cause**. ⇄ Majid: *"the jet stream **breaks down**"* — a different
  claim.

## Thermal engine — Tibet as a monsoon cause (route-in I-6)

- **Koteswaram 1952:** ellipsoidal plateau **~4,000 m**, **~4.5 M km²**, rim **6,000-8,000 m**,
  summer **2-3 °C warmer than air at the same level** → rising air → **anticyclonic outflow at
  300-200 mb (9-12 km)** → **subsidence over the equatorial Indian Ocean** → **returns to the west
  coast from the south-west as equatorial westerlies**, *"causing copious rainfall"* (Fig. 5.5).
- **Tropical easterly jet:** **100-200 mb**, *"often exceeding **100 knots**"*, peninsular tip to
  **~20° N**; **Koteswaram and P.R. Krishna, 1952**. ⇄ **C-17:** core **13 km / 150 mb** (f. 125) ⇄
  axis **12 km / 200 mb** (f. 126).
- **Snow:** *"the easterly jet does not come into existence if the snow over the Tibet Plateau does
  not melt… thick and widespread snow → weak monsoon and less rainfall."*
- **Winter split (used, not derived):** southern branch **~240 km/h at 200 mb** ⇄ northern
  **70-90 km/h**; reunite **off the east coast of China**; subsidence → dry outblowing northerlies
  over north-west India and Pakistan.
- **Thomson 1951 / Flohn 1960 / Stephenson 1965** — *"**Flohn's concept is widely accepted**"*.

## Evidence base and the nomenclature problem

- **Raman & Ramanathan:** easterlies become very active **after** the rains begin; **latent heat →
  inversion → rainfall**. **Ananth Krishnan:** subtropical cyclones **20°-25° N → 30° N in 5-6
  weeks**. **S. Parthasarthy:** *"a **weak north-east trade wind** results in a weak monsoon."*
- **IIOE 1962-65** (ICSU, SCOR, UNESCO, WMO) → **ISMEX 1973** → **Monsoon-77** → **MONEX-1979**
  (GARP/ICSU, WMO, World Weather Watch; **45 countries**, **52 ships 10°N-10°S**, **104 aircraft
  missions**; Winter MONEX **1 Dec 1978-5 Mar 1979**, Summer MONEX and **WAMEX** **1 May-31 Aug
  1979**; **IMMC at Kuala Lumpur and New Delhi**).
- **The finding:** *"a **specific zone off the coast of Kenya**"* where southern-hemisphere air
  crosses the equator, and *"**fluctuations**… resulted in the **fluctuations of rainfall over
  Maharashtra**"*.
- ⚠️ **MONEX-1979 was itself abnormal:** Kerala onset **delayed 12 days**, break spells, **one**
  depression.
- ✗ **C-32 / N-04:** *Mascarene High*, *Somali Jet*, *Findlater Jet* are in **no certified local
  book**. **Phenomenon certified; names are modern nomenclature.**

## Onset — criteria, normals, actuals

- **After 10 May**, all three gates must concur: **(a)** 60 % of **14 stations** report **≥ 2.5 mm on
  two consecutive days** — *Minicoy, Amini, Thiruvananthapuram, Punalur, Kollam, Allapuzha, Kottayam,
  Kochi, Thrissur, Kozhikode, Thalassery, Kannur, Kudulu, Mangalore*; **(b)** westerlies to
  **600 hPa** (eq-10°N, 55°-80°E) and **15-20 kt at 925 hPa** (5-10°N, 70°-80°E); **(c)** INSAT
  **OLR < 200 W m⁻²** (5-10°N, 70°-75°E).
- **NLM** = northern-most limit on any day; advance *"usually **in surges**"*.
- **Normals:** Andaman **20 May** ⇄ **25 May** ⇄ 📰 **22 May**; Kerala **1 June**; whole country
  **15 July** (Khullar **and** the IMD onset page) ⇄ 📰 **8 July** (IMD end-of-season). **C-15,
  C-24.**
- **Bay faster than Arabian:** ~**20° N in the Bay by the third week of May** while still ~**7° N**
  in the Arabian Sea. **Burst** = sudden onset with thunder, lightning, heavy downpour.
- **Variability:** **60 % of onsets 29 May-7 June**; earliest **11 May 1918 and 1955**; latest
  **18 June 1972** — a **38-day** spread.
- 📰 **2026:** Andaman **16 May** (6 days early, Sri Vijaya Puram); further advance **18 May**
  (Comorin); **nearly stationary**; resumed **23 May**; Lakshadweep **30 May**; **Kerala 4 June**
  (3 days late); **whole country 9 July** (normal 8 July); delays **7-10 days** over Maharashtra,
  Gujarat, MP, Chhattisgarh, Odisha and **5-10 days** over NE India.

## Arabian branch — three streams

- **Stronger for two reasons:** larger sea; and the **entire** current advances on India while only
  **part** of the Bay current enters, the rest to **Myanmar, Thailand, Malaysia**.
- **Stream 1:** perpendicular to the Ghats, **10°-20° N**, **over 250 cm**; **Mumbai 190**,
  **Khandala 60** at ~50 km, **Pune 50** at ~160 km (**seasonal**); **crest 400-500 cm annual** →
  **30-50 cm within 80-100 km** leeward; then a **narrow arid belt**, after which rainfall
  **increases again eastwards**.
- **Stream 2:** **Narmada-Tapi troughs** → **Nagpur ~60 cm**.
- **Stream 3:** **parallel to the Aravali**, which *"offers no obstacle"* → *"the whole of Rajasthan
  is a desert area"*; exception **Mount Abu ~170 cm** against **60-80 cm**.
- 📰 **2026: South Peninsula 542.0 mm = 76 %** of LPA 716.2 mm, *"4th lowest since 1901, 2nd lowest
  since 2001"*.

## Bay branch — funnel and ladder

- **Two streams.** **(1)** Khasi funnel: **Mawsynram 25°18′N 91°35′E, 1,329 m, 1,221 cm**, daily max
  **99 cm**; **Cherrapunji 25°15′N 91°44′E, 1,313 m, 1,102 cm**, daily max **103.6 cm**; **16 km**
  apart, **119 cm** apart; **Guwahati 161 cm at 90 km**. ⇄ **C-20:** Leong **425 in [1,079.5 cm]**
  plus **905 in [2,298.7 cm] in 1861**.
- **(2)** Deflected **westward** at the foothills; **Kolkata 7 June**; **Kolkata 119 → Patna 105 →
  Allahabad 91 ⇄ 76 → Delhi 51 ⇄ 56 → Bikaner 24 cm** (**C-13**); *"progressively depleted… not
  blocked"*.
- **Merge along Agra-Ferozepur**; established over most of the country by **end June**; reaches
  Kashmir by **mid-July** *"only as a **feeble current**"*.
- **Tamil Nadu's double shadow:** rain shadow of the **Arabian** current **and** parallel to the
  **Bay** current.
- 📰 **2026: East & NE India 74 %** of LPA 1,367.3 mm, *"lowest since 1901"* — **1,017.1 mm** in the
  highlights ⇄ **1,016.3 mm** in Table 1 (**X-02**).

## Trough and depressions

- Trough from the **Ganga basin to the head of the Bay**; north **< 1,000 mb**, south
  **1,008-1,010 mb**; the **1,009 mb isobar** crosses **Kerala and Tamil Nadu** — about **10 mb
  across the country**: a **low-gradient system**. ✗ **C-28:** Fig. 5.18 legend reads *"ISOBARS (IN
  **MILLIMETRES**)"*.
- *"A major part of the monsoon rainfall is generated by **depressions**… more so in the **Bay of
  Bengal**."* **3-4 per month, June-September.**
- **Genesis:** June **16°-21° N, west of 92° E** (NW-moving) · July-August **north of 18° N, west of
  90° E** (WNW-moving) · September **north of 15° N, west of 90° E**; **Arabian Sea June 17°-20° N**.
- **Landfall** *"sucked inward through the deltas"* of the **Ganga, Mahanadi, Godavari, Krishna,
  Cauvery**; track **along the trough**, merging with the seasonal low over north-west India; yield
  *"as much as **60 cm in a single day**"*; failure — *"their **absence** or a **change in their
  tracks** results in deficit or no rain."*
- 📰 **2026:** **14 LPS · 6 monsoon depressions · 3 deep depressions · 77 LPS days against a normal
  of 57**. **June: no low-pressure area at all** (climatology 3 systems / ~11 days) → **65 % of
  LPA**. **July: 4 systems = climatology but 24 LPS days against 13.56** → **101 %**, central India
  normal to excess. ⚠️ **Count and persistence are different variables.** ✗ **C-30:** no arithmetic
  across the two taxonomies.

## Breaks

- **July-August**, *"more frequently during the **second week of August**"*; rain ceases *"outside
  the **Himalayan belt and southeast peninsula**"*; the trough's axis lies at the **foothills** and
  *"heavy rainfall occurs over the **sub-Himalayan regions**"* — **a relocation, not an absence**.
- **Duration:** floor **≥ 3 consecutive days** (📰 reported, **U-05**) · normal *"**about a week**"*
  · rare max **two to three weeks**.
- **Frequency:** *"**one or two**"* per season; *"in **85 out of 100 years** there is a break"*.
- ✗ **C-11:** *"over **500-1000 km**"* is in **no source** — **refused**; give the **core monsoon
  zone** instead.
- ⇄ **C-14:** *"collapse of the **Tibetan High**"* (f. 146) ⇄ *"related to **tropical cyclones**…
  in the Bay of Bengal"* (f. 148 iv).
- 📰 **Certified method:** IMD 2026 uses **standardized rainfall anomalies over the core monsoon
  zone** with **+1** and **−1** thresholds; 2026 showed *"alternating wet and dry spells"*, **> +1**
  early July, **< −1** mid-July, dry spells in weeks 3-4 of August, **> +4** in late September.
- ⚠️ **MJO** — intra-seasonal; convective phase over the Indian Ocean favours **active** spells,
  suppressed phase supports **breaks**.

## Withdrawal

- **The rule:** *"the south-west monsoons **reach north-west India last of all and withdraw from
  there first of all**."*
- ✗ **C-08:** Leong p. 124 — *"The Punjab plains **which receive the south-west monsoon earliest**
  are the first to see the withdrawal"* — **true conclusion, false premise**.
- **Durations:** **Punjab** first week of July → second week of September = **2½ months**;
  **Coromandel** first week of June → mid-December = **more than 6 months**.
- **Sequence:** extreme north-west **September** · peninsula **October** · extreme south-eastern tip
  **December**.
- **Character:** *"unlike the sudden burst… the withdrawal is rather **gradual** and takes about
  **three months**."*
- **Fig. 5.21:** 1 Sep · 15 Sep · 1 Oct · 15 Oct · 1 Nov · 15 Nov · 1 Dec · 15 Dec — ✗ the **1 Sep**
  isochrone is **superseded** (**C-24**). 📰 **IMD normal commencement from west Rajasthan: 17
  September**; **2026 actual 19 September**, *"2 days delayed"*.
- **October heat:** Rajasthan **37 °C**, peninsula **33 °C**, north-east India **30 °C**; night
  minima **20°-25 °C**.

## North-east monsoon (route-in I-5)

- **October-November is the main rainy season** in **Tamil Nadu** and adjoining **Andhra Pradesh
  south of the Krishna delta**, and a **secondary** season for **Kerala** → **Kerala is bi-modal**.
- **Krishna Rao and Jagannathan** (⛔ **undated**, **U-08**): Tamil Nadu Oct-Nov **38.25 cm ≈
  38.73 %** of the annual; **coastal areas about 50 %**. [38.25 / 0.3873 = **98.8 cm** implied annual
  — internally coherent.]
- **Leong p. 123: Madras 50 in [127.0 cm] in Oct-Nov = half its annual.**
- ⚠️ **Three denominators:** state **38.73 %** · coast **~50 %** · station *"half"*.
- ↪ **Western disturbances** (incidence **1 to 3**, snow in J&K, HP, Kumaon) are **named and routed**
  to `Geography/advanced/13`.

## Annual rainfall, classes and variability

- **National average ~118 cm**, *"the highest for a country of such vast dimensions anywhere in the
  world"*.
- **Classes:** very high **≥ 200 cm** (west coast 200-400, localised 400-800; **Meghalaya ~1,000
  cm**) · high **100-200** · low **50-100** · very low **< 50** (western Rajasthan, Kachchh, most of
  Ladakh).
- **100 cm isohyet:** Gujarat coast → south parallel to the Ghats crest → Kanniyakumari → east over
  southern J&K, HP, northern UP → **bends west east of Allahabad** over Bundelkhand → WSW over
  western MP, eastern Maharashtra, northern Andhra and Telangana.
- ⇄ **C-16:** minimum **about 12 cm** ⇄ **hardly 15 cm**. ⇄ **C-19:** *"over 250 cm"* is a **class
  floor**; the maximum is **1,221 cm**. ⇄ **C-18:** *"three fourths"* and *"about 87 %"* in one
  paragraph; **75 %** at f. 155 — **87 % is the plains share** (92 cm against 14 cm = **86.8 %**),
  **75 % is national**.
- **CV = (SD / Mean) × 100:** **15-35 %** nationally · **> 40 %** Rajasthan, Gujarat, parts of
  Haryana and Punjab · **> 60 %** extreme west · **80 %** extreme western Rajasthan, *"incidentally…
  the areas which receive minimum rainfall"* · **15-20 %** west coast, sub-Himalaya, NE hills; also
  high at **Leh-Ladakh** and **Rayalaseema**.
- **Corollary:** *"amount of rainfall and **reliability**… **inversely proportional** to the
  coefficient of variability"* — Assam and West Bengal within **10-15 %**, Rajasthan **30-50 % or
  more**.
- **Torrentiality:** **40-45 rainy days**; **50-60 cm in a day** not uncommon; **103.6 cm in 24 h**
  at Cherrapunji; **1,102 cm in 180 rainy days**; **Sri Ganganagar 12 cm in 10-12 rainy days**;
  *"**it pours, it never rains in India**"*.
- ✗ **C-25, Table 5.3 (1951 Census):** **11 %** > 200 · **21 %** 125-200 · **37 %** 75-125 ·
  **24 %** 35-75 · **7 %** < 35 [sums to 100; sub-75 classes = **31 %** = *"about one third"*].
  **Fig. 5.25** classes skip **60-80**.
- 📰 **2026:** **18 of 36 subdivisions normal (53 % of area)**, **17 deficient (42 %)**, **1 (Odisha)
  excess (5 %)** — in an **87 %** season. ⇄ **X-06:** districts **282 + 34 + 312 + 90 + 16 = 734** of
  a stated **741**; percentages sum **99**.

## Teleconnections

- **El Niño** = *"a narrow **warm current** off the coast of **Peru in December**"*; Spanish for
  *"**The Child Christ**"*.
- **Southern Oscillation** = the *"**sea-saw pattern**"* between Pacific and Indian oceans, found by
  **Sir Gilbert Walker**, head of the Indian Meteorological service [**1920** in Khullar ⇄ usually
  **1923-24**; say *"the early 1920s"* — **C-29**]; the equatorial vertical circulation is the
  **Walker Circulation**; **EN + SO = ENSO**; periodicity *"**is not fixed**"*, **two to five
  years**.
- **SOI = Tahiti − Port Darwin.** **Darwin 12°30′S 131°E**; **Tahiti 17°45′S 149°30′W** (Fig. 5.9).
  **Positive** → good monsoon prospects; **negative** → poor monsoon, higher east-Pacific rain.
- **Statistics:** **1870-1970 — 43 deficient years, 19 with El Niño (44.2 %), and 6 El Niño years
  with good rain.** **1972-73 to 2013-14 — 14 droughts, 9 El Niño + 5 not.** Severe drought with
  strong El Niño: **1972-73, 1979-80, 1987-88, 2009-10, 2013-14**. Drought without El Niño:
  **1991-92, 1994-95, 1997-98, 2004-05, 2006-07** — ✗ **C-27: 1997-98 is wrong on both limbs.**
- **The governing sentence:** *"although there is a tendency for poor monsoons to be associated with
  an El Nino, **there is no one-to-one correspondence**."*
- **Institutions:** **TOGA 1985** · **CLIVAR January 1995** · **IMAP** (Department of Space).
- **Four timescales:** **MJO** intra-seasonal · **ENSO** inter-annual · **IOD** seasonal to
  inter-annual · **PDO** decadal. ⚠️ **Modoki** = **central**-Pacific pattern, not a weak El Niño.
- 📰 **2026 observed:** *"a **weak El Niño** developed during in June and further **strengthen**…
  and a **strong El Niño** prevailed during end of the season"*; *"the **IOD remained in the neutral
  phase throughout**"*.

## Forecasting and verification

- **Post-1987 (Khullar f. 131):** *"parametric and power regression models"*, **15 parameters** in
  four categories — **temperature, pressure, wind pattern, snow cover**. *Temperature:* El Niño
  current year · El Niño previous year · northern India (March) · east coast (March) · central India
  (May) · northern hemisphere (Jan-Feb). *Wind:* **500 hPa ridge (April)** · **50 hPa ridge-trough
  extent (Jan-Feb)** · **10 hPa (30 km) westerly wind (January)**.
- 📰 **Since 2021:** a **Multi-Model Ensemble (MME)** of coupled global models **including IMD's
  MMCFS**. **C-26.** ⛔ **U-07's skill percentages are dropped.**
- 📰 **2026 cycle:** first stage **13 April ⇄ 15 April** (**X-01**), **92 % ± 5 %** → [87, 97];
  second stage **29 May**, **90 % ± 4 %** → [86, 94]; probabilistic **Deficient < 90 at 60 %** against
  a climatological **16 %**, *"84 % probability for below normal or less"*; **realised 30 September:
  87 % of LPA, 759.4 mm**, *"4th lowest since 2001, 13th lowest since 1901"*. **LPA 1971-2020 =
  87 cm / 868.6 mm.**
- 📰 **Verified:** seasonal quantitative, seasonal probabilistic, MCZ. **Failed:** **Kerala onset**,
  forecast **26 May ± 4 days**, realised **4 June**, *"only the **second instance (after 2015)**
  since… **2005**"*; **three of four regions** — only **South Peninsula** in range, departures
  **NW −6 %, Central −3 %, NE −26 %, SP −24 %**; **two of four months** *"underestimated for July and
  September"* (65 / 101 / 84 / 92 %).
- ⇄ **X-04** 8 days ⇄ 9 days · ⇄ **X-07/U-06** MCZ **779.9 mm = 94 %** of **826.0** yet below the
  printed band **782.8-881.2**, which 826.0 does not reproduce · ⚠️ **X-05** monthly actuals sum
  **758.5** against **759.4** · ✗ **X-03** June LPA printed as **"165.4 cm"** · ⚠️ **X-08**
  climatological probabilities sum **99** · 📰 **X-14** Jan-Mar 2026 **snow-cover extent** *"slightly
  below normal"* with a *"**general inverse relationship**"*.
- 📰 **Mission Mausam:** Cabinet **11 September 2024**; **₹2,000 crore**; initial phase to **March
  2026**; **IMD + NCMRWF + IITM**; **50 DWRs, 60 radiosonde/wind stations, 10 wind profilers, 25
  radiometers**; phase II **2026-31** indicated. ⚠️ **Not** Project Mausam. ✗ **C-10:** C1's PIB
  citation is dead.

## Consequence, and what this topic does not own

- **Vegetation:** trees normally **deciduous** (a **drought** response); *"more open and less
  luxuriant than the equatorial jungle"*; *"**far fewer species**"*; **teak** — *"Burma alone…
  **three quarters of the world's production**"* ⏱; **sal, acacia, eucalyptus** (N. Australia),
  **bamboo thickets**; thinning to **thorny scrub and savanna** ↪ Topic 17.
- **Teak logistics:** hills to **3,000 ft [914 m]**; **~100 years** to mature; **green logs will not
  float** → **girdled/"poisoned"**; floated down the **Chindwin and Irrawaddy to Rangoon**; **~18
  months**.
- **Shifting-cultivation names:** *ladang* (Malaysia) · *taungya* (Burma) · *tamrai* (Thailand) ·
  *caingin* (Philippines) · *humah* (Java) · *chena* (Sri Lanka) · *milpa* (Africa/Central America).
- **Leong:** *"In no part of the world has the climate affected **Man's way of life so profoundly**
  as in the monsoon lands."* **Khullar f. 155:** *"Indian budget is a **gamble in the monsoon**"*;
  the monsoon is *"the **pivot** upon which the whole economic life of India swings"*.
- ⚠️ **C1's qualification:** irrigation, buffer stocks, insurance and better forecasting have
  **loosened** the dependence; the residual is **concentrated on rain-fed smallholders** — a
  **distributional** problem.
- ⛔ **Refusals operating:** **R-01** no answer letter · **R-02** no climate-change attribution ·
  **R-03** no trend claims · **R-04** no disaster-management depth · **R-05** no
  agricultural-economic depth · **R-06** no re-derivation of jets or ocean mechanics · **R-07** no
  invented numbers, no silent conversion, **500-1000 km refused** · **R-08** the coverage package is
  never evidence.
